Trade Policy Review Body
 
TRADE POLICY REVIEW
REPORT BY THE SECRETARIAT
COLOMBIA
This report, prepared for the Trade Policy Review of Colombia, has been drawn up by the 
WTO Secretariat on its own responsibility. The Secretariat has, as required by the Agreement 
establishing the Trade Policy Review Mechanism (Annex 3 of the Marrakesh Agreement Establishing 
the World Trade Organization), sought clarification from Colombia on its trade policies and practices.
Any technical questions arising from this report may be addressed to Ms Eugenia Lizano 
(tel. 022 739 6578), Mr Carlos Perez del Castillo (tel. 022 739 5336), Ms Stephanie Dorange-Patoret 
(tel. 022 739 5497) or Ms Varinia Gonzales (tel. 022 739 6894).
Document WT/TPR/G/472 contains the policy statement submitted by or Colombia.
Note: This report is subject to restricted circulation and press embargo until the end of the 
first session of the meeting of the Trade Policy Review Body on Colombia. This report was drafted 
in Spanish.

SUMMARY
1.
Colombia is of the view that international trade plays an important role in promoting economic 
and social development, with the aim of closing social and gender gaps, and reducing inequality and 
poverty. In this regard, Colombia has sought to implement inclusive and sustainable economic and 
trade policies during the period under review. Moreover, since 2018, the time of its last Trade Policy 
Review, one of the main national policy objectives has been to move from an extractive economy, 
based primarily on the export of mining and energy products, to a knowledge economy, based on 
innovation, science and technology, and trade in services. Hence, the importance attached to 
improving the quality policy and the intellectual property regime. To encourage sectoral 
diversification, the Reindustrialization Policy was introduced in 2023, which defines the strategic 
sectors, including the Just Energy Transition (TEJ), the aim of which is to move towards the use of 
sustainable energy and ensure energy sovereignty. To further advance structural change, a credit 
pact was signed with private banks in 2024, aimed at increasing financing for five priority sectors 
(agriculture; manufacturing and energy transformation; tourism; housing and infrastructure; and 
the popular economy).
2.
Overall, exports and imports of goods and services have accounted for between 35% and 40% 
of GDP since 2018, except in 2020 and 2022. The share of GDP represented by international trade 
(goods and services) fluctuated as a result of the higher international prices of products such as 
petroleum and petroleum products, one of the country's main export products, and the performance 
of transport and tourism, the leading service exports. The economic slowdown in 2023 and 2024 
also led to a decline in overall trade.
3.
Although public policies are seeking to promote export diversification, exports remain highly 
concentrated in mining and energy products (precious stones, precious metals, coal, petroleum and 
petroleum products), accounting for over 60% of total exports during the review period, followed by 
agricultural products (coffee, bananas, and flowers), which accounted for 64.7% of agricultural 
exports in 2023. Imports have not diversified either; the authorities indicated that approximately 
50% of imports are intermediate goods and inputs, mainly destined for industry (not necessarily 
extractive). Trade is also focused on a small number of trading partners; the top ones are still the 
United States, China and the European Union. The most recent data available for 2022 still do not 
point to a high degree of integration in trade chains.
4.
Colombia approved the WTO Agreement on Trade Facilitation in 2018 and, in 2022, notified 
that it had implemented 100% of its commitments. In line with this Agreement and with the National 
Logistics Policy, guidelines were established to simplify customs procedures, which required changes 
to customs regulations. However, these changes will be implemented gradually as customs and 
related computer systems are brought up to date, and are primarily aimed at reducing times and 
costs and preventing smuggling through more efficient border controls. While the measures that 
have already been implemented have helped to reduce operating costs, according to estimates by 
the United Nations Economic and Social Commission for Asia and the Pacific (ESCAP) and the 
World Bank, Colombia's trade costs were 1.4 times higher than in the world's largest economies 
in 2022, largely because of the state of infrastructure and logistics services, excessive regulation 
and overlap among government agencies.
5.
Colombia is an upper middle-income economy and its economic performance was mixed 
during 2018-24. After contracting as a result of external and internal shocks, the economy recorded 
a significant growth rate of 10.8% in 2022, before slowing to 0.7% in 2023. The country's 
GDP growth estimates for 2024 are 1.7%. Despite the fact that GDP per capita reached USD 7,943 
in 2024, compared to USD 6,925 in 2018, and that Colombia implemented public policies to reduce 
monetary poverty, the poverty rate remained relatively high, at 33% in 2023 (35.5% in 2018), and 
income distribution has not improved (the Gini coefficient was 0.54 in 2023, latest available data).
6.
Colombia has not fundamentally changed its trade policy stance and has sought to increase 
its international market share through its participation in the multilateral system and through trade 
negotiations. Colombia is an active Member of the WTO and believes that the multilateral trading 
system must be based on non-discriminatory, clear and transparent rules. In this regard, Colombia 
supports WTO reform, the inclusion of new trade issues, and the need to restore the dispute 
settlement mechanism. Colombia also participates in a number of joint initiatives, such as 
e-commerce, MSMEs, investment facilitation for development, as well as in the trade and 
environment discussions. During the review period, Colombia notified various trade and related 

WT/TPR/S/472
- 7 -
 
measures to the WTO, but, to date, it still has outstanding notifications in certain areas. In 
March 2025, Colombia adopted the WTO Agreement on Fisheries Subsidies. Colombia is a party to 
the Information Technology Agreement (ITA) and an observer to the plurilateral Agreements on 
Trade in Civil Aircraft and on Government Procurement. Since 2017, two complaints have been filed 
against Colombia's trade policies.
7.
Colombia maintains a relatively open foreign investment regime, with few restrictions. During 
the period under review, Colombia sought to attract investment to other sectors considered to be 
strategic. However, investment remains concentrated in the mining and energy sector, as well as 
foreign trade, which exacerbates the vulnerability of the Colombian economy, particularly in the light 
of its heavy reliance on the extractive industries in an international context of transitioning towards 
an energy mix less dependent on hydrocarbons. In this regard, diversifying the economic structure, 
by boosting non-traditional sectors, increasing investment in renewable energy sources and adopting 
climate-change adaptation and mitigation policies, would have positive macroeconomic impacts that 
would offset the negative effects of potential fluctuations in international coal and oil prices. 
Colombia has sought to mitigate these fluctuations by using the Fuel Price Stabilization Fund; 
however, since 2021, that Fund has been mainly financed by public money, resulting in greater fiscal 
pressure and, therefore, greater vulnerability to the economy's fiscal position.
8.
Colombia continues to offer several incentives, most of which are horizontal, to boost 
investment, employment, innovation and inclusion. These include a series of tariff concessions and 
specific programmes, such as free trade zones and the Vallejo Plan, which grant exemptions from 
tariffs, VAT and income tax. These programmes do not appear to be evaluated regularly, it is 
therefore difficult to determine their costs and benefits. These incentives could, partly, explain the 
limited tax revenue from tariffs, which was 0.4% of GDP in 2024.
9.
During the period under review, Colombia's applied MFN tariff did not change substantially. In 
this regard, the most important change in the tariff structure was an increase in the share of tariff 
lines with a 0% rate, up from 49.7% in 2017 to 53.3% in 2024. Thus, the simple average tariff 
decreased slightly from 7.1% in 2017 to 6.8% in 2024. The average MFN tariff applied to agricultural 
products (WTO definition), at 16.1% (15.4% in 2017), was higher than the overall simple average, 
while for non-agricultural products, the average was lower, at 5.3% (5.8% in 2017).
10.
Colombia continues to use the Andean Price Band System (SAFP), a system of variable tariffs 
applied to some agricultural products (181 tariff lines at the 10-digit level). Under the SAFP, tariffs 
are calculated and modified every 15 days in accordance with international prices. If the SAFP is 
taken into account, the simple average of MFN duties on agricultural products in 2024 was 19.2%, 
down from 20.7% in 2017. While the purpose of this mechanism is to stabilize import costs, an 
analysis of the SAFP shows that the tariffs calculated using this system do not appear to affect the 
quantity imported. Conversely, updating the tariffs every 15 days means import costs are less 
predictable.
11.
In addition to tariffs, imports are subject to internal taxes. Colombia continues to levy the 
national consumption tax (INC), which has not been substantially modified. In the case of beer, the 
basis for calculating the INC depends on whether it is imported or domestically produced. Colombia 
also imposes taxes on fossil fuels depending on their carbon content. In addition, in 2022, to protect 
the environment and health, Colombia introduced other taxes on single-use plastics and 
ultra-processed beverages and food products, both imported and local.
12.
Colombia also applies import and export prohibitions or restrictions as part of its international 
commitments or to meet other objectives, such as protecting public health and ensuring product 
quality and national security. Colombia maintains an import licensing system that includes both 
automatic licensing (import registration) and non-automatic licensing (prior licensing). Products 
subject to this requirement are listed in circulars, which are modified according to the circumstances.
13.
During the review period, Colombia amended the regulations governing the implementation 
of anti-dumping and countervailing measures, thereby reducing procedural time frames and, for 
countervailing measures, introducing procedural stages after the preliminary determination. 
No changes were made to regulations governing the implementation of safeguard measures; 
Colombia continues to use three types of safeguard, in addition to the WTO safeguard. A drop in the 
use of anti-dumping measures was recorded during the review period. Colombia initiated 

WT/TPR/S/472
- 8 -
 
two countervailing measure investigations. Colombia also initiated 10 safeguard investigations and 
imposed four definitive measures.
14.
During the 2017-24 period, Colombia made efforts to foster innovation and scientific and 
technological development, as well to improve the quality of national products by guaranteeing the 
use of international standards, with a view to accessing foreign markets (internationalization 
strategy). To this end, Colombia has tried to modernize its quality system in order to ensure a more 
reliable and internationally-recognized quality infrastructure. For instance, since 2018, the technical 
regulation drafting process has had to include a regulatory impact analysis (RIA), and specific periods 
for national public consultation and international notification. In addition, the Colombian Agricultural 
Institute (ICA) has introduced a plant traceability system that is mandatory for all plants and edible 
plant products. A risk assessment system for inspections of imported agricultural products and 
by-products (SISPAP) has been established with a view to reducing the number of physical 
inspections.
15.
During the period under review, Colombia continued to encourage good economic competition 
practices, yet some anti-competitive practices persist, such as collusion in government procurement 
processes, which was the main reason for sanctions between 2018 and 2024. Other aspects of the 
competition regime were modified during the review period, including the collaboration benefits 
programme and the procedure for notifying, verifying and approving economic concentrations. State 
participation in the economy remains considerable. There are numerous state-owned enterprises, 
some of which are leading operators in their markets, such Ecopetrol and the Grupo Bicentenario, 
which groups together financial entities with a state share in one holding company. The State also 
retains a number of monopolies, such as the revenue-generating spirits monopoly, under which the 
departments have the exclusive right to manufacture and import spirits and control 
interdepartmental sales.
16.
In order to strengthen the government procurement regime and prevent anti-competitive 
practices such as collusion, Colombia has adopted a number of good practices, including the use of 
the electronic procurement platform and standard specifications. However, in spite of efforts to 
improve procedural efficiency and transparency, risks attributable to conflicts of interest and bribery 
still exist, and there have been significant losses (2.2% of GDP in 2024) from unfinished works. 
Furthermore, since 2018, the most widely used method has been single tendering. Colombia 
continues to use government procurement as a tool to promote, through preferences, the purchasing 
of goods and services of domestic origin, the participation of MSMEs, women's economic 
empowerment, and the development of the popular and community economy. Colombia has also 
adopted responsible procurement practices to promote the circular economy.
17.
Innovation is vital if Colombia is to successfully transform its productive model from one based 
on extractive activities to one based on knowledge. In this context, during the review period, the 
regime for protecting intellectual property rights was strengthened by simplifying the procedures 
involved; incentives, registration fee discounts and technical support were also provided. Despite 
this, indicators show that the level of innovation remains limited. Colombia has adopted new 
measures to improve enforcement, but piracy remains a challenge.
18.
The agricultural sector generates around 20% of exports. Colombia is a net exporter of 
agricultural products and is one of the world's leading producers of coffee, bananas and flowers. At 
the domestic level, the agricultural sector remains important for Colombia's economy, given its 
contribution to poverty alleviation, self-sufficiency, the creation of jobs – particularly for 
women – and economic development in the most disadvantaged areas. Colombia implements a 
number of programmes to support the sector: extension programmes, special lines of credit, rural 
capitalization incentives, an agricultural guarantee fund, and agricultural insurance, which have been 
redesigned to provide more technical assistance to small farmers and the lesser developed regions 
of the country. Colombia also continues to use the SAFP to maintain import cost stability, and price 
stabilization funds for certain agricultural products to secure remunerative income for producers, 
regulate domestic production, and promote exports. Colombia has a "monitored freedom" regime 
for the prices of products such as agrochemicals and milk.
19.
In the interests of promoting inclusion and sustainability, Colombia amended its mining policy 
during the review period in order to, inter alia, formalize small-scale mining activity and protect the 
environment. In 2020, the Renewable Energy Law was amended to allow the sale of energy produced 
using renewable sources on the electricity market. In 2022, amendments were made to the 

WT/TPR/S/472
- 9 -
 
General Royalties Scheme (SGR) for mining and energy in order to promote projects aimed at 
preserving peace and the environment.
20.
During the review period, the financial sector's prudential indicators showed it performed well, 
despite the pandemic. Colombia enhanced financial service oversight by applying prudential 
standards that incorporate international standards, and also implemented oversight rules for 
financial conglomerates and holding companies, including the Grupo Bicentenario. According to the 
authorities, all currency exchange in Colombia must be conducted through the foreign exchange 
market and registered with the Central Bank for statistical purposes. As a result, all exchange 
operations relating to foreign trade (imports and exports) must be registered. These procedures 
were amended during the review period to include new forms of payment.
21.
Colombia has continued its digital transformation process, amending a number of regulations 
to encourage investment in the mobile broadband service. The coverage and quality of the service, 
and hence its use, has therefore increased. While the digital divide still exists, improved access has 
resulted in the growth of e-commerce. This growth also stems from a simplified foreign exchange 
market registration process. For example, since 2021, foreign trade transactions made with a credit 
card may be registered. Furthermore, e-commerce import and export operations may now be carried 
out through companies specializing in electronic deposits and payments (SEDPE) and through 
payment aggregators (PSPA).
22.
In 2023, the tourism sector saw a record number of visitors, with foreign currency inflows 
reaching an all-time high, making it a key sector for trade in services, as well as for the attraction 
of FDI and the creation of jobs, most of which are held by women. The sector nevertheless remains 
dependent on a limited number of markets. In this context, commercial air transport policy is key 
to the development of tourism; since 2018, new air routes have been opened, and the frequency 
and capacity of services have been increased.
23.
In short, Colombia made no substantial changes to either its economic policy or its trade policy 
during the review period, even though its national policy plans state that the country is seeking to 
amend its productive structure so that it can reduce its reliance on the extractive economy, 
particularly against an international backdrop marked by highly volatile commodity prices, and 
develop a more sustainable and inclusive knowledge-based economy. In order to promote the 
economic transition and export diversification, Colombia has modernized its quality system to offer 
a more reliable and internationally-recognized framework, and has simplified intellectual property 
right registration. However, to ensure these changes are successful, Colombia needs to increase 
investment in both physical and human capital in order to boost productivity and integrate into global 
value chains.

WT/TPR/S/472
- 10 -
 
1  ECONOMIC ENVIRONMENT
1.1  Main features of the economy
1.1.  Colombia considers international trade to be essential to promoting sustainable development 
and inclusion, thereby reducing poverty. The share of international trade (exports plus imports of 
goods and services) in GDP fluctuated between 35% and 40% in the period 2018-24 when 
international shocks had a strong impact. In this context, Colombia sought to diversify its exports 
and attract foreign direct investment (FDI) in order to participate more actively in global value 
chains, promoting innovation and exports of services. However, despite such efforts, Colombian 
exports remain highly concentrated in minerals, metals and plant products, which accounted for 
76.5% of total exports in 2023. In addition, the most recent data available for 2022 still do not point 
to a high degree of integration in trade chains.1 The country's imports have not diversified either; 
the authorities have indicated that approximately 50% of imports are intermediate goods, inputs 
primarily for industry (not necessarily extractive). Similarly, 25.2% of FDI flows go to the oil, mining 
and quarrying sectors. This concentration in extractive industries exposes the economy to 
fluctuations in international markets. Colombia's foreign trade performance is also dependent on 
trade costs, which, according to the United Nations Economic and Social Commission for Asia and 
the Pacific (ESCAP) and the World Bank, were 1.4 times higher than in the world's largest economies 
in 2022.
1.2.  Between 2018 and 2023, real GDP growth averaged 2.8% and GDP per capita increased. 
In 2024, GDP per capita reached USD 7,943 compared to USD 6,925 in 2018 (Table 1.1). Although 
Colombia implemented public policies to reduce monetary poverty during the period under review 
(2018-24), it remained relatively high.2 In 2023, monetary poverty was 33%, lower than that 
recorded in 2018 (35.5%); however, extreme monetary poverty was 11.4% in 2023, an increase on 
2018 (10.4%). Moreover, income distribution has not improved; in 2023, the Gini coefficient was 
0.546, compared to 0.527 in 2018.3 In addition, according to World Bank data, between 2018 and 
2022 (the most recent year for which data are available), the proportion of income in the hands of 
the highest-earning 10% of the population rose from 39% to 43.5%.4

1.3.  The authorities are aware of the vulnerabilities of the Colombian economy, given the heavy 
reliance on the extractive industries in an international context of transitioning towards an energy 
mix less dependent on hydrocarbons. In this context, the authorities are of the view that diversifying 
the economic structure, by boosting non-traditional sectors, increasing investment in renewable 
energy sources and adopting climate-change adaptation and mitigation policies, will have positive 
macroeconomic impacts that will offset the negative effects of potential fluctuations in international 
coal and oil prices, thus contributing to macroeconomic and environmental sustainability. The 
authorities therefore consider the Just Energy Transition to be a key objective, the aim of which is 
to move towards the use of sustainable energies and ensure energy sovereignty. To encourage 
sectoral diversification, the Reindustrialization Policy was introduced in 2023, which defines the 
strategic sectors, including the Just Energy Transition, to promote industrial development.5 
Moreover, the Just Energy Transition is one of the fundamental pillars for economic diversification 
under the National Development Plan (2022-26).6
1.4.  During the period 2018-24, Colombia's economic performance was mixed but generally 
positive. GDP growth estimates for 2024 are 1.7%, compared with 0.7% in 2023. During the review 
period, Colombia had to deal with the negative impacts of external shocks (COVID-19 pandemic; 
fluctuations in international prices of raw materials, especially hydrocarbons and minerals; and 
higher transport costs) and internal shocks (national strike in 2021), all of which affected its 
economic growth.7 The national strike in 2021, which lasted for three months, primarily affected the 
agricultural sector, of great importance to Colombia in terms of trade as the country has traditionally 
5 National Economic and Social Policy Council (CONPES) Document No. 4129, 2023. Viewed at: 
https://colaboracion.dnp.gov.co/CDT/Conpes/Económicos/4129.pdf.
6 Ministry of Finance and Public Credit (2024), Marco Fiscal de Mediano Plazo 2024. Estrategia fiscal 
para la reactivación económica sostenible. Viewed at: 
https://www.minhacienda.gov.co/documents/20119/706695/MFMP+2024.pdf/a33501c8-01c1-5ddc-0027-
96aa06353a00?t=1719523560000.
7 These include the following: the drop in the international oil price in 2020; the 2022 energy crisis that 
led to higher oil and coal prices; and the disruption to global supply chains in 2022, which resulted in higher 
transport costs for merchandise.

WT/TPR/S/472
- 13 -
 
been one of the world's leading exporters of coffee and bananas and, more recently, fresh cut 
flowers. This strike caused losses estimated at USD 3.3 billion.8
1.2  Recent economic developments
1.5.  GDP growth fluctuated in the period 2018-24. The 2018-19 biennium was characterized by 
moderate economic growth, while there was a contraction of 7.2% in 2020 owing primarily to the 
impact of the COVID-19 pandemic. Growth accelerated in 2021 and 2022, mainly as a result of the 
recovery in economic activity following the pandemic and increased global demand, which had a 
positive impact on the price of raw materials. Strong growth in domestic demand fuelled inflation, 
which led to the adoption of tighter monetary policies (see below). As a result of this policy, domestic 
demand contracted in 2023, which mainly affected gross capital formation and led to GDP growth of 
only 0.7% (Table 1.3). Real GDP growth is expected to reach 1.7% in 2024, thanks to the recovery 
in domestic demand, in particular gross capital formation.9
1.6.  The structure of the economy did not change substantially during the review period. Services, 
which remain the largest sector in terms of GDP share (58%), is the sector that has contributed 
most to economic growth (Chart 1.1). The second largest sector is manufacturing, even though its 
share of GDP decreased slightly during the review period, from 11.2% in 2018 to 10.1% in 2024. 
The agricultural sector's share of GDP increased during the period, although it fluctuated 
considerably owing to the pandemic and the 2021 national strike, before rebounding positively in 
2022; however, its contribution to economic growth has shrunk and was lower than that of the 
manufacturing sector, except in 2023 and 2024 (Table 1.1).
1.7.  Although, overall, the structure of GDP has remained stable, the construction sector declined 
sharply between 2018 and 2024, as its share of GDP fell from 6.6% in 2018 to 4.1% in 2023, despite 
the fact that it began to show signs of recovery in 2024 following the resumption of civil works 
paralysed since the health crisis.10 The share of mining decreased from 5.9% in 2018 to 4.4% in 
2024, despite the recovery in international oil prices and record coal prices in 2022.

1.8.  The unemployment rate varied considerably during the period analysed, rising from 10% in 
2018 to 16.5% in 2020, as a result of the decline in productive activity and subsequent, 
pandemic-related job losses. The unemployment rate began to fall in 2021, mirroring the economic 
recovery, reaching 10.2% in both 2023 and 2024 (Table 1.1).
1.9.  During the review period, economic growth was rooted primarily in domestic consumption, 
which increased at an average annual rate of 3.9% between 2018 and 2024; this was higher than 
the GDP growth rate, despite the sharp contraction caused by the pandemic in 2020. Private 
consumption rose from 69.1% of GDP in 2018 to 75.8% of GDP in 2024, thus, it remained around 
or above 70% of GDP throughout the period (Chart 1.2).
1.10.  In addition to private consumption, GDP growth is explained by higher investment and public 
consumption as components of demand, even though the share of investment fell from 22% in 2018 
to 17% in 2023, and was one of the main causes of the economic slowdown in 2023, particularly 
because of the contraction in gross capital formation (Chart 1.2).11 Investment recovered slightly as 
a result of civil works carried out in late 2024 (Table 1.1).12 There is a direct link between investment 
(capital formation) and imports, as most machinery and equipment recorded as investment are 
imported. As a result, higher investment, to the extent that it is based on imports, also reduces 
GDP, as can be seen clearly in 2020 and 2023 (Chart 1.2).
1.11.  This is due, in particular, to the fact that the Colombian economy is highly focused on the 
primary extractive sector for which machinery has to be imported, which is why gross capital 
formation is concentrated in machinery and equipment.13 Like investment, household consumption

also has a strong imported goods component, including durable and semi-durable goods.14 Moreover,

driver of economic growth.
1.12.  Regarding foreign trade, the contribution of exports to economic growth varied considerably 
in accordance with international prices of major export products: Exports made a very positive 
contribution in 2021 and 2022 thanks to the high international prices of hydrocarbons, whereas in 
2018, 2019 and 2023, their contribution was smaller owing to lower international prices for major 
export products. Imports, in turn, respond mainly to the behaviour of gross capital formation and 
private consumption, mitigating the impact that exports can have on economic growth. The 
contribution of net exports to GDP was therefore negative during the period 2018-24 (Chart 1.2).

1.2.1  Fiscal policy
1.13.  During the review period, Colombia continued to implement a fiscal rule, the objective of 
which is to ensure the sustainability of public finances and promote public expenditure efficiency.15 
However, the fiscal rule was suspended during 2020 and 2021, before being implemented again in 
2022, with a number of amendments made in 2021.16 Since 2022, the fiscal rule has provided

greater flexibility to the management of fiscal policy, based on the net public debt from the previous 
fiscal year and the fiscal balance (structural net primary balance).
1.14.  In 2018 and 2019, the primary balance improved and the total fiscal deficit decreased; 
however, this situation was reversed as a result of the pandemic when the fiscal deficit rose to 7.8% 
of GDP in 2020 and 7% in 2021, reflecting the growth in expenditure when the fiscal rule was 
suspended (Table 1.2). The primary balance improved following the re-establishment of the fiscal 
rule in 2022, reaching 1%, with a deficit of 5.3% of GDP, in line with the fiscal rule.17 In 2023, the 
primary and total deficits amounted to 0.3% and 4.2% of GDP, respectively.18 However, in order to 
comply with the fiscal rule and address the increase in total expenditure in 2023 (21.9% of GDP), a 
number of allocations under the General Budget of the Nation were postponed.19 In 2024, the total 
deficit reached 6.7% of GDP, which meant that it would not achieve the annual targets established 
under the fiscal rule.20 This was because of the fall in the tax revenue of and higher spending by the 
Central Government at the same time as interest rate growth increased the cost of debt servicing. 
However, after the 2025 Financial Plan was updated, the fiscal rule for 2024 was met, with a primary 
deficit of 2.4% of GDP.21 Tax revenues were lower as a result of the delays to the reform of the 
computer systems of the National Tax and Customs Directorate (DIAN), as well as the lack of human 
resources22, despite the fact that DIAN implemented measures to reduce tax avoidance and tax 
evasion.23 In order to deal with the increase in spending, measures were proposed to close the deficit 
of the Fuel Price Stabilization Fund by adjusting the price of fossil fuels.24

1.15.  In 2023, total revenue reached a level equivalent to 18.7% of GDP, higher than in 2018. This 
is mainly because of an increase in tax revenue, from 13.7% of GDP in 2018 to 16.6%. However, 
tax revenue fell to 14.4% of GDP in 2024.
1.16.  In terms of tax revenue, foreign trade taxes accounted for only 2.4% of GDP in 2023 (2.2% 
in 2024), of which 2.1% came from value-added tax (VAT) and only 0.3% from tariffs; this amount 
decreased even further in 2024. The low rate of collection of tariff duties in Colombia stems from 
the fact that over 50% of tariff lines have an MFN tariff of 0% and that Colombia is implementing a 
range of tax incentive schemes (sections 3 and 4).
1.2.2  Monetary and exchange rate policy
1.17.  Monetary policy in Colombia aims to maintain a low and stable inflation rate, as well as to 
achieve the maximum sustainable level of output and employment. To attain these objectives, the 
Central Bank of Colombia (BRC) follows an inflation targeting strategy with a target of 3% under a 
flexible exchange-rate regime. The main policy instrument is the monetary policy rate (MPR); other 
instruments used include open market operations25, intervention rates26, and the purchase and sale 
of foreign currency on the foreign exchange market (see below).
1.18.  Chart 1.3 shows that between 2018 and 2021 (September), an expansionary monetary policy, 
characterized by the stability of the MPR and its reduction during the pandemic, was implemented 
to increase liquidity in the economy.27 Headline inflation remained around the target inflation level 
between 2018 and 2020. However, as inflation rose in September 2021, the BRC reversed its 
monetary policy, adopting a contractionary policy based on successive MPR increases.28 Although 
inflation trended downward in 2024 (5.2% in December), it remained above the inflation goal as a 
result of the inflation inertia within the services sector, which was why monetary policy remained 
tight.29
25 Open-market operations are very short-term liquidity operations (1, 7 and 14 days) that influence the 
short-term interest rate. Online information viewed at: https://www.banrep.gov.co/es/glosario/operaciones-
mercado-abierto-oma.
26 The monetary-policy intervention rate is the minimum rate on one-day monetary expansion auctions.
27 Between January 2018 and October 2020, the MPR was reduced on various occasions, falling 
progressively from 4.75% to 1.75%; it remained at this level until September 2021.
28 The MPR rose from 2% in 2021 (September) to 13.25% in May 2023.
29 BRC (2024), Informe de Política Monetaria, octubre, p. 31. Viewed at: 
https://repositorio.banrep.gov.co/server/api/core/bitstreams/8eaf213f-85d9-4456-931f-

1.19.  Colombia continues to have a flexible exchange rate. Exchange-rate flexibility is considered a 
fundamental element in dealing with external shocks, hitting inflation targets and maintaining 
financial stability by reducing exchange-rate risk incentives and developing the hedging market. 
Monetary expansion or contraction caused by the purchase or sale of foreign currency is neutralized 
so that the short-term interest rate does not deviate from the level set by the BRC.30
1.20.  During the review period, the BRC intervened to stabilize the foreign-exchange market and 
increase international reserves. Specifically, the BRC used various instruments to intervene in the 
foreign exchange market during the health crisis (2020-21) as depreciation averaged 12.5%, rising 
from COP 3,282 (2019) to COP 3,694 for USD 1 (2020).31 In 2018-19, a programme to build 
international reserves was launched to cover the potential external liquidity needs of Colombia, with 
reserves in the amount of USD 1,878.4 million.32 Furthermore, in 2024, the BRC managed to 
accumulate USD 1,479.4 million in international reserves through another intervention to maintain 
dollar liquidity and thereby compensate for the reduced access to Colombia's Flexible Credit Line 
with the IMF.33
1.21.  During the period 2018-24 (September), broadly speaking, there was a near-continuous 
nominal depreciation of the exchange rate, which peaked in 2022 and 2023, causing an increase in 
the cost of imported products and, therefore, in the inflation rate of goods (Chart 1.3). In 2023, 
there was an appreciation of the exchange rate owing to a slight recovery in oil prices, the reduction 
of interest rates by the United States Federal Reserve, lower inflation, higher FDI flows and a greater 
supply of foreign currency on the spot market. However, the currency began to weaken again in 
2024 as a result of the depreciation of the nominal exchange rate in the wake of oil price volatility, 
a stronger United States dollar and lower economic growth.34 The authorities indicated that low tax

1.2.3  Balance of payments
1.22.  In general, Colombia's current account balance was in deficit, mainly because of the primary 
income (factor income) deficit and the trade balance of goods and services, offset by secondary 
income (current transfers). The primary income deficit, which is the largest, is partly the result of 
profit transfers (outflows) abroad (mainly from the mining and energy sector) and payments related 
to foreign debt. The current account deficit was particularly volatile during the review period, rising 
from 4.2% of GDP in 2018 to 6% in 2022 (its peak), before falling to 1.8% in 2024 (Chart 1.4 and 
Table 1.3). Direct investment is the main source of financing for the current account deficit.
Chart 1.4 Current and financial accounts, 2018-24 (% of GDP)
Note:
The negative values of the financial account reflect the inflow of assets to Colombia.

https://suameca.banrep.gov.co/estadisticas-economicas/#/catalogo.
1.23.  As Colombia's trade is largely concentrated in a few primary products (especially oil and its 
derivatives, coal, and coffee), its trade balance is highly exposed to volatility in international prices 
for these products, which in turn affect the terms of trade.
1.24.  In 2018 and 2019, the current account deficit remained within the 4% range, before shrinking 
in 2020 (to 3.4%) owing to the fall in domestic demand caused by the pandemic. However, in 2021, 
this situation was reversed as the economy recovered, as 10.8% GDP growth created greater 
demand for imports, coinciding with an increase in freight costs as a result of the shipping container 
shortage and problems in global supply chains, which exacerbated the deficit. In 2022, the current 
account recorded its largest deficit in the wake of greater transfers (payments) of profits abroad by 
companies with FDI, widening the primary income deficit (factor income) despite the higher value 
of exports; higher imports (record values); and increased imports of transport and travel services.35 
In 2023, the deficit narrowed as a result of the substantial drop in imports (inputs, capital goods for 
industry, transport equipment, fuels and lubricants)36, which improved the balance of goods, and as

a result of a smaller deficit in the balance of services, mainly because of the recovery in tourism37 
and fewer imports of transport services, alongside the standardization of global supply chains.38 
Furthermore, the fall in international commodity prices (oil and coal) reduced profit transfers and,

1.25.  In 2024, the current account deficit was 1.8% of GDP. This slight reduction was due to the 
improvement in the balance of services and primary income (factor income), together with the 
secondary income surplus, which offset the deficit in trade in goods.40
1.26.  During the review period, direct investment and financial derivatives and other investments41 
were the main sources of financing for the current deficit. In addition, special drawing rights (SDRs), 
which form part of the financial account, helped to ease the strain on the current account during the 
pandemic.42

1.3  Trade in goods and services
1.27.  Between 2018 and 2023, exports and imports of goods and services as a percentage of GDP 
ranged from 35% to 40%, except in 2020 and 2022 (Table 1.4). The drop to 33.1% in 2020 and the 
increase to 47.2% in 2022 were a consequence of, among other factors mentioned above, the 
international economic situation stemming from the pandemic and higher international commodity 
prices.

1.28.  Trade in goods as a percentage of GDP remains relatively modest (Table 1.4), according to 
the authorities, owing to factors such as the use of non-tariff measures43, the lack of infrastructure 
and logistical services, excessive regulation and overlap among government agencies.44 However,

according to estimates by ESCAP and the World Bank, trade costs fell between 2018 and 2022 (most 
recent available data).45
1.29.  Overall, the share of international trade (of goods and services) fluctuated between 2018 and 
2024 owing to various external shocks, especially the pandemic in 2020, which had a major impact 
on international trade, causing it to fall to 33.1% of GDP in 2020 (Chart 1.5). In 2022, that share 
was 47.2% owing to the higher international prices of products such as oil and its derivatives, one 
of the country's main export products. The economic downturn in 2023 and 2024 led to a contraction 
in trade in general.
1.30.  The smaller contribution of goods exports to the growth of international trade in Colombia in 
2018, 2019 and 2023 was the result of low international prices for major export products. However, 
between 2021 and 2022, when international prices showed signs of improving, goods exports 
contributed significantly to trade growth. However, imports of goods, which respond primarily to 
private consumption and capital formation, have the greatest impact on the growth of international 
trade in Colombia.

1.3.1  Trends and patterns in trade in merchandise and services
1.31.  Colombia continues to be a net importer of goods. In addition, it mostly exports primary 
goods, the performance of which is closely linked to international market prices.
1.32.  The composition of goods exports remained relatively stable throughout the period reviewed. 
Mineral products, which include crude oil, coal and derivative products, continue to be Colombia's 
main export products (Chart 1.6); in 2023, crude oil and coal accounted for 25.4% and 16.2%, 
respectively, of total export value (32.8% and 15.7% in 2018).46 In addition, Colombia remains one 
of the main global exporters of coffee, bananas and flowers, considered the second most important 
export products, followed, since 2019, by precious stones (emeralds) and precious metals, which 
exceeded chemical products (Chart 1.6 and Table A1.1).

1.33.  Although the level of concentration of goods exports remained high between 2018 and 2023, 
the Herfindahl-Hirschman Index (HHI) fell from 1,455 points to 1,063 points47; in 2024, according 
to the authorities, it was 936 points. These data show that Colombia is achieving some degree of 
diversification as, according to the National Administrative Department of Statistics (DANE), the 
value of non-traditional export products increased between 2018 and 2023.48
1.34.  The United States remains the primary destination for Colombian exports, accounting for 
between 27% and 31.1% of exports during the review period, followed by the EU-2749 and, since 
2022, Panama, which has replaced China in third place. This is because of a change in the 
composition of goods exported to these two markets.50 Since 2021, India has become a major 
market for coal exports (Chart 1.7).51 These five markets accounted for 56.2% of Colombia's total 
exports in 2018 and 61.3% in 2023, indicating that exports continue to be dependent on a few 
markets (Table A1.2).

1.35.  The structure of imports also remained stable during the period analysed. Manufacturing 
accounts for the majority of imports, although its share fell from 80.2% to 75.0% of total imports 
between 2018 and 2023.52 About half of imports of manufactured products are machinery and 
mechanical appliances and electrical equipment, chemicals (mainly medicines) and transport 
equipment (primarily motor vehicles) (Chart 1.8 and Table A1.3). Plastics, textiles and mineral 
products (mainly petroleum products) are also among the main imported products. Colombia also 
imports agricultural products, such as cereals (maize and wheat) and agribusiness products (animal 
feed) (Table A1.3).

1.3.2  Trends and patterns in FDI
1.38.  In 2022, FDI recovered, exceeding pre-pandemic amounts (Table 1.6). The services sector, 
followed by the petroleum and manufacturing industries are the main recipients of FDI. Within the 
services sector, FDI is attracted almost exclusively by financial and business activities, as well as 
trade, which includes some tourism-related activities. In 2022, there was a strong inflow of foreign 
capital into Colombia's financial and business sectors through a series of acquisitions, in addition to 
a substantial increase in FDI from Anguilla thanks to transactions in the mining sector (Table 1.6).53 
In general, during the review period, FDI flows came mainly from the United States and Spain.

2  TRADE AND INVESTMENT REGIMES
2.1  General framework
2.1.  Colombia is a Republic that has a presidential system and is governed by three independent 
branches: the Executive, the Legislature and the Judiciary. During the period under review, the 
Political Constitution of Colombia of 1991 underwent some reforms (Table 2.1).
Table 2.1 Reforms to the Political Constitution (non-exhaustive list)
Legislative act
Characteristics
No. 4 of 2019
Reform of the tax control regime overseen by the Office of the Comptroller-General of the 
Republic. The Comptroller of the Republic is elected by Congress.
No. 5 of 2019
Royalties and Compensation Scheme. The General Royalties Scheme (SGR) is a new 
system for coordinating between the central administration and regional entities to ensure 
the efficient use and distribution of income generated from the utilization of non-
renewable natural resources. These funds are used to finance projects that contribute to 
social, economic and environmental development. 
No. 2 of 2023
Congress meets for two ordinary sessions each year, which together form a single 
legislature. The first session runs from 20 July to 16 December, and the second from 
16 February to 20 June. Draft statutory lawsa are passed between 16 February and 
15 March, and the Political Constitution cannot be amended during this period.
No. 3 of 2023
Creation of the Agrarian and Rural Jurisdiction, whose courts and tribunals provide 
effective access to justice for, and ensure the protection of, farmers, ethnic groups, Black, 
Afro-Colombian, Palenquera and Raizal communities, indigenous peoples and 
communities, the Roma community and victims of the armed conflict.
a
Statutory laws regulate the following: (a) individuals' fundamental rights and duties and the 
procedures and remedies for protecting them; (b) the administration of justice; (c) the organization 
of, and rules governing, political parties, rules governing the opposition and electoral functions; 
(d) institutions and mechanisms for citizen participation; (e) states of emergency; and (f) electoral 
equality of candidates for the Presidency of the Republic.
Source:
Single Regulatory Information System (SUIN-JURISCOL), Acts Amending the Political Constitution 
of 1991. Viewed at: https://www.suin-juriscol.gov.co/legislacion/actoslegislativoscp.html.
2.2.  The President of the Republic is the Head of State. The President and Vice-President are elected 
together through universal suffrage every four years and, since 2018, cannot be re-elected.54 The 
Executive Branch comprises 18 ministries and 5 technical entities known as "administrative 
departments".55 In 2022, the Ministry of Sport and the Ministry of Technology and Innovation, which 
were previously administrative departments, were established.
2.3.  The Congress of the Republic, comprising the Senate (102 members) and the House of 
Representatives (172 members), is the Legislative Branch. Members of both houses are elected 
through universal suffrage for a renewable four-year term.
2.4.  The Judiciary consists of the Constitutional Court, the Supreme Court of Justice, the Council of 
State, the National Commission of Judicial Discipline, the Superior Council of the Judiciary, the Office 
of the Attorney General of the Nation, the Agrarian and Rural Jurisdiction established in 2023, the 
courts, and judges. Colombia has two special jurisdictions: (a) the authorities of indigenous peoples 
can exercise judicial functions within their territories, in accordance with their own rules, procedures 
and customs, provided that they do run contrary to the Constitution or the law; and (b) the 
jurisdiction for peace, for the equitable resolution of individual and community conflicts.56 The 
Military Criminal Justice system, which is under the purview of the Executive Branch, can also 
exercise judicial functions.
54 Article 197 of the Political Constitution, as amended by Legislative Act No. 2 of 2015.
55 The administrative departments are part of the Government; they have the same hierarchical 
structure as ministries but do not have the right of legislative initiative. They are technical entities tasked with 
running and coordinating a service and providing the Executive Branch with the information it needs for 
decision-making. Information viewed at: https://www.dane.gov.co/files/induccion-institucional/doc/guia-
elementos-basicos-estado-colombiano.pdf.
56 The special jurisdictions have powers in very specific areas; the powers of an indigenous jurisdiction 
are limited to the community's territory, based on its customs and respecting its autonomy, while the 
jurisdiction for peace "seeks to bring about the comprehensive and peaceful resolution of community or 
individual conflicts" (Article 1 of Law No. 497/1999).

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 29 -
 
2.5.  The Political Constitution is the highest-ranking instrument in Colombia's legal system, 
followed, in descending order, by international treaties57, laws (including laws approving 
international treaties), decrees (including single regulatory decrees), resolutions, circulars, 
departmental ordinances and municipal agreements.
2.6.  National revenue and tax contributions must be established by law. Congress sets out the 
general rules governing foreign trade and those amending tariffs, charges and other provisions 
concerning the customs regime.58 However, Law No. 1609 stipulates that amendments to tariffs, 
charges and other provisions related to the customs regime may be enacted through general 
Executive decrees and resolutions issued by the Ministry of Trade, Industry and Tourism (MinCIT), 
the Ministry of Finance and Public Credit and the National Tax and Customs Directorate (DIAN).59
2.7.  The negotiation and signing of international treaties falls within the exclusive purview of the 
President of the Republic, together with the competent minister or ministers or the Council of 
Ministers. Once signed, treaties require approval by Congress for ratification.60 An economic or trade 
agreement may be implemented provisionally, provided that the domestic ratification process is 
under way.61
2.8.  The procedure for preparing laws has not been modified since the previous review.62 Draft laws 
may be prepared by the Senate, the House of Representatives, the Executive Branch or 5% of the 
electorate.63 They are approved after being debated twice in each House of Congress. The President 
may request Congress to pass a draft law as a matter of urgency.
2.9.  Under the Constitution, the President of the Republic may be invested with exceptional and 
temporary legislative powers in order to issue decree laws and legislative decrees with the force of 
law.64 Legislative decrees are issued in times of emergency (war abroad, state of domestic unrest 
and state of emergency) and are subject to oversight by the Constitutional Court. Congress may 
repeal and amend decree laws and legislative decrees at any time on its own initiative.65
2.10.  During the period under review, Colombia continued to "overhaul its legal system", seeking 
to simplify and streamline regulations. Colombia is still working to compile all existing regulations 
on a given topic in one new legal standard known as a Single Regulatory Decree (DUR).
2.2  Trade Policy formulation and objectives
2.11.  MinCIT remains responsible for formulating trade policy and implementing the decisions of 
the Higher Council for Foreign Trade (CSCE), which is the highest authority in this area.66 The CSCE 
sets out the broad lines of the country's trade policy, approves the negotiation of trade agreements 
and issues recommendations on all matters relating to foreign trade and competitiveness. MinCIT's 
Committee on Customs, Tariff and Foreign Trade Matters (Triple A Committee) advises the CSCE. 
The Triple A Committee also analyses the customs and foreign trade regime and issues 
recommendations on, inter alia, tariff changes and the price stabilization mechanism.67 In addition, 
the National Economic and Social Policy Council (CONPES) manages Colombia's economic and social 
development policies.
57 Article 93 of the Political Constitution stipulates that international human rights treaties rank 
alongside the Constitution.
58 Article 150 of the Political Constitution.
59 Law No. 1609.
60 Article 241.10 of the Political Constitution. The Constitutional Court ensures that the agreement does 
not violate the provisions of the Constitution.
61 Articles 150, 189, 224 and 241 of the Political Constitution.
62 WTO document WT/TPR/S/372/Rev.1, 18 September 2018.
63 A draft law can also be prepared by 30% of departmental deputies or municipal councillors.
64 Article 150.10 of the Political Constitution.
65 Articles 150, 212-215 and 341 of the Political Constitution.

the Director of the National Planning Department and the Governor of the Central Bank of Colombia.

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 30 -
 
2.12.  MinCIT represents Colombia in international trade negotiations. Mechanisms are in place to 
ensure consultation with the private sector when defining the trade negotiation strategy. MinCIT 
consults with private sector and trade union representatives when formulating trade policy. 
Moreover, the National Trade Facilitation Committee (NTFC) was established in August 2018; it 
involves eight public sector entities, six private sector entities and six regional dialogue forums and 
sectoral forums (for sectors such as coffee, flowers, bananas, avocados, automotive parts and 
pharmaceutical products). MinCIT oversees trade policy implementation.68
2.13.  Generally speaking, Colombia's trade policy is outlined in national development plans (PNDs). 
During the period under review, Colombia implemented the PND 2018-22 (Pact for Colombia, Pact 
for Equity) and the PND 2022-26 (Colombia World Power of Life). The aim of the PND 2014-18 was 
to use trade agreements to increase exports, boosting production in the non-mining energy 
sectors.69 The aim of the PND 2022-26 is to drive the country's productive transformation, 
internationalization and climate action, with one specific objective being to diversify the exportable 
supply of non-energy goods and services (so that they account for 56% of total exports) and to 
diversify markets through regional and multilateral negotiations. The aim is also to foster the 
integration of Colombian companies into regional and global value chains.70 According to the 
authorities, to achieve the aforementioned goals, tariff and non-tariff measures need to be revised, 
and export procedures standardized and streamlined. To this end, MinCIT is working to identify 
potential trade barriers and to develop a strategy aimed primarily at increasing the production of 
industrial, agroindustrial and agricultural products, boosting modern services exports and promoting 
tourism.
2.14.  Since 2022, the main objective of Colombia's National Reindustrialization Policy has been to 
"transform an extractive economy into a knowledge-based, productive and sustainable economy 
built on four pillars: (a) a fair energy transition; (b) agroindustrialization and food sovereignty; 
(c) the reindustrialization of the health sector (in terms of both producing medicines and medical 
supplies and delivering health services); and (d) reindustrialization for defence and life".71
2.15.  Colombia considers trade to be key to achieving these goals. It is therefore seeking to 
promote: (a) internationalization through regional integration and integration into non-traditional 
markets in Asia and Africa; (b) the closing of gaps in the poorest areas of the country; (c) the 
inclusion of MSMEs and the popular and community economy72 in value chains through the "Zasca" 
initiative or reindustrialization centres73; (d) investment in R&D&I so that it accounts for 0.5% of 
GDP; and (e) reindustrialization. To that end, Colombia has focused mainly on developing a public 
policy to strengthen the popular economy. Public-popular partnerships have been developed to 
ensure that those involved in the popular economy are included in collective representation bodies, 
and regulatory amendments have been enacted to enable those actors to take part in various 
markets, including, for example, government procurement.74 In addition, "Public-Popular 
Partnership" contracts are being promoted for a wide range of goods and services.75 To eliminate 
disparities, efforts have been made to develop certain regions – such as the zones most affected by

2.16.  The environment has become more important in the development of Colombia's trade policy.77 
In order to promote the use of sustainable production methods and regenerate the environment, 
the following objectives have been included in the PND 2022-26: increase non-conventional sources 
of electric power (raising the renewable energy production capacity by 2,000 MW); use resources 
more efficiently; end deforestation and restore degraded areas; cut CO2 emissions from the transport 
sector (to 2.14 million tonnes of particles); and ensure water-focused land use planning.78
2.17.  During the period under review, Colombia also implemented inclusion policies in several areas. 
In terms of trade policy, MinCIT seeks to strengthen the participation of women and vulnerable 
groups in agro-industry and the rural economy, through the provision of special credit lines and new 
financial products and through technical assistance programmes (Table 2.2).

2.19.  At Ministerial Conferences, Colombia has underscored the need to make progress on 
agriculture negotiations and scale back domestic support measures and has reiterated the

multilateral system and its support for making procedural improvements within the WTO and 
restoring the dispute settlement mechanism.81 During the seventh Appraisal of the Trade Policy 
Review Mechanism (TPRM) in 2023, Colombia expressed its support for including trade policies 
related to new and emerging issues, such as trade and environment and trade and inclusiveness,

2.20.  Colombia approved the Agreement on Trade Facilitation (TFA) through Law No. 1879 of 2018

Technology Agreement (ITA) and an observer to the plurilateral Agreements on Trade in Civil Aircraft 
and on Government Procurement.84 In March 2025, Colombia deposited its instrument of acceptance 
for the Agreement on Fisheries Subsidies.85
2.21.  During the period under review, two specific trade measures implemented by Colombia were 
challenged.86 Colombia was not involved in any dispute as a complainant and participated in 
21 disputes as a third party.87 In 2024, the European Union initiated a procedure to examine whether 
Colombia had complied with the recommendations and rulings in the arbitrators' award and panel 
report on Colombian duties on frozen fries.88
2.22.  During the period under review, Colombia notified the WTO of its trade and trade-related 
measures (Table A2.1). However, as at December 2024, it had outstanding notifications in areas 
such as agriculture, quantitative restrictions, subsidies and countervailing measures, and state 
trading enterprises.89
2.3.2  Regional and preferential agreements
2.23.  Colombia participates in several trade agreements (Chart 2.1) and is a founding member of 
the Andean Community of Nations (CAN). It is also a member of the Pacific Alliance and of the 
Latin American Integration Association (LAIA), through which it has signed partial scope 
agreements.
2.24.  CAN is one of the Colombia's main regional trading systems.90 It is a free trade area for goods 
produced in the region, with common intra-community trade rules concerning customs regulations, 
tariff nomenclature, services, trade defence measures, intellectual property and technical 
regulations. Trade agreements with third-party partners may be negotiated by member countries as 
a community, jointly or individually. Changes that took place within CAN during the review period 
include the adoption of tariff preferences for goods produced in member countries' free zones in 
2019 and the introduction of digital certificates of origin in 2021. CAN is developing a community-

2.25.  During the review period, two new trade agreements entered into force: one with Israel 
(2020) and one with the United Kingdom (2022). In 2022, the Pacific Alliance signed a trade 
agreement with Singapore, and in 2024 Colombia signed an economic partnership agreement with 
the United Arab Emirates; these agreements have not yet entered into force.
2.26.  The Agreement between Colombia and Israel entered into force on 11 August 2020 and covers 
both goods and services. The tariff liberalization process is subject to a transition period, during which 
tariffs will be eliminated and reduced; this period will come to an end in 2038 for Colombia and 2031 
for Israel. When the agreement entered into force, 836 tariff lines became duty free (in addition to the 
4,175 lines that already had a 0% tariff). Colombia will eliminate tariffs on 1,634 lines in 2029. At the 
end of the tariff elimination period, 85.4% of tariff lines will be duty free. The Andean Price Band 
System will continue to apply. As an exception, Colombia kept measures relating to the taxation of 
alcoholic beverages in place until 2021. Colombia also negotiated tariff quotas for 12 categories of 
agricultural products (cut flowers and food preparations). The rules of origin permit bilateral cumulation 
between the Parties. While the Agreement does not contain any specific conditions on gender or the 
environment, the Parties undertook to implement the Agreement in a manner that is consistent with 
protecting and preserving the environment and promoting sustainable development.91
2.27.  The Agreement between the United Kingdom and Colombia, Ecuador and Peru is a trade 
continuity agreement that maintains the terms negotiated for the Agreement between the 
European Union and the Andean countries. As such, the tariff elimination programme, rules of origin 
and the Andean Price Band System apply to the United Kingdom. In the Agreement with the United 
Kingdom, some preferences were renegotiated and the legal and technical amendments necessary 
for their implementation were made. The Agreement was signed on 15 May 2019 and entered into 
force on 28 June 2022; it was provisionally applied between 2019 and 2022.92

2.3.3  Other agreements and arrangements
2.29.  Colombia is a member of the Global System of Trade Preferences (GSTP) and benefits from 
the unilateral tariff preferences granted by Australia, Japan and New Zealand.
2.4  Investment regime
2.30.  Colombia's domestic and foreign investment regime is still governed by Law No. 9 of 1991 
and Single Regulatory Decree No. 1068 of 2015, as amended by Decree No. 119 of 2017. The 
Political Constitution guarantees equal treatment of foreign and domestic investment93 and Colombia 
accords national treatment to investments made by foreign companies. Colombia has a relatively 
open investment regime; investment is allowed in almost all sectors with some exceptions, mainly 
for national security reasons (Table 2.4).

2.31.  No prior authorization is required for foreign investment, except in the mining and 
hydrocarbons sector and the financial services and insurance sector.94 However, registration with 
the Central Bank of Colombia (BRC) is mandatory to be able to transfer profits or reinvest them in 
the country, and to sell or liquidate investments. Registration comes in two forms: under the general 
foreign exchange regime (sectors other than hydrocarbons and mining) or under the special 
exchange regime, for the hydrocarbons and mining sector.95
2.32.  Subsidiaries of foreign companies that are engaged in oil, natural gas, coal, ferronickel or 
uranium prospecting or extraction, or exclusively in services linked to the hydrocarbons sector, are 
eligible for the special foreign exchange regime, under which they are not required to re-inject into 
the foreign exchange market the proceeds from sales denominated in foreign currency.96 This means 
that they can receive payment for their exports directly in accounts held abroad or directly at the 
parent company, without the resources having to enter Colombia. They may use the foreign 
exchange market only to transfer capital abroad in the event of liquidation, to transfer profits from 
the domestic sale of oil or natural gas or from the provision of services associated with the 
hydrocarbons sector, or where foreign exchange is required to cover expenses in local currency. As 
of 2020, the aforementioned subsidiaries are also authorized to use the foreign exchange market 
for the transfer abroad of the foreign currency equivalent of amounts generated from other 
operations.97 Companies can, however, notify the BRC if they wish to opt for the general regime and 
will be exempted from the special regime for at least 10 years. In this case, all foreign exchange 
transactions would be subject to the standard foreign exchange rules, meaning that they would have 
to be conducted through the foreign exchange market.
2.33.  Investors may establish themselves in Colombia either through commercial companies, such 
as simplified joint stock companies (SAS), limited liability companies and public limited companies, 
or through subsidiaries of foreign companies. SAS is the company form most commonly used by 
business owners, as it provides flexibility in the creation and management of the company and 
freedom for shareholders in determining the company's structure and functioning.98
2.34.  The aim of the Committee on Foreign Direct Investment and on the Facilitation Service for 
Attracting Foreign Direct Investment (IED-SIFAI Committee) is to put forward policy to promote and 
attract foreign investment and improve the business climate.99 ProColombia, under the purview of 
94 Authorizations are issued by the Ministry of Mining and Energy and the Financial Supervisory

MinCIT, is the organization responsible for promoting, inter alia, foreign investment (except in the 
mining and hydrocarbons sectors), exports (except mining and energy) and the country's image. Its 
roles include facilitating contact between investors through trade promotion activities and providing 
specialized services to business leaders interested in investing in Colombia.100 Between 2018 and 
2024, ProColombia provided support to over 1,000 investment projects, in areas such as agri-food, 
tourism, pharmaceutical products, cosmetics and chemical products.101
2.35.  The National Development Plan 2022-26 lists the following as priority sectors for attracting 
investment: (a) agroindustrialization and food sovereignty; (b) a fair energy transition; 
(c) reindustrialization of the health sector; (d) reindustrialization for defence and life; and 
(e) territories (manufacturing and value-added industrial processes).
2.36.  A 2019 study conducted by the National Economic and Social Policy Council (CONPES) found 
that the procedures for investing in Colombia were extremely complex, information was widely 
scattered, and the regulations were hard to understand. CONPES concluded that a Single Window 
for Investment (VUI)102 should be created to facilitate procedures and reduce related costs.103 In 
December 2021, MinCIT set up the VUI platform, which includes a service to facilitate foreign direct 
investment (SIED). Its main aim is to facilitate processes and procedures for investors. The SIED 
also has tools for managing consultations on tax, financial and intellectual property matters and for 
resolving investment-related conflicts in order to prevent international disputes.104
2.37.  Colombia has eight reciprocal investment promotion and protection agreements (APPRIs) in 
force, with China, France, India, Japan, Peru, Spain, Switzerland and the United Kingdom, and five 
signed, with Brazil, Singapore, Türkiye, the United Arab Emirates and the Bolivarian Republic of 
Venezuela.105 Colombia is a member of ICSID and MIGA.
2.4.1  Investment incentives
2.38.  Colombia continues to use special regimes to promote both national and foreign investment. 
These regimes are geared towards generating jobs, fostering reindustrialization, promoting energy 
production from non-conventional sources to boost the energy transition, strengthening exports and 
integrating new technologies.
2.39.  Colombia has several incentive schemes, such as free zones106; incentives for the purchase of 
raw materials and capital goods not produced in Colombia107; incentives to foster the production of 
energy from non-conventional sources108; incentives for automotive production109; and the Shipyard 
Industry Promotion Programme.110 These regimes offer tariff concessions and other tax benefits 
(Table 2.5).

3  TRADE POLICIES AND PRACTICES BY MEASURE
3.1  Measures directly affecting imports
3.1.1  Customs procedures, valuation, and requirements
3.1.  Colombia approved the WTO Agreement on Trade Facilitation (TFA) in 2018.1 In line with this 
Agreement and the National Logistics Policy of 2020, guidelines were established to simplify customs 
procedures, which required changes to customs regulations, in addition to the modernization of 
electronic customs systems.2 Changes to customs procedures, which will be implemented gradually 
once the electronic systems have been modernized, are primarily aimed at reducing times and costs 
and using improved border control to prevent smuggling.
3.2.  During the review period, various changes were made to the legislation governing customs 
procedures. Decree No. 390 of 20163 was replaced by Decree No. 1165 of 2019, which has itself 
been amended several times. The new Decree established new regulations for customs procedures, 
customs declarations and the penalty regime to improve the performance of the customs system in 
accordance with trade policy (Box 3.1). The most recent amendment, made in 2024, introduced 
significant changes to foreign trade operations.4 However, not all provisions have yet been 
implemented, as some of them will be rolled out gradually. In some cases, the provisions can only 
be implemented when regulations therefor have been adopted and, in other cases, only when the 
computer systems have been modernized.5 For instance, the new rules establish that the customs 
service must be in operation 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. However, no regulations have yet been 
issued for this provision, which came into force on 7 June 2024, so the hours defined by the Sectional 
Customs Directorates remain in place for the time being.6 Other examples are the use of the advance 
import declaration (Article 21) and electronic traceability devices (Article 64), the destruction of 
waste and scrap (Article 63), forms of provision (Article 59) and new terms for processing advance 
ruling applications (Article 39). These aspects are still pending regulation and therefore currently 
have no practical impact on customs procedures.
Box 3.1 Main amendments to customs legislation, 2018-24

in the decrees implementing the Framework Law issued by the national Government" (Article 5.4 of Law 
No. 1609 of 2013).

This introduces a new definition of goods subject to customs control; the obligation to use the advance import 
declaration, with exceptions; new consequences for non-compliance with the consolidated payment of 
customs duties for AEOs, users of simplified customs procedures and users with a special import declaration; 
and new measures to regulate the exit of goods from free zones to the rest of the world.
Source:
Prepared by the WTO Secretariat.
3.3.  Importers (exporters) must be domiciled and/or legally represented in Colombia and must be 
registered in the Single Tax Register (RUT)7 and in the Single Window for Foreign Trade (VUCE). 
Importers may act directly, for any amount, or through a customs agency. The importer or customs 
broker transmits the import declaration to the customs authority, using official forms, through the 
electronic information services of the National Tax and Customs Directorate (DIAN), or, 
exceptionally, through documentary evidence if authorized to do so by DIAN.8 Original supporting 
documents must be obtained before the import declaration is submitted.9 These documents, 
including import licences and authorizations, are processed through the VUCE.10 Since 2020, upon 
receipt of the import declaration with all required supporting documents, the Ministry of Trade, 
Industry and Tourism (MinCIT) has one working day to approve it, once approval for the product to 
be imported has been received from the control entity. If the declaration is incomplete, the 
authorities have one working day to inform the applicant through the VUCE. The importers 
(exporters) must keep the supporting documents and payment receipts for five years for subsequent 
verification or control.11
3.4.  Customs duties and other monetary obligations must be paid through banks and financial 
institutions authorized by DIAN12, i.e. banking institutions authorized by the Financial Supervisory 
Authority of Colombia, which must also provide coverage throughout the national territory.
3.5.  Regarding Authorized Economic Operators (AEOs), one of the main changes introduced during 
the review period is that, in addition to importers and exporters13, customs agencies (since 2018)14 
and port facilities and operators (since 2020)15 may acquire this status. At the time of writing of this 
report, Mutual Recognition Agreements for AEOs had been signed with, inter alia: the Pacific Alliance 
(2018), the Andean Community (2019), Costa Rica (2019), Brazil (2021), Uruguay (2021) and the 
United States (2023).
3.6.  The National Trade Facilitation Committee (NTFC) was established in 2018, with the 
participation of public and private sector entities, and regional and sectoral dialogue forums.16 The 
authorities have indicated that the NTFC is consulted before customs regulations and procedures are 
formulated.
3.7.  The customs authority uses criteria based on risk analysis techniques, through a risk 
management system, to determine whether cargo requires a documentary or physical inspection in 
the place of arrival, the warehouse or the free zone. Goods can be cleared immediately (automatic 
release or green channel) or be subject to documentary inspection (yellow channel) or physical 
inspection (red channel). If the inspection is non-intrusive, the process is free of charge for 
operators. When the customs authority determines that a customs inspection must be carried out, 
the declarant must be present.17 Colombia continues to use a simultaneous inspection policy for 
imports through the Integrated Risk Management System, a VUCE module that brings together the 
various 
control 
authorities

and

channel. The amount of imports that underwent a physical inspection decreased (Table 3.1), 
averaging 7.6%.

3.9.  Inspection of the cargo (verification of the weight, number and condition of packages) is carried 
out by the customs authorities without opening it, except when it is considered necessary to do so 
owing to the risk profile of the goods in question.18
3.10.  The main legislation regulating customs value determination in Colombia, in hierarchical 
order, continue to be the WTO Customs Valuation Agreement (CVA) (Law No. 170 of 1994), 
Andean Decision No. 571 of 2003 and its Regulations (Resolution No. 1684 of 2014) and national 
standards.19 At the national level, during the review period, Decree No. 1165 of 2019 was partially 
amended by Decree No. 659 of 2024, with the aim of promoting security with a new penalty and 
seizure regime20 and reducing the time periods for issuing advance rulings (from 3 to 2 months) and 
for requesting information from users (from 45 to 30 calendar days), as well as extending the period 
for the user to respond (from 2 months to 2 months extendible, once only, for another two years).21 
Despite these amendments, Colombia continues to use the same valuation methods; the customs 
value is determined in accordance with the methods established in the CVA.
3.11.  Colombia continues to maintain a data bank of reference prices in order to keep its databases 
updated; this is used as a control tool for the risk management system when there are doubts about 
the value of imports.22 The use of a reference price does not lead to automatic rejection of the 
declared value for imported goods and declared values cannot be replaced with reference prices 
without a prior value study.23 In certain cases, the customs value may be declared provisionally, 
acting as a guarantee of payment of customs duties and of penalties and interest, if any.24
3.1.2  Rules of origin
3.12.  In 2018, Colombia began to use non-preferential rules of origin for goods subject to trade 
defence measures.25 The main criteria for determining such rules are similar to those used for 
preferential rules; there are criteria for products wholly obtained and resulting from a substantial 
transformation, and a clause indicating minimal operations that do not confer origin (de minimis).
3.13.  Colombia applies preferential rules of origin under its trade agreements. During the review 
period, Colombia expanded some of its trade agreements. In this context, rules of origin have been

3.1.3  Tariffs
3.1.3.1  Structure and features of the MFN tariff
3.14.  In 2024, the Colombian tariff contained 7,975 lines at the HS 2022 10-digit level. Colombia 
only applies ad valorem tariffs. Under the Andean Price Band System (SAFP), variable tariffs are 
applied to certain agricultural products.
3.15.  In 2024, Colombia's simple average applied MFN tariff rate, without taking the SAFP into 
account, was 6.8%, down from 7.1% in 2017. The average MFN tariff applied to agricultural products 
(WTO definition), at 16.1% (15.4% in 2017), was higher than the overall simple average, while for 
non-agricultural products, the average was lower, at 5.3% (5.8% in 2017). If the SAFP is taken into 
account, the simple average of MFN duties on agricultural products was 19.2% in 2024, down from 
20.7% in 2017 (Table 3.3).

3.16.  The main change to the structure of the MFN tariff applied by Colombia since 2017 has been 
the increase in tariff lines with a 0% tariff, rising from 49.7% of total lines in 2017 to 53.3% in 2024. 
As in 2017, in 2024 the structure of Colombia's MFN tariff consisted of 13 rates, ranging from 0% 
to 98%.26 In 2024, 94.4% of tariff lines were subject to rates lower than 15%, and 53.3% were 
duty-free. The increase in the percentage of lines with a 0% rate arises from the decision by the 
MinCIT Committee on Customs, Tariff and Foreign Trade Matters to promote industry.27 There are 
348 tariff lines subject to an MFN tariff above 25% (i.e. 30%, 35%, 40%, 60%, 70%, 80%, 94% or 
98%), including white maize (HS 1005), clothing (HS 61 and 62); vegetables (HS 0713); guts, 
bladders and stomachs of animals, other than fish (HS 0504); bovine meat (HS 02); rice (HS 1006); 
whey (HS 0404); and milk and cream (HS 0402) (Chart 3.1).
