3.17.  Colombia has bound all its tariff lines at rates of between 0% and 227%. The highest binding 
levels are on agricultural products, although the applied tariffs on these products are below those 
levels. Regarding non-agricultural products, fish and fish products are bound at levels between 35% 
and 104%; footwear, hides and skins, and arms and ammunition are bound at 35%, although applied 
tariffs on these products are below the bound tariff, especially for hides and skins (Table A3.1).
3.18.  In 2024, a comparison of the applied MFN tariff and the bound tariff, taking into account only 
those lines that are strictly comparable because of the change in nomenclature, showed that the 
applied tariff exceeded the bound tariff for seven tariff lines (Table 3.4). The authorities indicated 
that they were making the necessary revisions.

Andean Price Band System (SAFP)
3.19.  Colombia continues to use the Andean Community (CAN) SAFP for 13 agricultural products 
with the aim of stabilizing, through variable tariffs, the import price of these products, which tend 
to have volatile international prices.28 Every 15 days, CAN calculates a reference price based on the 
international price, which is then used to calculate the tariff duty applicable to the product 
concerned.29 This mechanism is optional.30 Colombia uses the SAFP for a total of 181 tariff lines31, 
although the SAFP was suspended for 24 lines and thus currently covers a total of 157 lines. 
However, in addition to CAN, the National Tax and Customs Directorate (DIAN) also publishes 
reference tariffs for the products covered (DIAN tariffs), which sometimes differ from the tariffs 
actually applied at the time of importation.32 For the 24 lines for which application has been 
suspended, the Ministry of Agriculture, in coordination with DIAN, establishes the applied tariffs, 
which are published in decrees.
3.20.  During the review period, Colombia suspended application of the SAFP for certain products 
and established a fixed tariff in its place; for example, for whole milk, maize and white rice, rates 
were set at 98% and 94%, 40% and 80%, respectively.33 Moreover, during the pandemic, use of 
the SAFP was temporarily suspended for yellow flint maize (HS 1005.90.11.00), sorghum 
(HS 1007.90.00.00), soya (HS 1201.90.00.00), soyabean cake (HS 2304.00.00.00)34 and, since 
2022, wheat (HS 1001.19.00.00, 1001.99.10.10, 1001.99.10.90 and 1001.99.20.00).35
3.21.  The SAFP, which changes every 15 days, may affect predictability and alter the costs of 
imports. For this reason, the functioning of the SAFP is analysed, with two main objectives: (1) to

seek to establish whether international prices affect the reference prices used by CAN, as these are 
used as the basis for calculating the SAFP variable tariff; and (2) to determine whether there is a 
link, in the opposite case, between the tariff and the quantity imported, as there were no recorded 
imports of certain products, such as chicken cuts (9 HS lines), yellow maize (7 HS lines) and palm oil 
(5 HS lines), during the review period (Table A3.2).
3.22.  For the first objective, three products - palm oil, white maize and barley - were chosen as 
examples owing to the availability of data.36 The correlation coefficients between the international 
prices and the CAN reference prices were calculated for these products; these were 0.91 (palm oil), 
0.60 (white maize) and -0.03 (barley). The behaviour of both prices in the period 2018-24 
(or 2018-23) was compared for each product (Chart 3.2). For palm oil and white maize, the trend 
in both prices is similar, which is consistent with the correlation coefficients: the bigger the 
coefficient, the greater the link between the international prices and the CAN reference prices. 
However, it should be noted that the CAN reference price is adjusted to the international price with 
a short delay.37 In the case of barley, however, the international price does not influence the CAN 
reference price. Despite the fact that the SAFP was designed to reduce fluctuations in international 
prices, these fluctuations are not fully or immediately reflected in the reference prices as markets 
are much more dynamic today than when the System was created.
Chart 3.2 Comparison between international prices and CAN reference prices for 
three products subject to the SAFP
36 Palm oil and white maize were chosen because they are the only products whose international prices 
are expressed in the same metric unit used by CAN: dollars per metric tonne. Although barley prices are not 
expressed in metric tonnes, this product was chosen because CAN did not change its reference price in the 
period 2018-23, even though the international price fluctuated.
37 In the case of palm oil, there was a delay of three to four periods between 2021 and 2022. The 
three-period delay is clear in the increase in the international price recorded on 15 January 2021, which was 
only reflected in the CAN price as of 15 February 2021, while the four-period delay can be seen in the fall of 
the international price on 15 July 2022, which did not affect the CAN price until 15 September 2022. In the 
case of white maize, delays of up to two periods were recorded. The drop in the international price on 
15 May 2019 was not reflected in the CAN price until 15 June 2019, and the reduction of the international price 
on 15 July 2022 was not reflected in a reduced CAN price until 15 August 2022.

3.23.  To achieve the second objective, a comparison was made of the imported quantities of all 
13 products subject to the SAFP (quantity imported) and their respective tariffs between 2018 and 
2024 (Table A3.1). The tariffs considered were those suggested by CAN in their circulars 
(CAN tariffs)38, those calculated by DIAN for the SAFP (DIAN tariffs)39 and those actually applied 
(applied tariffs) at the time of import.40 The charts indicate that, in some cases, the tariffs suggested 
by CAN are aligned with the tariff calculated by DIAN, especially in the case of soya bean oil, pork, 
yellow maize and soya beans. There is a link in the case of palm oil, chicken cuts, wheat, white and 
raw sugar, and barley; and there is no link in the case of white maize and white rice. The relationship 
between the DIAN tariff and the applied tariff also varies from one product to another (the most 
obvious examples are white rice and barley). Furthermore, the applied tariffs are lower than the 
DIAN tariffs in all cases. In general, neither the DIAN tariffs nor those actually applied affect the 
quantity imported as most of them concern basic necessities with relatively stable demand. For 
instance, in the case of pork, the applied tariff remained relatively stable, while there were significant 
variations in the quantity imported, and, in the case of yellow maize, there were increases in both 
the applied tariff and the quantity imported. (Table A3.1).
3.1.3.2  Preferential tariffs
3.24.  Colombia grants preferential treatment to imports from partners with which it has preferential 
agreements (Section 2.3.2). In most cases, the average preferential tariff is below the average 
MFN tariff, ranging from 0% in the agreement with the Andean Community to 7.2% in the agreement 
with Nicaragua. The agreements with the highest average preferential tariff are the partial scope 
agreements signed under the LAIA framework, although Colombia has also negotiated increased 
market access agreements with several of these partners (Chile, Mexico and MERCOSUR).41 In all 
cases, preferential tariffs are higher for agricultural products, averaging 19.1% (Nicaragua) and 
18.6% (Panama), slightly below the average MFN tariff of 19.2% (Table 3.5).

39 Simple average of the tariffs published in the DIAN circulars on total tariffs under the Andean Price

3.1.3.3  Tariff quotas
3.25.  Colombia has tariff quotas for various agricultural products. However, in its most recent 
notification, from 2020, Colombia notified that these quotas have not been activated as the in-quota 
tariff is higher than the MFN tariff.42
3.26.  To promote the use of clean-technology vehicles, between 2017 and 2025, Colombia had tariff 
quotas on imports of electric vehicles and electric charging systems, with in-quota tariffs of 5% and 
0% respectively. According to the regulations, import quotas should be increased gradually during 
the period of validity of the measure (until 2027).43 However, this was abolished in 2024 following 
a study by the Ministry of the Environment and Sustainable Development, which determined that 
the measure did not have the desired impact on reducing emissions.44
3.27.  Colombia also applies tariff quotas for hybrid vehicles (12 HS lines).45 In-quota imports have 
a 5% tariff (out-of-quota rate of 35%); the allocated quota increased gradually during the review 
period, from 1,500 vehicles (2018-19) to 2,300 (2020-22), and to 3,000 since 2023. Use of the 
quota ranged from 33.6% to 100% in the period 2018-24. In 2020, during the pandemic, the rate 
of use was 233.6%.
3.28.  In 2021, a new automotive quota, the Tariff Instrument for Automotive Improvement and 
Road Safety (IAMAS), was established. It was aimed at enterprises in the Automotive Industry 
Promotion Programme (PROFIA) or involved in processing and assembly procedures in order to 
promote the competitiveness of the automotive industry, attract investment and improve the vehicle 
fleet.46 This measure will be in force until 2027, with an in-quota tariff of 0%, and an out-of-quota 
tariff of 5%, 15% or 35% depending on the good in question.47 Access to these quotas is subject to 
requirements relating to production and investment48, and to road safety.49 However, the IAMAS 
quota cannot be used to import motor vehicles produced in Colombia by companies covered by 
PROFIA or the processing and assembly procedures. The quota is not fixed per unit, but by taking 
into account the amount authorized annually by the Higher Fiscal Policy Council (CONFIS).50
3.1.3.4  Tariff concessions
3.29.  Colombia grants tariff exemptions under various import and export regimes, such as the 
temporary import regime, Special Import-Export Systems (SEIEX or the Vallejo Plan),free zones and 
Special Economic Export Zones. The import regimes did not undergo substantial changes during the 
review period (Box 3.2). Goods imported into the free port of San Andrés, Providencia and 
Santa Catalina are also exempt from customs duties.51 In addition, tariff concessions are granted 
for donations and inputs and capital goods to promote industry in certain regions that, in terms of 
the overall development of the national economy, are lagging behind for reasons such as their 
geographical position and low level of productivity, employment or per capita income.52

44 Decree No. 1550 of 2024.

Box 3.2 Import regimes, 2024
Reimportation after outward processing (Article 196).
For the reimportation of goods exported temporarily for manufacturing, repair or processing, customs duties 
shall be payable only on the value added abroad, and the tariffs corresponding to the tariff subheading of the 
imported finished product shall be applied.
Reimportation in the same state (Article 198).
Temporarily or definitively exported goods may be imported without paying customs duties provided that they 
have not been modified abroad, they are clearly the same goods that were exported, the internal taxes have 
been cancelled and the profits obtained through exportation repatriated.
Importation under a guarantee (Article 199).
Goods may be imported without paying customs duties when they have been repaired abroad under a 
manufacturer's or supplier's guarantee or they replace other previously exported goods that were found to be 
damaged, defective or unsuitable for the purpose for which they were imported.
Temporary admission for re-exportation in the same state (Article 200).
Importation into the national customs territory, with the suspension of customs duties, of certain goods that 
are to be re-exported within a specified period without having undergone any modification other than normal 
wear and tear arising from use, and on the basis of which their circulation shall be restricted.
Temporary admission of leased goods (Article 211).
Capital goods, as well as their parts and accessories, may be imported temporarily when they are subject to 
a lease agreement with or without an option to buy, enter for a period of more than 6 months and pay the 
customs duties in force on the date of submission and acceptance of the declaration.
Temporary admission for inward processing (Article 222).
Temporary admission of capital goods for inward processing (Article 223).
This is the procedure under which temporary admission with suspension of customs duties is permitted for 
capital goods, as well as parts and spares, that are to be re-exported after repair or overhaul, for a period of 
no more than six months and on the basis of which their circulation is restricted. Where there is good cause, 
the customs authority may authorize an extension to the periods provided for in this Article for a length of 
time not exceeding the period initially granted.
Temporary admission under the Special Import-Export Systems (Article 228).
Admission is permitted with total or partial suspension of customs duties for goods that are to be exported 
fully or in part within a certain time frame after having undergone processing, manufacturing or repair, and 
the inputs required for such operations.
Admission is permitted for intermediate goods, capital goods and spare parts used in the production of goods 
for export or for the provision of services directly linked to the production or export of such goods or for the 
export of services. The circulation of goods imported in this way is restricted.
Temporary admission for industrial processing (Article 244).
This is the procedure under which temporary admission is permitted for raw materials and inputs that are to 
be industrially worked, processed or manufactured by Authorized Economic Operators (importers or exporters 
and customs users benefiting from the simplified procedure), on the basis of which their circulation shall be 
restricted.
Goods that have been industrially worked, processed or manufactured are to be exclusively for export, when 
so indicated by the DIAN Special Administrative Unit.
Temporary admission for industrial processing by importers (Article 245).
Temporary duty-free admission for raw materials and inputs that are to be industrially worked, processed or 
manufactured is permitted.
At least 30% of goods that have been industrially worked, processed or manufactured by importers must be 
exported.
Importation for processing and/or assembly (Article 250).
This is the procedure under which goods are imported for processing and/or assembly, by industries 
recognized and authorized for the purpose by the DIAN Special Administrative Unit, and on the basis of which 
their circulation shall be restricted. Parties authorized to use this procedure must submit an import declaration, 
indicating the means of processing and/or assembly, without payment of customs duties.
Source:
Decree No. 1165 of 2019, and amendments thereto.

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia

3.1.4  Other charges affecting imports
3.1.4.1  Sales tax (VAT)
3.31.  Imports, like goods produced in Colombia, are subject to sales tax (VAT). The sale of movable 
and immovable property, the sale or transfer of rights on intangible assets related to industrial 
property, and the supply of services from abroad or within Colombia are also subject to VAT.55 During 
the period under review, Colombia maintained the same VAT rates: the general rate of 19%, the 
reduced rate of 5% and the 0% (exempt) rate.56 Colombia also exempts certain products and 
services57 from VAT, whether they are imported or produced locally.58 The taxable base for 
calculating VAT, in the case of imports, is the customs value plus import duties.
3.32.  The general VAT rate of 19% applies to 86% of all tariff lines, the reduced rate of 5% applies 
to 3% of the total, and 11% of lines have the 0% rate. For some products (85 tariff lines at the 
HS 10-digit level), Colombia applies a different VAT rate depending on the end use of the goods, 
e.g. maize and rice, which are subject to the 0% rate for human consumption and the 5% rate for 
industrial use, and track-laying tractors for agricultural use, which are exempt, while other 
track-laying tractors are subject to the 19% rate.59
3.33.  Imports of certain types of machinery and equipment not produced in Colombia are exempt 
from the payment of VAT, as are weapons and munitions for national defence; machinery for waste 
treatment and environmental monitoring and control; and goods and equipment imported under 
international cooperation agreements for public entities (excluding state-owned industrial and 
commercial enterprises).60 Expedited shipments of a value not exceeding USD 200 and from 
countries with which Colombia has free trade agreements are also exempt from VAT.61
3.1.4.2  National consumption tax (INC)
3.34.  Colombia continues to apply the national consumption tax (INC) on certain domestic and 
imported products, with ad valorem rates of 4%, 8%, 16% and 48%, except in the case of spirits, 
cigarettes, processed tobacco and plastic bags, which are subject to a compound rate. During the 
review period, the only rates that changed were those for spirits, cigarettes, processed tobacco and

56 Goods and services exempt from VAT include, inter alia: (a) fresh or chilled meat, eggs, milk, fish,

3.35.  The tax base for the INC on imported products is the customs value plus tariffs and VAT (if 
any). In the case of beer, the tax base for the INC depends on whether it is imported or domestically 
produced. For imported beer, the tax base is the customs value plus applied tariffs, plus a 30% 
marketing margin, whereas for local products, the tax base is the retail price.62
3.36.  The revenue collected from the INC on alcoholic beverages is allocated to the departments 
and used to finance the health and sports sectors.
3.1.4.3  National carbon tax
3.37.  Since 2016, Colombia has imposed a national carbon tax on the sale and import of fossil 
fuels.63 The tax takes the form of a specific rate set according to carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions, 
which means it varies depending on the type of fuel (Table 3.7).64 Some 80% of the revenue 
generated is invested in projects to reduce deforestation and coastal erosion and to preserve water 
sources and biodiversity, and the remaining 20% is allocated to the National Comprehensive 
Programme for the Substitution of Illicit Crops (PNIS), through the Fund for Sustainability and 
Climate Resilience (FONSUREC)65, and the Colombia Peace Fund. In 2023, the tax collected 
amounted to COP 575 billion, equivalent to 0.04% of GDP.66

3.1.4.4  National tax on petrol and engine fuel oils
3.38.  Colombia applies a national tax on petrol and engine fuel oils, whether imported or 
domestically produced. The tax rate is specific and charged per gallon. During the period under 
review, the rates increased by 48% (Table 3.8). The revenue collected is used to finance the Fuel 
Price Stabilization Fund (FEPC). The tax collected amounted to COP 1.83 trillion (third quarter 2024), 
equivalent to 0.11% of GDP (compared to COP 2.173 trillion in 2023, equivalent to 0.14% of GDP).67
3.39.  In addition, a consumption surtax applies to these products. In 2017, the surtax was 6% or 
25% ad valorem. Since 2021, the surtax is specific and varies according to the product and the place 
of sale68 (Table 3.8). The revenue collected is used for the maintenance of the national road network. 
With respect to both the tax and the surcharge, petrol is more heavily taxed than engine fuel oils.

3.1.4.5  Tax on single-use plastic
3.40.  Since the end of 2022, in order to reduce the production and consumption of certain single-use 
plastic products used for wrapping or packaging goods, Colombia imposes a tax on single-use 
67 Information provided by the authorities and Ministry of Finance, Central Government Fiscal Balance 
1994-2023.
68 Law No. 2093 of 2021.

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 53 -
 
plastics. This tax applies to both imported and local products and is set at a rate equivalent to 
0.00005 tax value units (UVT) per gram of the packaging or wrapping weight.69
3.1.4.6  Health-related taxes
3.41.  Since the end of 2022, Colombia applies two health-related taxes: the tax on ultra-processed 
sugary beverages (IBUA) and the tax on ultra-processed food products and/or those with high added 
sugar, sodium, or saturated fats (ICUI).70 Both taxes apply to imported and locally produced goods. 
The IBUA is a specific tax that varies according to sugar content, ranging from COP 28 per gram to 
COP 65 per gram in 2025. The ICUI is an ad valorem tax of 20%. In 2024, the IBUA affected 39 
tariff lines at the HS 10-digit level71, while the ICUI applied to 99 tariff lines at the 10-digit level.72
3.1.5  Import prohibitions, restrictions, and licensing
3.42.  Colombia imposes prohibitions or restrictions on imports (or exports) in order to protect the 
environment, public health and national security, or to comply with commitments under international 
agreements and conventions, such as the Montreal Protocol, the Minamata Convention on Mercury, 
and the Convention on Biological Diversity. Colombia currently (in 2025) prohibits the importation 
of chemical, biological and nuclear weapons as well as nuclear waste and toxic waste73; war toys74; 
asbestos and products thereof75; mercury and mercury-added products76; unusable lead-acid 
batteries and their waste and scrap materials77; exotic or non-native species declared invasive; and 
certain liquid fuels derived from petroleum or gaseous fuels.78 Weapons, narcotic drugs and 
substances, and publications that go against morality and public decency are also prohibited from 
being imported.79
3.43.  In response to the health emergency caused by the coronavirus disease (COVID-19), in 2020, 
the importation of fuel alcohol (ethanol) was restricted with the aim of eliminating the temporary 
surplus of the product caused by the drop in demand for fuels.80 These measures were later lifted.81
3.1.5.1  Import licensing
3.44.  Colombia maintains an import licensing system that includes both automatic licensing (import 
registration) and non-automatic licensing (prior licensing). The system continues to be regulated by 
Decree No. 925 of 2013, which establishes the application procedure.82 Products subject to this 
requirement are listed in circulars, which are modified when necessary.83
3.1.5.2  Automatic licensing (import registration)
3.45.  According to Colombian regulations, automatic licensing (or import registration) is required 
to import "freely importable" goods, provided that the specific requirements, permits, or 
authorizations are attained (Box 3.4).84 As notified to the WTO, the purpose of automatic licensing 
(or import registration) is not to restrict imports, but to implement trade policy. These licences are

(264 HS lines); fish and crustaceans, molluscs and other aquatic invertebrates (256 HS lines);

3.1.5.3  Non-automatic licences (prior licences)
3.46.  Non-automatic licences (prior licences) are not intended to restrict imports, but are used to 
regulate compliance with certain special requirements, as stated in Article 14 of Decree No. 925 of 
2013. These licences apply to goods entering Colombia from anywhere in the world, including 
Colombian free trade zones (Box 3.5). Applications for non-automatic licences (prior licences) are 
submitted through the VUCE in Spanish (except for names, trademarks, patents, titles or foreign 
terms that cannot be translated)87, and serve as one of the supporting documents for the import 
declaration. The non-automatic licence (prior licence) is therefore required to release the goods. 
However, if the goods arrive at the port without the required licence, it may still be processed in 
situ. If the licence is granted, the goods must be cleared within one month of their arrival, extendable 
by an additional month. Otherwise, the importer will have to choose between reshipping or 
abandoning the goods, and will therefore not be able to bring the goods into the country.88 A 
non-automatic licence costs approximately USD 9.02.89 In 2024, non-automatic licences specifically 
applied to 387 HS 10-digit tariff lines, mainly related to organic and inorganic chemicals 
(232 HS lines); arms and ammunition (69 HS lines); explosives, pyrotechnic products, and 
combustible materials (14 HS lines); and meat and edible meat offal (11 HS lines).90
3.47.  Applications for non-automatic licences (prior licences) are evaluated and approved or denied 
by the MinCIT Foreign Trade Directorate. The Register of Producers of Domestic Goods is a tool for

3.48.  To import to the Free Port of San Andrés and Providencia, "...no import registration or licence 
or any other endorsement, authorization or certification is required, except when importing alcoholic 
beverages, which must be accompanied by the relevant health certificate".96

3.1.6  Anti-dumping, countervailing, and safeguard measures
3.1.6.1  Anti-dumping measures
3.52.  Anti-dumping measures are regulated by Andean Community (CAN) legislation, which was 
not amended during the review period, and by national legislation, namely Decree No. 1750 of 2015 
and Decree No. 1794 of 2020, which was incorporated into Decree No. 1074 of 2015 (Single 
Regulatory Decree of the Trade, Industry and Tourism Sector).105 The new regulations reduced the 
time frames for procedures at the various stages of the investigation through the adoption of the 
principle of efficiency, according to the authorities (Table 3.9). In addition, certain requirements 
were eliminated, such as the need for authorization from the Committee on Trade Practices (CPC) 
to send the "essential facts" to the interested parties. Moreover, in order to streamline procedures, 
since August 2022, applications for trade defence measures have been processed via an online 
platform managed by MinCIT.106

3.53.  MinCIT, through the Foreign Trade Directorate (DCE), is the entity responsible for regulating 
the application of anti-dumping duties. Although the DCE is the investigating authority, it delegates 
the power to investigate to the Trade Practices Subdirectorate (SPC), which is also responsible for 
preparing the technical reports and issuing the preliminary and final determinations. The CPC is 
composed of various entities107 and relies on the technical reports of the SPC to make 
recommendations to the DCE, which makes the final decision regarding the type of measure to be 
applied: 
(a) 
preliminary/final 
measures; 
(b) 
extension 
of 
final 
measures; 
(c) establishment/elimination of price undertakings; or (d) termination of the investigation and/or 
final measures.
3.54.  Colombia may initiate an investigation at the request of the domestic industry108 or ex officio 
if there is sufficient evidence to determine injury, threat of injury, or material retardation caused to 
the domestic industry by dumped imports.109 Overall, although the time period for conducting the 
various stages of the investigation has been shortened, the procedure itself has not changed. 
(Chart 3.3).
Chart 3.3 Procedures for AD and SCM investigations
Source:
MinCIT – Trade Practices Committee.
3.55.  Definitive anti-dumping duties are valid for five years, subject to extension. The amount may 
be up to the full margin of dumping. The authorities have stated that anti-dumping and subsidies 
investigations may be initiated simultaneously for the same product/origin. However, no imported 
product from the same origin may be subject to both anti-dumping and countervailing duties at the 
same time.110
3.56.  Anti-dumping legislation provides for the possibility of initiating an anti-circumvention 
investigation, which must be concluded within a maximum period of four months. An investigation 
may be initiated at the request of the domestic industry or ex officio. A guarantee may be required 
for imports originating from countries under investigation from the outset of the investigation. In 
the case of circumvention, the anti-dumping duties applied may be extended to imports of like 
products or parts thereof from third countries or from the country subject to the duty.111
3.57.  Between 1 January 2018 and 30 September 2024, Colombia initiated 16 anti-dumping 
investigations, of which six ended without duties being imposed and four resulted in the application 
of definitive measures. During the same period, 16 anti-dumping measures came to an end. As at 
September 2024, Colombia had 12 definitive duties in force, primarily on products from China, three

3.58.  At the regional level, anti-dumping investigations concerning products originating in 
CAN countries are handled by the General Secretariat of the Andean Community (SGCAN).114 
Producers and governments in CAN countries must apply to the SGCAN for the initiation of an 
investigation, as the SGCAN cannot act ex officio. In the case of Colombia, the request must come 
from MinCIT.
3.1.6.2  Countervailing measures
3.59.  Countervailing measures are also regulated by CAN legislation and national legislation. At the 
national level, Decree No. 299 of 1995 was repealed by Decree No. 653 of 2022 on countervailing 
measures, which was incorporated into Decree No. 1074 of 2015 (Single Regulatory Decree of the 
Trade, Industry, and Tourism Sector). The main change introduced by the new regulations, as in the 
case of anti-dumping measures, was the reduction in time frames for the various stages of the 
investigation. In addition, new stages were introduced after the preliminary determination. 
Previously, the only procedural stages established were those relating to the oral hearing between 
the parties (Article 40) and the conclusion of the investigation (Article 41), i.e. there were no set 
procedural steps after the preliminary determination. These steps were introduced through Decree 
No. 653 of 2022, together with other provisions not included in Decree No. 299 of 1995.115 The 
procedures and various stages of the investigation are very similar to those used in anti-dumping 
investigations, except that in countervailing investigations there is, in the first instance, a 
consultation stage with the authorities of the country of origin of the imports. The amount of the 
countervailing duties may not exceed the margin of the subsidy, but must be sufficient to remove 
the injury caused.
3.60.  During the period under review, Colombia initiated two investigations. In 2019, imports of 
fuel alcohol (ethanol) (HS 2207.20) originating in the United States were investigated and a 
definitive measure was imposed, despite the fact that the legislation in force at the time contained 
no provisions for continuing the procedure after the preliminary determination.116 In 2024, another 
investigation was initiated, which is still ongoing, relating to milk powder (HS 0402.10.10; 
0402.10.90; 0402.21.11; 0402.21.19; 0402.21.91; 0402.21.99; 0402.29.11; 0402.29.19; 
0402.29.91 and 0402.29.99) originating in the United States.117 Provisional countervailing duties 
were imposed in September 2024.118
3.1.6.3  Safeguards
3.61.  The legislation regarding safeguard measures was not amended during the period under 
review. According to the legislation in force, Colombia may impose, in addition to the CAN safeguard, 
three types of measures: the WTO safeguard, the special safeguard (expedited procedure, with 
measures of shorter duration), and the bilateral safeguard (Table 3.11). As with other trade defence 
measures, the investigating authority is the MinCIT Trade Practices Subdirectorate.

3.63.  Colombia has reserved the right to use the special safeguard for agricultural products, but 
according to the authorities, it was not used during the period under review.119
3.2  Measures directly affecting exports
3.2.1  Customs procedures and requirements
3.64.  In Colombia, exporters, whether companies or natural persons, must obtain a Single Tax 
Register (RUT) number from DIAN, and register with the VUCE. Exporters may use the VUCE to 
carry out procedures related to, inter alia, the Register of Domestic Producers and registration with 
the Anti-Narcotics Police; prior export authorization or approval for products the export of which is 
restricted or controlled; and requests for the simultaneous inspection of cargo through the 
Integrated Simultaneous Inspection System (SIIS).120
3.65.  The customs procedures must be carried out by a customs agency authorized by DIAN if the 
f.o.b. value of the goods exceeds USD 30,000.121 To process shipment authorization requests, 
exporters or customs brokers must submit the export declaration and supporting documents, using 
the official forms, through MUISCA, the DIAN electronic system, or, exceptionally, as a hard copy, 
when so authorized by DIAN.122
119 WTO documents G/AG/N/COL/58, 13 June 2019, G/AG/N/COL/70, 17 May 2021, and 
G/AG/N/COL/79, 23 April 2024.
120 Information viewed at: http://www.vuce.gov.co/e_learning.php; and 
https://www.mincit.gov.co/mincomercioexterior/temas-de-interes/modulo-vuce-inspeccion-simultanea.
121 The required documents are: commercial invoice; packing list; prior export authorization or 
approval; certificate of origin (where applicable); and technical data sheet (where applicable).

3.66.  Customs examination procedures can also be undertaken through MUISCA, which uses 
technical risk analysis criteria to determine whether the merchandise can be shipped without being 
inspected (automatic examination/the green channel) or must undergo a documentary inspection 
(documentary examination/the amber channel) or a physical inspection (physical examination/the 
red channel). A customs inspection may be carried out at the request of the declarant.123 The 
majority of exports go through the green channel (Table 3.13). However, before shipping, all exports 
are scanned free of charge regardless of the assigned channel.124

3.67.  Supporting documents must be kept for five years in the event that DIAN should need to 
conduct a check.125 The supporting documents are also needed to channel foreign currency earnings 
through a foreign exchange market intermediary (IMC), in order for it to recorded with the 
Central Bank of Colombia (BRC). IMCs and clearing account126 holders must periodically provide the 
BRC with (the minimum) information on all foreign exchange operations carried out by residents and 
non-residents.
3.68.  Colombia has several export regimes, which were not changed substantially during the period 
under review.127 To promote the use of the Exporta Fácil (Easy Export) Programme, the postal traffic 
and express delivery export regime was changed. Accordingly, in 2021, the cap of USD 5,000 and 
the weight limit were eliminated, and exports coffee and made up textile articles were authorized.128 
The main objective of this change is to promote MSME exports by facilitating procedures and 
leveraging the infrastructure of the official postal operator.129 However, during 2017-19 the use of 
the Programme decreased, in terms of both the volume and value of exports.130 In order to 
streamline the export process, it is also necessary to integrate the DIAN platforms and the postal 
information system.
3.69.  During the review period, Colombia took other steps to facilitate export procedures and thus 
promote the participation of MSMEs. Initiatives such as the Canguro Exportador (Exporter 
Jump-start) Plan, a customs business initiative and centres of excellence. The Canguro Exportador 
Plan provides users with support with regard to export procedures and other customs processes.131 
The customs business initiative helps users to carry out customs operations132; while the centres of

excellence, a public-private partnership, serve the entire production chain.133 Moreover, to facilitate 
exports, Colombia continues to use a simultaneous inspection policy, implemented by means of the 
SIIS module on the VUCE, for exports of perishable products. Exporters can use the SIIS to request 
the simultaneous inspection of the merchandise by the control authorities (i.e. DIAN, the 
Anti-Narcotics Police, ICA, INVIMA, and port and airport terminals).134
3.70.  Colombia continues to grant AEO certification to exporters.135 Article 63 of Decree No. 1165 
of 2019 also refers to Usuarios Altamente Exportadores (high-value exporters) (ALTEX), who enjoy 
the following prerogatives: they may apply for a blanket authorization to ship partial loads; physical 
customs inspections will only be carried out at random; customs inspections of merchandise for 
export, when necessary, will be performed at ALTEX facilities; and they may post a general bond to 
cover all their transactions. Originally, the ALTEX status was valid of four years, until May 2020.136 
However, because of the health emergency, it was extended to 30 June 2022, when the end of the 
health emergency was decreed.137
3.2.2  Taxes, charges, and levies
3.71.  Colombia continues to impose a "coffee contribution", a specific tax that coffee producers 
must pay to the National Coffee Fund when exporting coffee.138 According to law, the contribution 
rate depends on the international price of mild coffee (Arabica), ranging between USD 0.02/lb and 
USD 0.06/lb.139 In addition, since 2003, the coffee contribution for processed coffee has been: 
(a) USD 1.08/lb for roasted coffee; (b) USD 0.48/lb for soluble coffee; and (c) USD 0.36/lb for 
coffee extract.140
3.72.  In 2019, Colombia established a Coffee Price Stabilization Fund (FEPCafé) to stabilize 
producers' incomes and protect them from climatic and market risks.141 The National Coffee Fund is 
one of the sources of financing for FEPCafé, contributing USD 0.005 per pound of coffee exported.142 
The maximum volume of coffee covered by FEPCafémay not exceed 70% of the productive capacity 
of the coffee farmer. In addition, the Coffee Farmer Income Compensation Stabilization Mechanism 
(MECIC) was created to provide relief for temporary losses when the domestic selling price falls 
below the average cost of production. Since 2024, the downward trend in Colombia's domestic price 
has been reversed, owing to changes in the international coffee market. MECIC was therefore not 
activated.143
3.73.  Exports of unset emeralds are subject to a 1% parafiscal contribution on the exported value 
in foreign currency. This contribution, administered by the National Emeralds Fund, is used to 
support the emerald industry at all stages, including international market promotion and social 
development in areas such as education and health.144
3.2.3  Export prohibitions, restrictions, and licensing
3.74.  Colombia maintains certain export restrictions or controls under Article XX of the GATT. 
Exports of coal, precious metals, precious and semi-precious stones, and mineral products require 
prior controls. Prior controls are also required for the export of some fishery products; products of 
wild fauna and flora not listed in the Appendices to the Convention on International Trade in 
Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora (CITES); ozone-depleting substances for export; and

narcotics and chemical substances and products. The export of goods of national cultural interest is 
prohibited.145 The relevant permits, approvals and certifications are granted by, among others, the 
National Mining Agency (precious stones, emeralds, and precious metals), the Ministry of the 
Environment (wild fauna and flora), the Department of the Environment (timber and seeds), the 
Ministry of Culture (art, culture and archaeology), the National Narcotics Fund and the National 
Aquaculture and Fisheries Authority(AUNAP). All these entities operate through the VUCE.146
3.75.  In general, Colombia maintains export quotas within the framework of certain trade treaties 
and in order to meet its commitments under international treaties.147 Quotas for agricultural products 
are administered by the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development and are requested through 
the VUCE.148 Colombia also has a quota for ferrous scrap exports, given the shortage of this raw 
material in the steel industry.149 In 2022, the Committee on Customs, Tariff and Foreign Trade 
Matters recommended establishing a quota of 61,012 tonnes, which will remain in force until 
May 2025, although the measure is reviewed annually.150
3.76.  In 2020, during the pandemic, Colombia imposed a six-month ban on the export of certain 
goods needed to address the health crisis.151 In addition, exports of products containing added 
mercury have been prohibited since 2021, to comply with commitments under the 
Minamata Convention152, as well as exports of asbestos and products made with asbestos, in order 
to protect public health.153
3.2.4  Export support and promotion
3.77.  During the period 2018-24, Colombia notified that no export subsidies had been provided for 
agricultural products in 2018154, 2019155 and 2020.156 In 2019, Colombia submitted its most recent 
notification concerning total exports (Table ES:2) in the context of export subsidy commitments 
for 2018.157
3.78.  Exports of goods or services are exempt from VAT.158
3.79.  Under the Special Export Programme (PEX), pursuant to a trade agreement, a person resident 
abroad may purchase raw materials, inputs, intermediate goods, packaging material or containers 
produced in Colombia and deliver them to a producer residing in the Colombian customs territory in 
order to be manufactured into a final good that must be exported in accordance with the instructions 
received from the foreign buyer.159 Industrial users in free trade zones may register with PEX but 
only as producers of final goods.160

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 65 -
 
3.80.  Colombia continues to use the Special Import-Export Systems (SEIEX) (Vallejo Plan), 
amended in 2020 to simplify procedures and specify the duration of the stages of the programmes.161 
The programmes are subject to MinCIT approval. The Vallejo Plan comprises three programmes 
– the raw materials and inputs programme, the capital goods and spare parts programme, and the 
services export programme – which may in turn have subprogrammes.162 Under these programmes, 
raw materials, inputs, capital goods and spare parts can be temporarily imported, usually with tariff 
exemptions and/or with a VAT deferral or exemption, provided that they are used to produce 
value-added goods for export. In the case of services, the imported goods must be directly linked 
to the provision of export services. Goods imported under these programmes do not require an 
import licence; however, they do require the necessary permits and authorizations.163 Any type of 
good may be produced under these programmes, except for one of the subprogrammes related to 
the import of capital goods, which will be approved only if the final goods for export are agricultural 
products as defined by the WTO (HS Chapters 1-24).164
3.81.  During the period under review, the most used SEIEX programmes were the raw materials 
and inputs programme, followed by the capital goods programme. In 2022 and 2023, the number 
of authorized programmes rose considerably, reaching 66 programmes, compared to 19 in 2019 and 
24 in 2024. The 2022-23 increase was due to the "Plan Vallejo Exprés"165, a tool for economic 
recovery, created in response to the drop in exports caused by the COVID-19 pandemic.166 The 
SEIEX programmes were primarily used by medium-sized and large enterprises.167
3.82.  The Special Economic Export Zones (ZEEEs) are geographically delimited areas created to 
promote investment and exports of goods and services from border municipalities.168 In this regard, 
companies that operate within ZEEEs and meet the investment requirements (75,000 tax value units 
(UVTs)) and export requirements (at least 80% of their sales), benefit from tax incentives and other 
advantages, such as access to financing, logistical support and training services.169 With respect to 
tax incentives, companies are exempt from income tax on the proportional share of income 
generated by exports, and from tariffs and other customs duties, as are industrial users in free trade 
zones. This regime will be valid for 50 years; however, to date, no companies are operating as 
ZEEEs.170
3.83.  International Trading Companies (CIs) export products purchased on the domestic market or 
manufactured by producers that are partners of the CI.171 However, they may also import goods or 
inputs, either to supply the domestic market or manufacture exportable products.172 Merchandise 
and raw materials acquired in Colombia or imported by CIs may be exempt from VAT and income 
tax withholding, provided they are exported within six months of the issuance of the certificate to 
the supplier.173 Services used for the production of an exportable good are also exempt from VAT. 
In addition, CIs may access other export promotion programmes, such as the Vallejo Plan and Tax 
Reimbursement Certificates (CERTs), although they cannot be established in a free trade zone.174

3.84.  From 2018 to 2020, the number of CIs registered by the MinCIT remained relatively stable at 
around 17. From 2021, 52 companies, mainly micro- and small enterprises in sectors such as 
agro-industrial (43%), mining (34%) and manufacturing (18%) sectors, were authorized 
annually.175
3.85.  Colombia has regulations in force that allow CERTs176 to be used as an instrument to support 
exports, although the instrument is not currently operational.177 Exporters could, by means of CERTs, 
obtain a full or partial refund of indirect taxes. In principle, the BRC fixes the percentage of the 
refund annually. However, during the period under review, this instrument was not used.
3.86.  ProColombia, as in the previous Review, is responsible for promoting exports (other than 
mining and energy exports), as well as foreign investment, tourism and the country's image. To 
achieve the goals outlined in the National Development Plans, i.e. to diversify the exportable supply 
to include goods from the non-mining and non-energy sectors and to promote the integration of 
Colombian companies into regional and global value chains, ProColombia provides the following 
services: (a) it identifies market opportunities and designs marketing/ integration strategies for 
Colombian products; (b) it designs internationalization strategies for companies; (c) it organizes 
activities to foster trade, investment and tourism; (d) it promotes the use of the country brand, 
"Marca País Colombia"; and (e) it supports foreign investors when investing in and/or purchasing 
goods and services.178
3.87.  During the period under review (2018-24), ProColombia supported 8,452 companies, of which 
84% were MSMEs with exports valued at USD 33,432 million and sent to 183 markets.179 The main 
exports were textiles and made up articles, plastics and rubber, chemicals, agro-industrial products 
and building materials, primarily to Brazil, Canada, Chile, Ecuador, Mexico, Peru and the 
United States.180
3.88.  The MinCIT has other support programmes for exporters, such as Fábricas de 
Internacionalización (Internationalization Factories) and "Ella Exporta a África" ("She Exports to 
Africa"). The Internationalization Factories seek to boost non-mining and non-energy exports181, 
promote an export culture and expand the export business network. The programme provides 
specialized advice on: (a) the design of new business models; (b) foreign trade consultancy; and 
(c) the promotion of strategic partnerships among companies to reduce costs and risks in 
internalization processes.
3.89.  On the other hand, the "Ella Exporta a África" programme, developed and funded by the 
Fondo Mujer Libre y Productiva (Free and Productive Woman Fund), and implemented in partnership 
with ProColombia, focuses on women's businesses with a view to accelerating their 
internationalization process in Africa. This includes: (a)technical assistance channelled through the 
Fábricas de Internacionalización; (b) strengthening internationalization, by providing productive 
assets and/or financing for specialized consultancies or services; and (c) scoping missions in 
Africa.182 In 2024, 30 companies were selected as beneficiaries of the programme. In 2025, the new 
call for the second cycle of the programme prioritizes businesses in the agri-food, metallurgical, 
chemical, fashion and Industry 4.0 production chains.183

3.2.5  Export finance, insurance, and guarantees
3.90.  Bancóldex, a state-owned bank, acts as the business development bank of Colombia and is 
part of the Grupo Bicentenario184, supervised by the Financial Supervisory Authority of Colombia. 
Bancóldex operates directly as a second-tier bank through pre-qualified banks. It offers financing 
for foreign trade operations through: lines of credit in US dollars to finance exports and working 
capital, medium-and long-term leasing to purchase machinery, foreign exchange hedging services, 
and a document discount instrument. In addition, Bancóldex provides technical assistance for the 
internationalization of Colombian enterprises, the development of the popular economy, the early 
stages of entrepreneurship, technological transformation and the development of strategic 
sectors.185
3.91.  The Bancóldex Strategic Plan 2024-26 ratified its role as a business development bank and a 
public policy instrument to support sustainable development, by helping to improve the productivity 
of companies, particularly MSMEs, and by promoting their internationalization.186 One of the Plan's 
financial instruments that sought to promote companies' financial inclusion and internationalization 
was the Internationalization for Productivity 2024 line of credit, in the amount of COP 119 billion, 
which benefited 220 MSME exporters. In addition, the Colombian Foreign Trade Trust Fund 
(Fiducoldex), a state agency the main shareholder in which is Bancóldex, and which is also part of 
the Grupo Bicentenario, provides financial instruments to support the work of Bancóldex.187
3.92.  Furthermore, 
the

Agrarian 
Bank 
(BAC), 
which 
is 
also 
part 
of 
the 
Grupo Bicentenario, supports agricultural exporters, especially rural families, through instruments 
such as pre-financing, letters of credit and documentary collection, with preferential rates and 
conditions.188
3.93.  Colombia has no official export insurance programmes.
3.3  Measures affecting production and trade
3.3.1  Incentives
3.94.  As in 2017, Colombia continues to implement a series of incentive programmes, some of 
which have been notified to the WTO. Colombia's last full notification on subsidies dates back to 
2020 and includes fisheries and aquaculture subsidies that were in force in 2016, 2017 and 2018.189 
Colombia notified support programmes administered by the National Aquaculture and Fisheries 
Authority (AUNAP) under the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development, which is the authority 
responsible for the sustainable development of fisheries resources (Section 4).
3.95.  During the period under review, Colombia notified its domestic support commitments in 2018, 
2019 and 2020.190 The programmes aimed, inter alia, to promote the development of the agricultural 
sector, through research, pest and disease control, and training, extension and marketing services. 
Among its development programmes, Colombia notified programmes pertaining to regional 
assistance, investment subsidies to enhance productivity, agricultural input subsidies for low-income

producers and support for diversification from illicit crops. It also notified its support for specific 
products such as cotton, rice, milk, maize and cassava (Section 4.1).
3.96.  During the period 2018-24, Colombia notified that it had not provided any food aid in 2018191, 
2019192 and 2020.193
3.97.  In general, incentive programmes aim to promote investment (domestic and foreign), 
employment, re-industrialization, regional development, competitiveness and exports. In addition 
to the sectoral programmes, a series of incentives and horizontal programmes have been developed 
to stimulate technological innovation, promote the development of MSMEs and enhance the inclusion 
of women in the economy (Section 2). Colombia also offers incentives based on location (special 
regimes) and establishment in free zones.194 Its sectoral programmes, other than those notified, 
include incentives for energy production from non-conventional sources195; incentives for automotive 
production196; the shipbuilding industry promotion programme197; incentives for investment in 
tourism198; and incentives promoting the creative economy, cultural expressions and public 
entertainment (income tax deduction of up to 165% of the amount invested in or donated to creative 
economy projects) (Sections 2 and 4).199 Most of these programmes offer tax incentives and tariff 
exemptions; thus, tax revenues from tariffs averaged 0.4% of GDP during the period 2017-24.
3.98.  Colombia continues to use price stabilization funds to guarantee producers' income and 
protect consumers. The most significant of these funds are the Fuel Price Stabilization Fund (FEPC) 
and the various price stabilization funds for agricultural products (Section 4).
3.3.1.1  Free zones
3.99.  There are three types of free zone regime: (a) industrial parks (permanent free zones), in 
which several companies operate in the same physical space; (b) a single company located anywhere 
in the country (special permanent free zones); and (c) national or international fairs, exhibitions, 
congresses and seminars of importance for the economy and/or international trade (temporary free 
zones). Free zones, both permanent and special, may be dedicated to the production of goods and/or 
services or commercial activities. Special permanent free zones for services have existed 
since 2021.200 Offshore permanent free zones may also be established for the exploration and 
production of hydrocarbons.
3.100.  Tax incentives, with the exception of the incentive related to income tax, were not 
substantially modified during the period under review (Table 3.14). Raw materials, parts, inputs and 
finished goods that free zone users purchase in the national territory and in other free zones are 
exempt from VAT, while imported goods are exempt from duty and VAT. Under the free zone regime, 
there is no requirement to export; however, the 2022 tax reform incentivized export operations by 
determining income tax for free zone industrial users. Therefore, for industrial users authorized or 
recognized as of 13 December 2022, who adhere to an Internationalization and Annual Sales Plan, 
income from goods and services exported from free zones is taxed at a preferential rate of 20%, 
while the rest of their income is taxed at the general income tax rate (30%, as of 2022).201 However, 
for users to benefit from this incentive, MinCIT must approve their Internationalization and Sales 
191 WTO document G/AG/N/COL/65, 2 October 2019.

3.101.  During the period under review, a number of regulations relating to the regime were updated, 
taking good practices into account, in order to streamline procedures and attract investment in 
Economy 4.0 (orange economy) projects and in the export of services.203 At the institutional level, 
the Technical Committee on Free Zones, a public-private entity under the National Competitiveness 
and Innovation System, was established to increase the zones' competitiveness and maintain cross-
sectoral dialogue. As from 2021, e-commerce transactions have been authorized from free zones 
(existing and new) to the rest of the national territory204, and there have been state concessions to 
develop airport and railway infrastructure.205 Other steps taken include: (a) simplifying the creation 
and extension procedure; (b) reducing investment and job creation commitments for new free zones 
located in municipalities with high poverty rates206; (c) allowing users to utilize the intangible assets 
they generate as new investment commitments; and (d) providing approval for 50% of employees 
to work remotely outside a special permanent free zone for services.207 The minimum investment 
and job creation requirements to establish a free zone were updated during the period under review 
(Table 3.15).

3.102.  Permanent and special free zones can be maintained for an initial period of 30 years, 
extendable for up to 30 additional years.208 Since 2019, extensions for permanent free zones have 
been granted provided there is an investment commitment, and a specific percentage of the 
investment must be used for scientific, technological and innovation activities. In addition to the 
investment requirement, special permanent free zones must also create direct and indirect jobs.209 
The investment and job creation requirements for special zones vary depending on the type of 
activity (goods, services or agri-business) and locality. Free zones dedicated exclusively to the dairy 
sector and those located in the departments of Putumayo, Nariño, Huila, Caquetá and Cauca and in 
the Cúcuta Metropolitan Area in the Norte de Santander department were eliminated in 2021.210
3.103.  The free zone regime is an important tool for attracting investment in high-value projects 
and promoting employment and exports. In 2024, Colombia had 115 free zones, of which 74 were 
special zones, which generated 182,000 jobs and a total of USD 3,128 million in exports.211
3.3.1.2  MSMEs
3.104.  CONPES document No. 4011 of 2020 and the National Development Plan 2022-26 recognize 
the importance of MSMEs in achieving socio-productive development in the country and the need to 
adopt policies that foster their growth.212 Thus, during the period under review, formalities were 
reduced in order to facilitate the establishment of MSMEs, and the parameters defining them were 
modified, thereby expanding the number of enterprises that could be considered as such. In addition, 
the National Administrative Department of Statistics (DANE) implemented a statistical tool, the 
Informal Economic Activity Information System (SIECI), to identify and formalize informal economic 
units. In terms of financing, insurance for MSMEs was introduced to protect investments funded by 
state resources, and MSME participation in government procurement is promoted through 
preferences.213 Furthermore, incentive programmes that focus mainly on MSMEs have been 
implemented (Table 3.16).

3.105.  MSMEs can operate in any sector of the economy and are classified based on their income, 
according to their activity, i.e. manufacturing, services or trade.214 In the agricultural sector, the 
Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development and iNNpulsa Colombia are working together to 
support small producers.215 MSMEs in the agricultural sector whose production initiatives focus on 
food security and that implement sustainable production practices shall access accelerator 
programmes under special conditions for their promotion and development and may use an 
identifying mark to accompany their products' brand.216 Furthermore, business ventures in which 
rural women make up the majority of the individuals involved have priority access to the 
programmes and will have access to non-repayable resources from special funds aimed at promoting 
and strengthening such businesses.217
3.3.2  Standards and other technical requirements
3.106.  MinCIT, through the Regulation Directorate, remains responsible for standardization, quality, 
certification, accreditation and metrology policies. Colombia notified the WTO that MinCIT is its 
national enquiry point under the Agreement on Technical Barriers to Trade (TBT).218 The National 
Quality Subsystem (SICAL/SNCA) is responsible for standardization, technical regulations, 
accreditation, conformity assessment, metrology and monitoring.219 SICAL comprises the public and 
private institutions that are part of Colombia's quality infrastructure (Table 3.17). MinCIT's 
Intersectoral Quality Commission (CIC)220 coordinates the entities that make up SICAL and is

3.108.  In the period 2018-25 (January), Colombia notified 155 measures, some of which are 
CAN technical regulations. Seven specific trade concerns were submitted in relation to the measures 
adopted by Colombia.222
3.3.2.1  Standardization
3.109.  The Colombian Technical Standards and Certification Institute (ICONTEC), a private 
non-profit organization, is Colombia's national standardization body.223 One of the Institute's 
objectives is to promote quality as a comprehensive concept, with a view to enhancing the 
competitiveness of domestic industry and economic development. ICONTEC develops Colombian 
Technical Standards (NTC) and Sectoral Technical Standards (NTS), both of which are voluntary. 
Sectoral Technical Standards are specific to a sector (such as hydrocarbons and education), are 
221 Decree No. 3257 of 2008.
222 The concerns relate to: conformity assessment (braking systems and their components and the 
technical regulation applicable to pneumatic tyres for motorcycles); certification of good practices (imported 
wines and beverages); non-notified technical regulations; the labelling of mobile devices; the technical 
regulation on rational energy-use labelling for electrical and gas end-use equipment; harmonization with 
international standards (sodium content in food); and the technical regulation on zinc-carbon and alkaline cells 
and batteries (WTO. Enquiry point – ePing SPS&TBT platform. Viewed at: Buscar notificaciones - ePing 
SPS&TBT platform and Buscar preocupaciones comerciales - ePing SPS&TBT platform).
223 ICONTEC represents Colombia in international and regional standardization bodies, such as the 
International Organization for Standardization (ISO), the Pan American Standards Commission (COPANT), the 
International Certification Network (IQNET) and the International Electrotechnical Commission (IEC).

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 73 -
 
developed by Sectoral Standardization Units (USN) in scientific and industrial metrology, and must 
be approved by MinCIT.224
3.110.  The process for developing and publishing Colombian Technical Standards consists of the 
following steps: (a) identification of the need; (b) assessment of the need; (c) incorporation in the 
standardization programme; (d) drafting and review of the preliminary draft by interested parties; 
(e) approval of the preliminary draft (Technical Committee); (f) public consultation and processing 
of comments; (g) final editing; (h) approval; (i) publication; and (j) implementation.225 
Colombian Technical Standards and Sectoral Technical Standards are reviewed at least every 
10 years to ensure their applicability and relevance.
3.111.  ICONTEC has an information centre (e-collection platform), accessible through a 
subscription, which contains national standards and the international standards developed by ISO 
and IEC.226
3.3.2.2  Technical regulations
3.112.  Technical regulations are mandatory. According to the authorities, as a general rule, 
technical regulations are based on regional or international good practices. Most technical regulations 
are based on ICONTEC and international standards. The development of technical regulations in 
Colombia is not centralized. Public bodies draft the technical regulations within their areas of 
competence. Each public body has internal procedures; however, MinCIT regulates the procedures 
and verifies that draft technical regulations comply with the rules and do not constitute unnecessary 
technical barriers to trade. Draft technical regulations are subject to discussion among all interested 
entities and persons throughout the drafting process. Technical regulations are approved by means 
of a legal instrument.227 SIC monitors and controls the requirements stipulated in the technical 
regulations to ensure that they are met at the time of marketing. Producers and importers of goods 
subject to technical regulations must be registered in MinCIT's Register of Producers and Importers 
before importing or marketing the product.
3.113.  Since 2018, the main changes made to the technical regulation drafting process have been 
the inclusion of good practices, such as the regulatory impact analysis (RIA), and the introduction 
of specific periods for national public consultation (15 days) and international notification 
(60 days).228 There are various types of RIA. The type applied depends on whether the text is a new 
technical regulation or an amendment, as well as on the difficulty and cost of complying with the 
technical regulation.229 Currently, the steps involved in drafting a technical regulation are: 
(a) definition of motive; (b) RIA conducted by the regulatory entity230; (c) preparation of the 
preliminary draft; (d) national public consultation; (e) preliminary opinion from MinCIT; 
(f) international notification; (g) issuance of the technical regulation; (h) signing and publication; 
and (i) entry into force.231
224 The Sectoral Standardization Units are comprised of accredited public entities, as well as 
associations, universities, trade unions and private non-profit organizations.

3.114.  Technical regulations remain subject to an automatic mechanism for reviewing their expiry, 
by way of a review by the regulatory entity, every five years, or before if necessary, to determine 
whether they should be modified, maintained or repealed.232
3.3.2.3  Conformity assessment, accreditation, and certification
3.115.  The National Accreditation Organization of Colombia (ONAC), a private entity, is responsible 
for assessing the conformity of products, whether domestic or imported, that are subject to technical 
regulations. The mechanisms and methods to assess the conformity of domestic and imported 
products with Colombian technical regulations are set out in the regulations themselves and, 
depending on the level of risk, may involve certification, inspection and/or testing carried out by 
conformity assessment bodies accredited or designated by ONAC.233 Colombia accepts certification 
issued by foreign accredited bodies provided that ONAC has mutual recognition agreements with 
them or approval procedures.234
3.116.  During the period under review, the number of conformity assessment bodies accredited by 
ONAC increased considerably, rising from 1,174 in 2017 to 1,965 in 2023, mainly in automotive 
diagnostic centres and driver recognition centres.235 Accredited bodies are subject to an annual 
evaluation and their accreditation is renewed for three years and, subsequently, five years, provided 
the results are satisfactory.236
3.117.  In 2019, ONAC developed a Comprehensive Tariff Model (MIT) to create a cost-time structure 
in accordance with the requirements of each accreditation type, for all accredited conformity 
assessment bodies.237 The authorities have indicated that the MIT is aiming for transparency to 
protect users.
3.118.  Colombia has other entities authorized to accredit products and services within their area of 
competence, such as the National Food and Drug Surveillance Institute (INVIMA); the 
Colombian Agricultural Institute (ICA); the National Institute of Forensic Medicine and Forensic 
Sciences (INMLCF); and the Institute of Hydrology, Meteorology and Environmental Studies 
(IDEAM).
3.119.  ICONTEC grants voluntary certification for imported and local products by means of the 
ICONTEC quality seal, which is accredited by ONAC. The seal is valid for three or six years depending 
on the scheme. To grant certification, ICONTEC conducts an audit of production control, assesses 
the manufacturer's quality management system, and runs laboratory tests on the product based on 
criteria set out in the voluntary standards. If the product meets the requirements, ICONTEC grants 
the certificate and the manufacturer may mark the certified product with a logo. Follow-up audits 
are subsequently carried out.238
3.3.2.4  Metrology
3.120.  In terms of metrology, during the period under review, efforts were made to strengthen the 
National Institute of Metrology (INM), which is responsible for scientific and industrial metrology in 
Colombia, by expanding its powers so that it can improve its measuring capacity. In 2019, a Sectoral 
Standardization Unit (USM) was established to draft and update metrology-related technical 
regulations.239 In addition, steps have been taken to try to integrate the INM's work into ONAC in

terms of laboratory accreditation, and into the entities under the Sanitary and Phytosanitary 
Measures System, by conducting trials and analytical tests to promote agricultural exports.240
3.121.  The Supervisory Authority for Industry and Trade (SIC) is responsible for legal metrology, 
although the INM has been the custodian of national measurement standards since 2021. SIC 
inspects, monitors and controls measuring instruments to ensure that they meet the requirements 
specified in the technical regulations in order to protect market agents, particularly consumers, 
thereby favouring domestic and international trade and preventing possible fraud. SIC performs 
metrological verifications by means of the Authorized Metrological Verification Bodies (OAVM), which 
must be accredited by ONAC.241
3.3.3  Sanitary and phytosanitary requirements
3.122.  The sanitary and phytosanitary (SPS) system of Colombia is regulated primarily by Law 
No. 101 of 1993 and Decrees No. 4765 of 2008 and No. 1071 of 2015, and amendments thereto. 
During the period under review, new legislation was adopted, including Resolution No. 00008071 
of 2017, establishing a risk profiling system for the inspection of imported products, Decree No. 931 
of 2018, creating the plant traceability system, and Decree No. 1517 of 2021, expanding the 
functions undertaken by the Intersectoral Commission on Sanitary and Phytosanitary Measures 
(CIMSF).
3.123.  No substantial changes were made during the period under review to the entities responsible 
for formulating SPS policy in Colombia and for protecting plant, animal and human health. The main 
bodies that develop SPS measures continue to be the Colombian Agricultural Institute (ICA), the 
National Food and Drug Surveillance Institute (INVIMA) and the National Institute of Health (INS). 
The CIMSF is the body that coordinates these entities.242 In 2021, in order to avoid the duplication 
of functions and to simplify administrative management, the CIMSF took on the functions of other 
commissions with services related to health and safety. It also coordinates SPS policy related to the 
meat sector.243
3.124.  The ICA remains responsible for issuing SPS measures to prevent SPS risks. The Institute 
establishes phytosanitary and animal health quarantine measures, and regulates genetically 
modified organisms (GMOs). In order to perform its tasks, the ICA has its own animal and plant 
diagnostic laboratories, and also accredits public and private laboratories. The ICA carries out 
technical controls on imported and locally produced products of animal and plant origin to prevent 
diseases and pests. It also certifies the SPS quality of exported products.244 Importers and exporters 
must register with the ICA.
3.125.  The INVIMA, attached to the Ministry of Health, is responsible for ensuring food safety and 
the quality of products that may affect health.245 The majority of standards and technical regulations 
implemented by INVIMA are based on international standards. The health inspections, surveillance 
and controls carried out by INVIMA are based on risk management.246
3.126.  The Regulation Directorate of MinCIT is the authority responsible for submitting notifications 
to the WTO Committee on Sanitary and Phytosanitary Measures and is also the designated enquiry 
point.247 Between 2018 and January 2025, Colombia submitted 191 notifications (including

(c) the inspection and interception record.252 The volume of imports processed through SISPAP 
increased considerably, since the quantity of rejected products is marginal (Table 3.19).253

3.128.  Moreover, in support of its strategy for the internationalization and diversification of 
non-energy exports and the promotion of agricultural exports, in 2018 the Ministry of Agriculture 
and Rural Development (MADR) implemented the plant traceability system, administered by the ICA. 
This system must be used for all edible plants and products for human consumption that are 
produced, processed, transported, distributed or marketed on the national or international 
market.254 One of the system's objectives, in addition to promoting the development of the sector, 
is to meet the requirements to export to certain markets.255 Exported products included in the 
system are mainly fresh plants (fruits, herbs and vegetables).256
3.129.  In Colombia, imported animal and plant products and agricultural inputs require the following 
ICA permits: (a) a phytosanitary requirements import document (DRFI); (b) an animal health import 
document; (c) a sale licence or registration (LV); (d) an inputs report (CI); and (e) an authorization 
certificate for agricultural inputs and livestock inputs (CA).257 Moreover, the ICA issues so-called 
"importation prohibited for use as an agricultural input" certificates for substances such as

pentachlorobenzene, hexabromobiphenyl and their derivatives (HS 2903.93.00; 2903.94.00; 
2903.99.10; 2903.99.20; 2903.99.30; 2903.99.90), which cannot be used as an agricultural input.
3.130.  In Colombia, technical regulations or SPS measures are based on international standards 
and guidelines, Andean Community standards, and good regulatory practices, and the economic 
effect of imposing them or not is also taken into consideration.258 The ICA began to assess the 
economic effects of regulations in 2023, for cases such as vaccination against foot-and-mouth 
disease, avian influenza and bovine brucellosis. Draft technical regulations or SPS measures are sent 
for national public consultation prior to their approval and publication. They are also notified at the 
international level (90- or 60-day comment periods). In the event of an emergency, once the 
emergency situation is over, the technical regulations or SPS measures must be repealed within 
12 months, with the possibility of a six-month extension.259
3.3.4  Competition policy and price controls
3.3.4.1  Competition policy
3.131.  In Colombia, the Political Constitution guarantees free economic competition.260 The 
competition regime is, generally speaking, regulated by Law No. 155 of 1959, establishing provisions 
on restrictive business practices261; Law No. 1340 of 2009, establishing competition protection rules; 
Decree No. 2153 of 1992, defining, inter alia, anti-competitive practices; and Single Regulatory 
Decree (DUR) of the Trade, Industry and Tourism Sector (Decree No. 1074 of 2015).262 There are 
also sectoral regulations governing competition in the telecommunications, financial and insurance 
services, healthcare, port services and public utilities markets.263 In the event of a conflict between 
sectoral regulations and generally applicable rules, the sectoral regulations shall prevail.264 Thus, all 
sectors of the economy and all public and private operators, both domestic and foreign, operating 
within or outside the Colombian territory, are subject to the laws regulating competition, provided 
that their practices affect fully or partially competition in Colombian markets.265
3.132.  While the Political Constitution prohibits public and private monopolies, it does allow the 
State to establish, by law and on grounds of public/social interest, and after compensating the 
individuals concerned, financial monopolies subject to efficiency requirements.266 In 2024, two 
state-owned financial monopolies (the production of distilled spirits, and games of luck and chance) 
operated in Colombia. In addition, only the State may manufacture, sell, import or export arms, 
ammunition and explosives.267 Sectors/activities in which the State has a financial monopoly or 
exclusive rights are subject to the competition regime.
3.133.  As a national competition authority, the Supervisory Authority for Industry and Trade (SIC), 
attached to MinCIT, oversees all sectors of the economy.268 However, in the financial sector, the 
Colombian Financial Supervisory Authority (SFC) reviews and approves economic integrations, and 
in the aeronautical sector, Aerocivil, the civil aviation authority, approves joint operations to provide 
air services, such as code-share agreements.269 In the telecommunications sector, the 
Communications Regulatory Commission (CRC) defines the relevant markets, makes findings of 
abuses of dominant position, and issues relevant regulatory measures.270
