3.134.  Generally speaking, practices that restrict or may restrict competition in markets are 
prohibited and sanctioned (general prohibition), such as: (a) anti-competitive agreements between 
two or more operators271; (b) unilateral anti-competitive actions272; and (c) abuses of dominant 
position.273 However, practices that help to improve economic efficiency and promote R&D or to 
exploit an intellectual property right, for example, are permitted, even though they may affect free 
competition.274
3.135.  The SIC also allows agreements or arrangements to be signed between two or more 
competitors that, while restricting free competition, are intended to uphold the stability of core 
sectors of interest to the economy275, when market conditions so require.276 These agreements or 
arrangements must be notified to and approved by the SIC, which also monitors their 
implementation. In the case of the agricultural sector, approval requires the prior, binding and 
reasoned opinion of the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development.277 Since 2018, no such 
agreements or arrangements have been signed.278
3.136.  The SIC investigates alleged anti-competitive practices, either ex officio or at the request of 
a party.279 The investigation procedure has not changed since 2018.280 In determining the existence 
of an anti-competitive practice, Colombian legislation does not provide for the use of the per se rule 
or the rule of reason, although the SIC does use them in practice.281 In 2022, the collaboration 
benefits programme was amended to cap the number of participants at six (previously it was 
unlimited); to encourage participation in the programme before an investigation is initiated (in order 
to obtain greater reductions); and to fix the reductions offered at between 15% and 100% 
(previously it was 25-100%).282 During the period under review, three applications for the 
programme were submitted (in 2018, 2019 and 2021).283 Involvement in the programme does not 
exempt participants from criminal liability; however, if there is found to have been collusion in 
government procurement processes, the sentence and period of disqualification from participating 
in government procurement processes are reduced.284
3.137.  Between 2018 and 2024, the SIC initiated 74 investigations into possible anti-competitive 
agreements/actions and two into potential abuses of dominant position.285 The total fines amounted 
to COP 695 billion; these were imposed mainly on the grounds of anti-competitive 
agreements/actions. Of those grounds, collusion in government procurement processes was the 
leading violation (Table 3.20).

3.138.  Sanctions imposed by the SIC for violations of the free competition regime may be appealed. 
The amount of the fine depends on whether the offender is a legal or natural person and on the 
impact of the practice.286
3.139.  All economic integrations (mergers, consolidations, and acquisitions) carried out in Colombia 
and abroad (if they affect competition in Colombia) must be notified (online), verified and approved 
ex ante, provided they exceed the threshold set by the SIC.287 However, economic integrations 
involving operators from the same business group do not have to be notified288; and for economic 
integrations where the parties holds less than 20% of the relevant market share, verification is not 
required (upon receipt of the notification, the SIC issues an acknowledgement of receipt which 
denotes automatic approval).289 On the other hand, economic integrations that infringe competition 
law may be approved, with or without conditions, if they help to improve economic efficiency.290 
In 2021, the notification, verification and approval procedure was modified, which, according to the 
authorities, has allowed for greater efficiency.291 The main change was to give the SIC more time to 
analyse additional information. Economic integrations are notified online.292 Since 2018, three 
economic integrations in the beer, telecommunications and chemical industry markets have been 
rejected.
3.140.  The PND 2018-22 also notes that some anti-competitive practices "persist over time", it 
therefore remains critical to promote good practices to foster competition.293 In this regard, the SIC 
publishes market studies and guides.294 It also makes recommendations on draft regulations that 
may affect free competition. Recommendations are issued ex officio (since 2020) or at the request 
of the regulator.295 Since 2018, the SIC has issued some 410 recommendations (27 ex officio); the 
main sectors analysed were mines and energy, telecommunications, and land transport. Colombia 
has a system for disseminating information related to competition to the public, known as the 
Competition Information System (SICOMP).
3.3.4.2  Price controls
3.141.  Colombia continues to use a pricing policy based on three regimes: (a) direct price controls, 
which consists of setting the maximum selling price; (b) regulated freedom, which consists of 
determining the methodology and variables for setting prices (prices increase or decrease as the 
286 Articles 25 and 26 of Law No 1340 of 2009. SIC decisions are issued through resolutions.
287 In 2024, the threshold was 1,641,044.99 tax value units (UVT) of the parties' operating income or 
total assets (individually or jointly) obtained in 2023 (SIC Resolution No. 82882 of 2023). In general, only 
operating income and/or total assets obtained in the national territory are taken into account. However, 
income or assets obtained abroad are taken into account when companies hold a share of the Colombian 
market exclusively as a result of exports and have no assets or income in Colombia (SIC Resolution No. 2751 
of 2021).

variables change); and, (c) monitored freedom, under which prices are set freely but they must be 
notified and increases must be justified and approved.296
3.142.  The regulator or ministry responsible for the sector are in change of implementing the pricing 
policy, ex officio or at the request of a party (producers, importers, distributors, consumer 
associations).297 Prices are controlled when it is determined that there is a market failure that hinders 
efficiency, such as the existence of a dominant operator. Each entity applies its own procedure to 
determine the existence of a failure, through market analysis, and relevant case law and SIC 
recommendations on competition are taken into account.298 The SIC is responsible for determining 
which regime that the regulator or ministry will use.299 For raw milk, agrochemicals, fuels (regular 
gasoline and motor fuel oil), and medicaments and medical devices, the SIC is also responsible for 
monitoring implementation of the regimes and, in the event of non-compliance, for imposing fines.300 
During the period under review, the authorities noted that companies operating in the medicaments, 
raw milk and fuel markets were sanctioned.
3.143.  The direct control regime, which, in 2018, the authorities indicated was an exceptional 
measure301, continues to be used for certain goods, particularly medicaments. In this case, prices 
for manufacturers, importers, wholesalers and distributors are controlled. Retail sales prices are 
controlled only in the case of one type of antiretroviral, the maximum price of which when selling to 
the public has been controlled since 2013 by the National Commission on the Pricing of Medicines 
and Medical Devices (CNPMDM).302 The list of medicaments subject to direct control is updated every 
year after having assessed market behaviour.303 Medicaments not included in the list are subject to 
monitored freedom.304
3.144.  Between 2018 and 2024, Colombia maintained a number of price stabilization funds; for 
example, for cotton, sugar, cocoa, coffee (since 2019), palm, and fuels The funds' main objectives 
are to protect consumers from international price fluctuations, guarantee a profitable income for 
producers, regulate domestic production, and boost exports. With regard to competition, the use of 
these funds is allowed, although it does constitute a restriction of competition.305
3.3.5  State trading and state-owned enterprises
3.3.5.1  State trading
3.145.  In 2017, Colombia stated in its last notification on state trading that nine Colombian state 
trading enterprises manufactured, marketed and exported distilled spirits306 and imported raw 
materials to produce such spirits.307 These companies operated under the revenue-generating 
distilled spirits monopoly exercised by the departments.308 There are currently monopolies in 24 of 
the 32 departments; in these cases, a permit is required to introduce imported or domestic distilled

spirits and potable alcohol (ethanol).309 The aim of this policy is to enable the departments to finance 
health and education services.
3.146.  The departments produce the spirits either at their own distilleries (public) or by signing 
maquila or concession contracts with distilleries belonging to other departments or private 
entities.310 In addition to the awarding process, the difference between a maquila contract and a 
concession is the ownership of the spirit's trademark. With maquila contracts, the department retains 
the right to the trademark, whereas when a concession is granted, the concession holder receives 
the right to the trademark.311 Departmental enterprises that exercise a revenue-generating 
monopoly benefit from a preferential income tax rate (9%).312
3.147.  A temporary permit from the governor's office is required to introduce spirits into a 
department. Permits are issued to both public and private operators for an initial period of 
10 years313, and an operating fee of 2% of annual sales is paid to maintain their validity.314 According 
to legislation, these permits are not used to establish quotas or set minimum selling prices.315 
However, the departments may stop issuing permits, for a maximum of six years, when an increase 
in foreign or domestic spirits threatens local production.316 Permit suspension procedures are 
governed by departmental regulations.317 Some departments have implemented this measure since 
2018. In addition, governor's offices may request that the National Government apply a safeguard 
measure for imported rum, regardless of origin (Safeguards).318
3.148.  As regards taxes, departments exercising a monopoly are not entitled to proceeds from the 
national consumption tax (INC) on distilled spirits. However, governor's offices may set a charge 
known as a "participación"319, which cannot be lower than the INC rate. They may also establish 
such a charge for each litre of potable alcohol that is sold or introduced. MinHacienda sets the rate 
of this charge annually.320
3.149.  The State retains the revenue-generating monopoly on gambling and betting. However, it 
can hire private operators to exercise it.321
3.150.  The import and export of arms, ammunition and explosives, other than for industrial use, 
are reserved for the State.322

3.3.5.2  State-owned enterprises
3.151.  In 2023, there were 32 state-owned enterprises in Colombia (compared to 39 in 2018), and 
66 companies had minority state holdings (unchanged from 2018).323 One reason for the drop in the 
number of state-owned enterprises was the creation of the Grupo Bicentenario (BG) in 2019, which 
grouped together all the state-owned enterprises in the financial sector.324 Furthermore, in 2021, 
the state-owned enterprise Ecopetrol acquired a state holding (51.4%) in the company Interconexión 
Eléctrica (ISA). In 2023, the assets of the 32 state-owned enterprises amounted to 7.9% of GDP, 
as in 2018, although the value increased from COP 78.7 trillion to COP 124 trillion. The enterprises 
generate about 34,700 direct jobs (as opposed to approximately 29,200 in 2018).325
3.152.  State-owned enterprises may be state-owned industrial and commercial enterprises (EICEs), 
semi-public companies (SEMs), residential public utility service providers (ESPs) or state welfare 
enterprises (ESEs). EICEs and SEMs carry out industrial and commercial activities. The main 
differences between these types of enterprises are state participation (100% in EICEs) and the legal 
regime. EICEs are governed by private law, while SEMs are governed by public law (if the State 
owns between 51% and 90% of the assets) or private law (if the State's share is 50% or less).326
3.153.  The 32 state-owned enterprises operate in various sectors of the economy, mainly in 
financial services, energy (hydrocarbons and electricity), telecommunications, transport, health, 
agriculture and defence.327 In general, state-owned enterprises compete with private operators, 
except in the defence sector, where the Defence Industry (INDUMIL) retains the monopoly on 
manufacturing, marketing, exporting and importing arms, ammunition and explosives.328 Certain 
state-owned enterprises, such as Ecopetrol, continue to be major operators in their respective 
markets.
3.154.  During the review period, Colombia continued to implement the General Policy on National 
State-owned Enterprises, adopted in 2015, with the aim of strengthening the management of the 
enterprises to increase their value.329 As a result, the Grupo Bicentenario (BG) was established in 
2019 to boost the efficiency of state-owned financial enterprises.330 In addition, rules have been 
established to improve the management of the resources of state-owned enterprises and the 
operation of boards of directors.331 During the period under review, Colombia continued centralizing 
asset management for state-owned enterprises, and, in 2023, the Ministry of Finance and Public 
Credit (MinHacienda) began to administer 18 state-owned enterprises that accounted for 97.3% of 
public enterprises' total assets.332 State-owned enterprises are subject to auditing and fiscal 
monitoring by the Office of the Comptroller-General of the Republic.333 In addition, every year 
MinHacienda submits to Congress a list of public enterprises that could be privatized.334 During the

3.3.6  Government procurement
3.155.  The government procurement regime is governed by Law No. 80 of 1993 establishing the 
General Procurement Statute, Law No. 1150 of 2007, which, inter alia, defines government 
procurement procedures, and various decrees, including the Single Regulatory Decree (DUR) of the 
Administrative Sector for National Planning (Decree No. 1082 of 2015).336 Government procurement 
procedures are implemented through general circulars, guides and manuals.337 Procuring entities 
also have government procurement manuals.338 At the WTO, Colombia has been an observer in the 
Committee on Government Procurement since 1996.
3.156.  Between 2018 and 2024, various amendments were made to Laws No. 80 and No. 1150 to 
improve government procurement programming, contract design and transparency.339 The 
amendments include the obligation to submit tenders in two sealed envelopes when bidding for 
public works contracts (Law No. 1882 of 2018) and the adoption of standardized documents for all 
procedures (Law No. 1882 of 2018 and Law No. 2022 of 2020). New preferences were introduced 
to foster MSME participation and the inclusion of women (Law No. 2069 of 2020 and Decree No. 
1860 of 2021), and the definition of "origin" was expanded to promote the procurement of Colombian 
services (Decree No. 680 of 2021).
3.157.  All public entities (national and local) must, in principle, comply with the procurement regime 
set out in Laws No. 80 and No. 1150. However, to achieve trade, industrial, financial or social 
objectives, certain public entities are governed by special procurement procedures (set out in specific 
laws or government procurement manuals340) or by private law contracts. Such is the case for the 
Central Bank and state-owned enterprises.341
3.158.  Contracts financed with resources from international organizations and concession contracts 
are governed by specific provisions.342 Law No. 1508 of 2012 governs public-private partnership 
contracts.
3.159.  During the period under review, the share of government procurement in public expenditure 
remained at around 40% between 2018 and 2021, but began decreasing in 2022, reaching 28.1% 
in 2023 (Table 3.21). According to the authorities, this could be attributed to a number of factors, 
including budgetary adjustments resulting from fiscal pressures and increased national debt, and/or 
the fact that procurement processes were not carried out for administrative reasons. Government 
procurement accounted for 13.1% of GDP in 2018 and decreased to 8.4% in 2024. During the review

3.160.  The government procurement system must ensure that public utilities are provided 
efficiently.343 The National Government Procurement Agency (Colombia Compra Eficiente), as the 
governing body of the Government Procurement and Contracts System, is responsible for ensuring 
that the System complies with the principles of equality, efficiency, effectiveness, transparency and 
accountability to optimize the use of public resources.344 The main control entities are the Office of 
the Comptroller-General of the Republic (fiscal control) and the Office of the Public Prosecutor 
(integrity of public officers participating in proceedings).345 Despite this, in 2022, the OECD noted 
the risk of corruption associated with government procurement, particularly in infrastructure 
projects. In addition, in 2021, the losses from unfinished works due to irregularities were estimated 
at 2.2% of GDP.346 According to the Office of the Auditor-General of the Republic of Colombia, at the 
end of 2023, unfinished works amounted to 0.4% of GDP.347
3.161.  Foreign nationals are accorded national treatment based on the principle of reciprocity348 or 
in accordance with preferential trade agreements. In both cases, foreign nationals have access to 
the preferences granted to domestic suppliers.349
3.162.  Colombia uses government procurement to promote production and employment. 
Preferences are therefore granted to promote the purchasing of goods and services of Colombian 
origin. Bids that include goods and services of Colombian origin benefit from a margin of preference 
of 10% to 20%, while bids for foreign goods and services that include inputs of Colombian origin

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 85 -
 
have a margin of preference of 5% to 15%.350 The definition of "origin", as it pertains to services, 
was amended in 2021 to include the domestic goods and labour required to supply those services.351
3.163.  Since 2021, Colombia has promoted MSME participation, women's empowerment and the 
inclusion of vulnerable groups in government procurement processes. To this end, preference 
margins were introduced: up to 0.25% for MSMEs and women-led businesses352; 1% for companies 
employing persons with disabilities353; and up to 3% for surveillance and security companies and 
cooperatives that employ persons with disabilities and women.354 In addition to preferences, less 
stringent criteria were also introduced to promote the participation of MSMEs and women-led 
businesses.355
3.164.  To foster inclusion, procuring entities have, since 2019, been able to reserve contracts if 
they determine that the quality and fulfilment of the contract are guaranteed; in such cases, between 
5% and 10% of the goods may be purchased from vulnerable groups.356 This provision does not 
cover MSMEs or women-led businesses.
3.165.  During the review period, Colombia tried to reduce the negative impact that government 
procurement could have on the environment357 by adopting responsible and inclusive procurement 
practices to develop the circular economy and enhance the participation of the popular and 
community economy.358
3.166.  Each procuring entity is responsible for its government procurement procedures and 
prepares its Annual Procurement Plan (PAA), which does not need to be approved by Colombia 
Compra Eficiente.359 The procedures can be carried out in person or online. Colombia's Electronic 
System for Government Procurement (SECOP), administered by Colombia Compra Eficiente, has 
three platforms: SECOP I, for publishing PAAs and bidding specifications; SECOP II, for carrying out 
transactions and publishing contract-related information; and the Colombian Government's E-Store 
(TVEC), for purchasing commonly used goods and services and the minimum quantity goods listed 
in the Catálogo del Gran Almacén (procurement catalogue).360 Since 2020, the use of SECOP II has 
been mandatory for all procuring entities. However, it has started to be used gradually.361 The 
authorities have indicated that around 41% of procuring entities use SECOP II.
3.167.  There were no changes to government procurement methods during the period 2018-24. 
The methods include open tendering (as a general rule, this is the method to be used), abridged 
selection, merit-based competition, single tendering and minimum amount procurement.362 With the 
exception of merit-based competition, which is used only for contracting consultancy services or 
architectural projects, these methods can be used to contract goods, services or works. The 
procurement method to be used depends on thresholds, which are determined based on each entity's

3.168.  To participate in government procurement, all suppliers established in Colombia (including 
branches of foreign companies) must be enrolled in the Single Bidders Register (RUP), which is 
administered by the Chambers of Commerce. However, enrolment is not required to participate in 
single tendering and minimum amount procurement, nor to purchase health services or agricultural 
products.366 Nor is enrolment required for actors of the popular economy.367 Foreign suppliers are 
not required to enrol in the RUP. Enrolment in the RUP bears a cost (in 2024, COP 741,000) which, 
according to the OECD, could be an obstacle to government procurement.368
3.169.  In general, Colombia limits the number of participants in a procurement process only if 
pre-qualification is necessary because of the contract's features369; in an emergency; or if the object 
to be procured is manufactured or distributed by a single supplier, in which case the supplier will be 
contracted through single tendering instead of public procurement.370 Furthermore, in a contracting 
process involving less than USD 125,000, two MSMEs (three MSMEs prior to 2021) that have been 
in operation for more than one year may request the procuring entity to allow only MSMEs to 
participate.371 The authorities have indicated that this restriction has been used since 2018. Only 
MSMEs domiciled in the department or municipality where the procurement will be conducted may 
be invited.372 According to the 2022 OECD Economic Survey, public purchases are often

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 87 -
 
characterized by a lack of competition, significant concentration among contractors and frequent 
contract renewals and amendments without new tenders.373
3.170.  To prevent collusion, since 2018, open tendering procurement for works requires that 
technical and economic tenders be submitted in two sealed envelopes and evaluated separately. 
One envelope contains the technical bid, which is evaluated first, and the other contains the 
economic bid, which is opened once the technical evaluation is complete. If required, participants 
have five working days to provide additional information for the technical evaluation.374
3.171.  The successful bid must be the most favourable.375 The lowest price is the determining factor 
for minimum amount and commonly used goods and services.376 For the other methods, the bid with 
the highest score or the best cost-benefit ratio is selected377; technical and economic criteria are 
evaluated, and since 2023, entities may include environmental and/or social criteria.378 If there is a 
tie, a mechanism is implemented whereby up to 12 criteria are applied successively and exclusively 
to select the contractor.379 For open tendering, contracts are awarded in public hearings.380 The use 
of this mechanism does not extend to other procurement methods. The award may be appealed 
before the administrative courts; if the appeal is dismissed, it may be submitted to the Chamber of 
Administrative Disputes of the Council of State.381
3.172.  During the review period, several operators were penalized for public procurement collusion, 
which is prohibited per se. The operators in question may be fined and/or imprisoned, in addition to 
being unable to participate in public procurement procedures for eight years. Participation in the 
collaboration benefits programme does not exempt offenders from criminal liability, but does allow 
them to mitigate the penalties.382
3.3.7  Intellectual property rights
3.173.  Colombia's productive transformation from an extractive to a knowledge economy is one of 
the country's main economic development objectives in the medium term. Intellectual property is a 
key element of this transformation, as it contributes to innovation and knowledge transfer.383 
Accordingly, the National Intellectual Property Policy for the period 2021-31 seeks to promote the 
use and exploitation of intellectual property rights.384 Other policies, such as the National 
Reindustrialization Policy adopted in 2023 and the National Science, Technology and Innovation 
Policy 2021-31, are also aimed at promoting innovation through the adoption of new technologies.
3.174.  During the review period, Colombia introduced tax incentives, including exemptions, credits 
and deductions, to promote activities related to science, technology and innovation.385 SMEs also

benefited from tax incentives to invest in science, technology and innovation projects, as well as 
discounts on administrative fees and industrial property (patents and trademarks) registration 
costs.386 In 2022, preferential rates were granted to young people aged 18-28 (trademarks)387,

and Technology estimates that investment in science, technology and innovation activities amounted 
to 0.8% of GDP in 2020 (most recent year for which data is available).
3.175.  As regards the use of intellectual property, utility models and industrial designs have been 
the most widely used type of protection by residents since 2018 (Chart 3.4). In 2022, patent 
applications by residents increased considerably following a campaign to promote the protection of 
inventions.390 However, few patents were granted during the review period. Between 2018 and 2024, 
residents obtained patents in areas such as mechanical engineering, chemistry, and medical 
technology, measuring and control instruments.391 The registration of trademarks by residents 
remains high, mainly for pharmaceutical products.392

3.176.  Colombia also encourages creation and innovation in the area of culture, as the creative 
industry has been identified as a strategic sector for diversifying the economy.393 In this regard, the 
authorities have indicated that a number of technical assistance programmes were implemented 
between 2018 and 2024 to help businesses in the creative economy obtain financing and strengthen 
their export capacity, among other things. Furthermore, income tax incentives, such as refunds and 
deductions, are granted to the film industry.394 Literary works are exempt from income tax395, as 
was the publishing industry until 2022.396 However, the performance of businesses operating in the 
creative economy was largely unsatisfactory owing to the lack of training and financial resources, 
the low research and creation index and the high concentration of cultural infrastructure.397 In 2021 
(most recent year for which data is available), the creative economy contributed 2.3% to GDP.398
3.177.  Intellectual property rights are protected under domestic law, primarily the Constitution 
(Article 61), and Andean regulations. Industrial property is regulated by Decision No. 486 of 2000399, 
copyright and related rights are regulated by Decision No. 351 of 1993 and Law No. 23 of 1982 (to 
the extent that it does not contradict Andean regulations), and plant varieties are covered by 
Decision No. 345 of 1993.400 Decree No. 1074 of 2015 (Single Regulatory Decree for the Trade, 
Industry and Tourism Sector) partially regulates Decision No. 486. In 2018, Colombia revised Law 
No. 23 and adopted new provisions to protect copyright and related rights in order to comply with 
the commitments made under various regional trade agreements.401
3.178.  The WTO Agreement on Trade-Related Aspects of Intellectual Property Rights (TRIPS) and 
the 2005 Protocol, as well as several WIPO-administered treaties, including the Marrakesh Treaty to 
Facilitate Access to Published Works for Persons Who Are Blind, Visually Impaired or Otherwise Print 
Disabled (Colombia deposited the instrument of ratification in 2025), are also part of the legal 
framework governing intellectual property rights in the country.402 Colombia is party to the 
International Convention for the Protection of New Varieties of Plants (UPOV Convention) (1978 Act), 
although the authorities have indicated that Colombia applies the rules under the 1991 Act.
3.179.  The administrative bodies for the intellectual property regime are still the Supervisory 
Authority for Industry and Trade (SIC), attached to MinCIT, for industrial property; the National 
Copyright Directorate (DNDA), attached to the Ministry of the Interior; the Colombian Agricultural 
Institute (ICA), under the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development, for breeders' rights and 
test data for agrochemical products403; and the National Food and Drug Surveillance Institute 
(INVIMA), attached to the Ministry of Health and Social Welfare (MinSalud), for test data for 
pharmaceutical products.404

3.180.  Colombia authorizes parallel imports of products protected by industrial property rights.405 
However, regarding patents, parallel imports are not permitted for patents granted for methods or 
procedures.406 Since 2018, Colombia has allowed parallel imports of works protected by copyright 
and related rights.407
3.181.  Patents and utility models are protected for 20 years after the application filing date. The 
system allows limitations on exclusive rights, such as the Bolar clause408 and compulsory licences.409 
These are granted on a number of grounds, such as the public interest410, which includes 
emergencies, national security, environmental protection and access to medicines, according to 
CAN's interpretation.411 In 2024, SIC granted MinSalud the first compulsory licence on public interest 
grounds, authorizing the production and importation of generic antiretrovirals containing a patented 
active ingredient.412 At the time of finalization of this report, the compulsory licence remained valid 
and had been used.413
3.182.  Applications for a patent or registration with SIC can be filed in person or online through the 
Industrial Property System (SIPI). The procedures are set out in the Single Circular (Title X) 
containing the applicable general provisions issued by SIC.414 Since 2018, various provisions on 
patents, industrial designs and distinctive signs have been amended.
3.183.  In 2020, changes were made to patent procedures relating to: (a) the filing of divisional 
applications for new and existing patents415; (b) the conversion of an application; and 
(c) patentability analysis, including the recognition of patentability results obtained abroad.416 New 
fees were also set.417 As for industrial designs, new forms such as graphical user interfaces (GUIs) 
and animated designs could be registered as of 2022.418
3.184.  Traditional and non-traditional trademarks; commercial, collective and certification 
trademarks; and common and well-known trademarks can be registered.419 Renowned trademarks

are protected by the decisions of the Andean Community Court of Justice.420 In 2021, CAN adopted 
Decision No. 876 to protect country marks.421
3.185.  The procedure for registering trademarks was changed several times during the review 
period. One of the main changes was the introduction of the fast-track formality examination, a 
procedure with no additional charge that can only be requested by certain applicants for the online 
registration of traditional trademarks.422 The protection of colour as a distinctive feature of the 
trademark was also permitted, except in the case of nominative, sound, olfactory and colour 
marks.423 Furthermore, the registration and transfer of distinctive signs between persons of the same 
business group were regulated.424 In addition, amendments were made regarding, inter alia, the 
description of the good or service targeted by the trademark; the process for notifying the applicant; 
changes of applicant or owner; the waiver or withdrawal of rights; and the division of a 
registration.425
3.186.  Copyright protects artistic, literary and scientific works and computer programs.426 The 
registration of works, performances and phonograms with the DNDA is voluntary and free of 
charge.427 In 2018, Colombia began to regulate the protection of orphan works428, increased the 
term of protection of legal persons' economic rights from 50 years to 70 years after the first 
authorized publication429, and established new cases in which works can be used free of charge and 
without the consent of the copyright holder.430 It also specified that owners may authorize or prohibit 
reproduction of the work, including in electronic form.431 In Colombia, technological protection 
measures can be circumvented to allow access for persons with visual impairments.432
3.187.  In Colombia, a conciliation process must be undertaken at an arbitration and conciliation 
centre run by SIC, the DNDA or the chambers of commerce, or at a privately run centre, before a 
civil or commercial complaint can be lodged.433 If the dispute is not resolved, the complaint is 
brought to the civil courts or SIC, the DNDA or the ICA.434 It should be noted that the courts have 
no specialized judges, whereas the other bodies do.435 The right holder may apply for injunctions

before or during the court proceedings.436 Rulings can be appealed before the district high courts, 
and Bogotá District High Court hears appeals against decisions rendered by SIC, the DNDA and the

3.188.  The amount of compensation in the field of industrial property is calculated on the basis of 
criteria such as the harm caused and the profits obtained.438 Trademark owners and, since 2018, 
holders of copyright and related rights may elect to receive a predetermined amount of 
compensation.439 Infringements under the intellectual property regime may also be subject to 
criminal action. In 2018, circumvention of technological protection measures was established as a 
criminal offence. The Office of the Public Prosecutor is responsible for investigating intellectual 
property offences and conducting prosecutions in court, at its own initiative or on the basis of a 
complaint.
3.189.  Border measures are regulated by Decree No. 1165 of 2019 (Title 17)440, which tacitly 
repealed Decree No. 390 of 2016. DIAN may, at the request of the right holder or ex officio, detain 
import, export and/or transit goods at Colombia's border if they are suspected to be pirated copies 
or counterfeit brands.441 Once the goods have been detained, the right holder may apply to DIAN 
for a temporary suspension of customs clearance. The holder has 10 days in which to file a complaint, 
submit a copy to DIAN and comply with other requirements, such as the posting of a guarantee, 
otherwise the goods must be released.442 In the Colombian market, the police act ex officio or at the 
request of a party.443
3.190.  Between 2018 and 2024, the value of products seized at the border fluctuated substantially 
(Table 3.23). The type of seized goods (used mobile phones) accounts for the increase in 2021. The 
products often include electronics, textiles, perfumes and toys.444 Importers/exporters are liable to 
a fine equivalent to 400 UVT per offence.445

4  TRADE POLICIES BY SECTOR
4.1  Agriculture, forestry and fisheries
4.1.1  Agriculture
4.1.  The agricultural and livestock sector (agriculture, livestock, hunting and forestry) remains 
important for Colombia's economy, given its contribution to poverty reduction, job creation for 
women and the economic development of certain regions. Moreover, Colombia continues to be one 
of the leading producers and exporters of agricultural products such as coffee, bananas, cocoa and 
flowers.
4.2.  The sector's share of GDP increased from 6% in 2018 to 9% in 2024 (Table 4.1). According to 
the authorities, the increase in production has mainly been due to advanced planting to cope with 
the drought caused by the Niña phenomenon, changes in credit incentives mainly benefiting small 
farmers with lower incomes1 and improvements in crop yields resulting from investments in new 
technologies and machinery. Growth in the agricultural sector fluctuated during the review period, 
registering a significant decline in 2022 due to the 2021 national strike. The strike blocked transport, 
causing a shortage of agricultural inputs and a loss of agricultural and livestock products, as they 
were unable to reach the market. Despite a decline since 2018, the agricultural and livestock sector 
continues to be a major employer. On average, between 2018 and 2024, some 15.4% of the 
economically active population worked in this sector (Table 4.1). It is worth noting that women 
account for half of the working-age population in Colombia,2 and that (at the end of 2024) 17.4% 
of women were employed in this sector.3 There are, for example, some 200,000 formal posts directly 
or indirectly related to floriculture, 60% of which are held by women.4
4.3.  During the period under review, the average contribution to GDP of fisheries remained modest, 
with little change (0.3%).5

trimestrales/pib-informacion-tecnica; labour market data provided by the authorities and based on 
data from the UN Comtrade database.
4.4.  Colombia remains a net exporter of agricultural products (by the WTO definition). Their share 
of exports initially increased from 17.1% to 24.8% (from 2018 to 2020), later declining in 2023, to 
19.9%. The main agricultural export products were, as in previous years, coffee, flowers and fruits, 
mainly bananas. These products accounted for 64.7% of agricultural exports in 2023 (68.4% in 
2018) (Table 4.3). As for imports, in 2023, agricultural products accounted for 14.5% of the total; 
Colombia imports mainly cereals (maize and wheat). During the period under review, maize imports 
grew significantly (around 78% in terms of value), increasing from 16.5% of total agricultural 
imports in 2018 to 20.5% in 2023. The authorities reported that the increase was mainly due to an 
increase in domestic demand owing to the growth of the compound feed industry for the livestock, 
poultry and aquaculture sectors. The main export markets are the United States (41%) and the 
EU27 (21%), followed by Canada and Ecuador, both with about 3% of the market. While those 
countries had been ranked in the same order in 2018, their market shares had changed somewhat, 
as they had stood at 39% for the United States and 25% for the EU27.
4.5.  Colombia's agricultural policy is based on the Constitution, which states in article 65 that "food 
production shall enjoy the special protection of the State". During the period 2018-20, following the 
guidelines of the Constitution and the NDP, the MADR thus outlined plans and strategies such as the 

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 95 -
 
Colombia Siembra Plan (2015-18),6 a Field for Equity 2019-227 and, from 2023, the Institutional 
Action Plan.8 In general terms, the policy objective is to create the social and economic conditions 
necessary to promote the development of an inclusive and sustainable agricultural sector. More 
specifically, the policy seeks to increase agricultural production, promote exports and ensure food 
security through the expansion of cultivated areas, increased investment and the promotion of 
sustainable technologies. Colombia is also continuing its processes of agricultural planning and the 
formalization of land tenure, not just to improve the living conditions of the rural population, but 
also to promote investment and, by so doing, to protect the rural ecosystem and build the capacity 
to mitigate the effects of climate change and the risks of pests and diseases that can affect 
production.
4.6.  During the period under review, Colombia has made no substantial changes to the trade policy 
instruments it uses, among which the SAFP and the price stabilization funds stand out. These 
instruments are aimed at safeguarding producers' incomes and maintaining domestic price stability.9 
The main change concerns direct support and credit policies for producers, which have been 
redirected to provide more technical assistance to small farmers and to the less developed regions 
of the country. Thus, access to financing has been facilitated through preferential credit rates and 
other financial instruments, enabling the financial inclusion of the agricultural and rural sector, with 
the aim of increasing productivity and inclusiveness.10
4.1.2  Measures affecting imports and exports
4.7.  In 2024, the average tariff applied to agricultural products (WTO definition) was 16.1%, slightly 
higher than in 2017 (15.4%), as well as higher than the tariff applied to non-agricultural products 
(5.4%) and the overall average tariff (6.8%). If the SAFP is taken into account, the arithmetic 
average of MFN duties for agricultural products in 2024 was even higher, at 19.2% (20.7% in 2017) 
(Table 3.3). Tariffs range from 0% to 98% (including the SAFP, between 0% and 171.5%). The tariff 
lines subject to an MFN tariff higher than 25% are white maize (HS 1005); vegetables (HS 0713); 
guts, bladders and stomachs of animals (other than fish) (HS 0504); beef (HS 02); rice (HS 1006); 
whey (HS 0404); and milk and cream (HS 0402) (Section 3).
4.8.  Colombia continues to use the SAFP to stabilize the import cost of 13 agricultural products 
through variable tariffs.11
4.9.  With regard to the bound tariff, the highest binding levels are for agricultural products, although 
the tariffs applied to these products are substantially below these levels (Table A3.1).
4.10.  In general, Colombia maintains export quotas under certain preferential trade agreements 
and to fulfil its commitments under international treaties. Quotas are requested from the MADR 
through the VUCE. In all cases, preferential tariffs are higher for agricultural products.
4.11.  Colombia maintains tariff quotas for agricultural products. Nevertheless, in its most recent 
notification, which refers to 2020, Colombia reported that these quotas have not been activated, as 
the in-quota tariff is higher than the MFN tariff.12

4.12.  Colombia has reserved the right to use the special safeguard for agricultural products, but 
according to the authorities, it was not used during the period under review.
4.13.  Some agricultural products, including eggs, milk and some fruit and horticultural products, 
both imported and domestic, as well as food from the basic basket of goods, are exempt from VAT 
(the general rate is 19% and there is a reduced rate, of 5%). For maize and rice, Colombia applies 
a different VAT rate depending on the end use of the goods (0% for human consumption and 5% 
for industrial use). In addition, cigarettes and manufactured tobacco are subject to the national 
consumption tax. Since 2022, a health tax applies to certain foods with high sugar, sodium or 
saturated fat content.
4.14.  Colombia uses a system of automatic and non-automatic (prior) import licensing, among other 
things to ensure animal and plant health. Therefore, in general, importing products and by-products 
of plant and animal origin requires import licences or permits administered by the ICA and the 
INVIMA (Section 3.3.3). In 2018, the ICA established a risk assessment system for inspections of 
imported agricultural products and by-products, based on: (a) the sanitary-zoosanitary risk category 
of the product; (b) the sanitary status of the country of origin; and (c) the inspection and interception 
record.
4.15.  For exports, Colombia prohibits or restricts the export of certain products, generally to protect 
the environment and public health, and to fulfil its commitments under international agreements 
such as the International Convention on Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora (CITES).
4.16.  All coffee exporters are subject to a specific levy for the coffee contribution, to finance the 
National Coffee Fund.
4.1.3  Support measures
4.17.  During the period 2018-24, Colombia reported that no export subsidies were provided for 
agricultural products, nor for any food aid in 2018,13 201914 and 2020.15
4.18.  In 2020, Colombia notified support programmes administered by the AUNAP of the MADR, 
responsible for the sustainable development of fisheries resources.16
4.19.  Colombia has reserved the right to use the special safeguard for agricultural products, but 
according to the authorities, it was not used during the period under review.17
4.20.  During the period under review, Colombia notified its domestic support commitments in 2018, 
2019 and 2020.18 The programmes aimed, inter alia, to promote the development of the agricultural 
sector, through research, pest and disease control and training, extension and marketing services. 
Colombia also notified, among its development programmes, regional assistance programmes, 
investment subsidies (to promote productivity), agricultural input subsidies for low-income 
producers and support for diversification to abandon illicit crops. In addition, product-specific 
support, such as for cotton, rice, milk, maize and cassava, was notified.
4.21.  The main objective of these programmes is to stabilize sales prices and to ensure the 
profitability of producers through government procurement, storage of surplus production and the 
promotion of consumption. The use of cotton fibre in the textile and apparel industry was promoted 
to maintain the cotton sector's profitability; for maize, consumption was encouraged through public 
procurement; the rice sector received support for storage and marketing; for cassava (in the 
department of Meta), the programme included storage and limits on marketed volumes; and for the 
13 WTO document G/AG/N/COL/65, 2 October 2019.
14 WTO document G/AG/N/COL/71, 17 May 2021.
15 WTO document G/AG/N/COL/77, 30 March 2023.
16 WTO document G/SCM/N/315/COL - G/SCM/N/343/COL, 1 July 2020.

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 97 -
 
dairy sector, public procurement and market stabilization measures were implemented, mainly for 
small producers.
4.22.  The Rice Storage Incentive Scheme, which was discontinued in 2024,19 sought to avoid a drop 
in producer prices when there were surpluses, normally in the second half of each year. In the 
second half of 2016, an incentive scheme was introduced for the storage of dried paddy rice or its 
equivalent in white rice.20 By 2023, the total value of the programme was COP 25 billion.21
4.23.  In order to promote reforestation and investment in new forest plantations to protect/exploit 
forest land, Colombia continues to use the Forest Incentive Certificate (CIF).22 Small, medium, and 
large producers benefiting from the CIF can receive cash assistance covering 50% (for introduced 
species) or 75% (for native species) of the total costs of planting or maintaining plantations for 
commercial purposes. Through the certificate, it is also possible to obtain 75% of the total cost of 
maintaining natural forests that are included in a forest establishment and management plan.
4.24.  Colombia continues to use price stabilization funds for certain agricultural products, such as 
cotton, sugar,23 cocoa, coffee, meat, milk and dairy products24 and palm kernel and palm oil.25 The 
funds' objectives are "to secure remunerative income for producers, taking into consideration 
international price fluctuations, to regulate domestic production and to promote exports". Each 
fund's steering committee therefore establishes a reference price or a price band based on the most 
representative international market quotation.26 When the international market price is below the 
reference price or the lower limit of a reference price band, producers, sellers and exporters receive 
compensation, and when the price is higher they pay a “stabilization payment” to the fund. The 
steering committee determines the stage of the marketing process in which the contributions and 
compensations will be applied to the producers, sellers and exporters. The funds are mainly financed 
by these contributions. Producers and exporters of cocoa must enter into a price stabilization 
agreement with the fund's administrative entity. Cotton producers and exporters must do so with 
the Colombian Cotton Confederation (CONALGODON).27
4.25.  The Coffee Price Stabilization Fund (FEPCafé), which applies exclusively to Colombian mild 
Arabica coffee and is administered by the Colombian Coffee Growers Federation, has two 
components: (a) temporary direct cash transfers, granted through the Coffee Farmer Income 
Compensation Stabilization Mechanism (MECIC) to offset fluctuations in coffee prices, and (b) 
incentives for risk management instruments, such as agricultural insurance or price and exchange 
rate hedging.28 To access the MECIC, producers must register with the Coffee Growers Information 
System (SICA) and sell their harvest through authorized buyers.29 The maximum amount covered 
by the Fund is 70% of the productive capacity, for each producer. FEPCafé's funding sources include 
public funds and the coffee contribution (US$ 0.005 per pound, paid by coffee producers when 
exporting).
4.26.  For cotton, in addition to the Cotton Price Stabilization Fund (FEPA), which allows for 
regulation of the domestic market through stabilization operations to safeguard the domestic price 
and prevent its decline in case of overproduction, other support programmes have also been

implemented.30 Moreover, in 2020, the Cotton Fibre Marketing Support Programme was established 
for the small and medium-sized producers in certain departments, to lessen the pandemic's impact. 
It was thus possible to maintain the production and supply of cotton to support domestic textile and 
apparel producers.31
4.27.  Additionally, there are livestock funds, with a federation known as the Federation of Livestock 
Funds (Fedefondos). The funds finance extension activities for any type of livestock, including: (a) 
the promotion and support of business development; (b) the integration of new technologies into 
production methods; (c) animal health campaigns; and (d) technical assistance.32
4.28.  The rice, cocoa and grain funds, created in 1983, are still functioning, according to the 
authorities.33 By law, those who purchase or process domestically produced paddy rice, cocoa, or 
wheat, barley, maize, sorghum and oats – whether for the domestic market (for final consumption 
or as an input) or for export – must contribute to the fund.34 "The development levy is based on the 
reference price set every six months by the MADR, or on the product's sale price, when the Ministry 
so determines by resolution, considering that special market conditions favour the interests of 
producers."35 The development levy on domestic production of grain legumes was established later.36 
The percentage for the contribution is set by legislation (Table 4.2). These contributions are used, 
inter alia, to promote the technological development of these crops; to ensure sanitary control; to 
promote marketing and exports; and to "support regulation of supply and demand to protect 
producers against abnormal price fluctuations and provide them with remunerative income".37

4.29.  During the COVID-1938 pandemic, the prices of agricultural inputs – specifically fertilizers and 
pesticides for agricultural use – were subject to a “monitored freedom” regime, which allowed prices 
to be freely set throughout the distribution chain, but with the requirement to report them to the 
MADR through the Agricultural Inputs Information Reporting System (SIRIIAGRO). If necessary, the 
MADR may intervene in the market by setting maximum selling prices for these products, but it has 
not done so during the period under review.39
4.30.  The price of raw milk is set through the System for Payment to the Supplier for Raw Milk, 
which establishes the parameters for determining a reference price based on composition (protein,

fat and total milk solid content per litre of raw milk). Producers receive bonuses or are subject to 
reductions from this reference price depending on the hygienic quality and composition of the milk.40 
The reference price is set annually. In addition, a differential payment system for milk and dairy 
products produced for export was implemented in the areas most affected by the conflict 
(ZOMACs).41 Under this system, a "competitive export price"42 is to be calculated, which is the price 
that the intermediary is to pay to the milk producer per litre or per litre equivalent in dairy products 
produced for export. The authorities have indicated that the system is in place, but no exports have 
been made under it.
4.31.  In Colombia, one of the main tools for supporting the agricultural sector is credit. In 2023, 
the objectives of agricultural and rural development credits (CFA) were reformulated. In addition to 
promoting production, employment and exports in the agricultural sector, they are now also to 
contribute to the adoption, through technology transfer, of sustainable and inclusive production 
practices. The aim is to improve social and economic conditions in the rural sector by promoting 
income distribution and food security.43
4.32.  The National Agricultural Credit System (SNCA) encompasses all institutions involved in 
formulating the credit policy for the agricultural sector and in coordinating the use of financial 
resources. The SNCA is composed of MADR, the National Agricultural Credit Commission (CNCA), 
the Fund for Financing of the Agricultural Sector (FINAGRO), the Agricultural Bank of Colombia (BAC) 
and other financial institutions whose main objective is the financing of the agricultural sector.44
4.33.  The CNCA, the lead body for financing in the sector, prepares an annual Indicative Agricultural 
and Rural Credit Plan45 as well as an Annual Incentives Plan, following the policy defined by the 
MADR and the overarching development strategies included in the NDP.46 The Indicative Credit Plan 
sets out the overall financing amounts required by the sector, interest rates and rediscount margins. 
For 2024, the amount allocated was between COP 24.9 trillion and COP 26.1 trillion, of which 
approximately 20% was to be used for small, low-income producers.47 The incentives plan sets the 
financial terms and conditions for special credit lines (LECs) and rural capitalization incentives 
(ICRs), through which financing is provided on preferential terms to small producers and special 
groups such as women and young people.
4.34.  In August 2024, the Credit Pact was signed between the National Government and the 
Colombian Banking and Financial Institutions Association (Asobancaria). It prioritizes financing for 
five sectors of the economy: housing, manufacturing, agriculture, the popular economy and tourism 
(Section 4.4.1) The authorities have indicated that COP 32.1 trillion was allocated under the Pact for 
2024; by December 2024, COP 6 trillion had been disbursed.
4.35.  FINAGRO48 is the financial institution responsible for channelling resources for Agricultural 
Development Credits (CAFs) through various financial instruments, namely: (a) special credit lines 
(LECs); (b) rural capitalization incentives (ICRs), which provide direct, non-reimbursable support for 
the purchase of capital goods; (c) agricultural guarantee funds (FAGs), which guarantee loans for

4.36.  Within the CAF framework, access to credit for the financing of investment and working capital 
is facilitated for all agricultural sectors and types of producers, with the exception of breeding 
activities for bullfighting and similar activities such as cockfighting and illicit crop production. 
However, better conditions are offered to small producers (including low-income small producers) 
and to rural population groups such as women, young people, ethnic groups, people in the popular 
economy and people in ZOMACs.49

4.37.  During the period under review, the total amount of loans for agricultural development was 
COP 179 trillion. During the same period, the amount of credits granted increased considerably 
(158%), with a slight decline in 2023 due to a larger share for small producers and small, low-
income producers (Table 4.4). Only 7.1% of the funds are provided to women. The sector that most 
benefited was livestock (13%), followed by rice (11%), short-cycle crops (10%) and sugar cane 
(9%).50

4.38.  During the period under review, 415,289 LEC credit transactions were carried out at 
preferential interest rates, benefiting 359,615 producers, for an amount of COP 8,423,168 million, 
and the subsidy amounted to COP 927,851 million. However, although the coverage was low, from 
2018 to 2024, the number of beneficiaries, as well as the total amount financed at preferential rates, 
grew at average annual rates of 29% and 32%, respectively (Table 4.5). Producers of rice and other 
crops (both short-cycle and perennial), as well as livestock producers, were the ones who made the 
greatest use of LECs.51

4.39.  Also, 42,599 capital incentive operations (ICRs) were carried out at preferential interest rates, 
for a total of COP 830,736 million, with the total subsidy amounting to COP 298,403 million 
(Table 4.5). Credits under the ICR were not granted in 2020 and declined substantially in 2021 due 
to the pandemic, but reached record levels in the following years to meet the need to adapt to an 
increase in post-pandemic demand. The producers who benefited most from these credit lines were 
the ones working with cocoa, tomatoes, coffee, palm and livestock, as well as fishermen.52
4.40.  Through the Agricultural Development Fund, the MADR co-finances projects to promote 
agricultural, fisheries and aquaculture production and rural development. During the period under 
review, the Fund disbursed approximately US$ 87.2 million (COP 361,743 million). It now mainly 
covers projects for the transfer of technology and the improvement of physical infrastructure for 
production.53 In addition to the Fund, the MADR administers other special funds, such as the 
Agricultural Solidarity Fund (FONSA) (administered by FINAGRO);54 the Rural Microfinance Fund 
(administered by FINAGRO); the Rural Women Development Fund (FOMMUR); and the Venture 
Capital Investment Fund.
4.1.4  Fisheries
4.41.  Although the Colombian fisheries subsector contributes little to GDP (0.3% in 2024), it is of 
vital importance to coastal populations, who live primarily on subsistence and artisanal fishing. In 
2022, Colombia's total fisheries production reached 320,000 tonnes, 60% of which came from

4.42.  Colombia is a net importer of fish and fishery products. During the review period, imports of 
fish and fishery products reached, on average, USD 511.2 million, while exports amounted to only 
USD 168.3 million. The main fish and fishery products imported were prepared or preserved tunas, 
skipjack tuna and bonito (30.5% of imports of fish and fishery products in 2023), shrimps and 
prawns (9.8%) and whole fish or pieces thereof (5.7%). For exports, the main products were tilapia 
fillets (35.6% of exports of fish and fishery products in 2023), tilapias (11.7%) and prepared or 
preserved tunas, skipjack tuna and bonito (10.9%). The import and/or export of certain fishery 
products requires authorization from the National Aquaculture and Fisheries Authority (AUNAP) 
through the Single Window for Foreign Trade (VUCE).57
55 FAO, Fishery and Aquaculture Country profiles - Colombia. Viewed at: 
https://www.fao.org/fishery/en/facp/col.
56 FAO, global capture production quantity database. Viewed at: https://www.fao.org/fishery/statistics-
query/en/capture/capture_quantity.
57 WTO document G/MA/QR/N/COL/1/Add.2, 19 January 2021.

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 103 -
 
4.43.  In 2020, to deliver on its commitment under the WTO Ministerial Decision on Fisheries 
Subsidies of 201758, Colombia notified to the WTO various AUNAP programmes targeting the 
fisheries sector.59 According to this notification, in 2016, 2017 and 2018, AUNAP allocated a total of 
COP 93,489 million (USD 31.3 million) to updating and implementing fisheries and aquaculture 
management measures at the national level, conducting inspections and monitoring of fisheries and 
aquaculture resources at the national level, and promoting the efficient and sustainable use of 
fisheries and aquaculture resources at the national level. In March 2025, Colombia deposited its 
instrument of acceptance of the WTO Agreement on Fisheries Subsidies.
4.44.  Colombia participates in a number of regional fisheries management organizations or 
arrangements (RFMO/A), directly or indirectly60, and is also a party to other regional and 
international fisheries-related instruments.61 In 2021, AUNAP joined the global Blue Justice 
Community initiative, an international cooperation platform that encourages communication and the 
development of tools to combat illegal, unreported and unregulated (IUU) fishing.62 Legislation also 
provides for measures to combat illegal fishing and the offence of illegal fishing activity in Colombian 
maritime territory, and applies to all natural and legal persons and de facto partnerships, regardless 
of nationality.63
4.45.  The legal framework governing the fisheries sector is made up of the General Law on the 
Fisheries Sector (Law No. 13 of 1990) and its implementing regulations64, Decree No. 1071 of 2015, 
the Comprehensive Policy for the Development of Sustainable Fishing in Colombia of 2015 and the 
National Plan for the Development of Sustainable Aquaculture in Colombia (PlaNDAS) of 2014. The 
main change was the enactment of Law No. 2268 of 2022, which regulates artisanal fishing. Under 
this Law, artisanal fishing is divided into commercial artisanal fishing and subsistence fishing. 
Commercial artisanal fishing is regulated by AUNAP, whereas subsistence fishing may be carried out 
freely throughout the national territory.65
4.46.  The Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MADR) is the sector's governing body. 
AUNAP, an agency under the MADR, is the regulatory body. It is responsible for, inter alia, managing 
fisheries and aquaculture resources, defining the requirements for the granting of fishing permits 
and authorizations, and establishing control and monitoring mechanisms for fishery activities.66 In 
addition, in 2022, AUNAP was granted new powers covering various areas, such as promoting the 
development of fishing communities, artisanal fishing exports, sustainable and responsible fishing 
practices, and financing programmes.67 As part of this review, the authorities indicated that AUNAP 
had implemented a number of measures related to its new powers.
4.47.  As regards fishing in waters under Colombian jurisdiction, Colombians enjoy preferential 
treatment compared to foreigners.68 However, the authorities indicated that, in practice, this 
provision does not apply to the award of fishing quotas as quotas are distributed without reference 
to nationality. Colombia has the Colombian Fisheries Statistical System (SEPEC), a fishing statistics 
platform, which is used to manage fisheries resources and set fishing quotas, distributed by AUNAP 
among the various quota holders.69
58 WTO document WT/MIN(17)/64 - WT/L/1031, 18 December 2017.

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 104 -
 
4.48.  Depending on its purpose, fishing in Colombia is classified as subsistence fishing, research 
fishing, sport fishing or commercial fishing (artisanal, industrial and ornamental).70 Commercial 
industrial fishing may be carried out by Colombian-flagged or foreign vessels chartered by Colombian 
fishing companies that allocate part of their catch to the domestic market. The catch must be 
unloaded in Colombian ports.71
4.49.  All types of fishing, except subsistence fishing, require a permit issued by AUNAP. The 
issuance of a fishing permit is subject to the fulfilment of several conditions, such as the obligation 
to unload catch in Colombian ports prior to marketing and to allocate a minimum percentage to the 
domestic market, the obligation to submit periodic reports to AUNAP, the provision of a guarantee 
on the fishing of tuna and similar species by foreign-flagged vessels, and the obligation to adopt the 
necessary measures to prevent the catch of dolphins, turtles, sharks, rays, and chimaeras.72 A 
commercial (artisanal and industrial) fishing permit is valid for five years. The marketing of fish 
products is covered for the same period.
4.50.  AUNAP may establish areas reserved exclusively for commercial artisanal fishing, provided 
that fishers demonstrate reasonable use of fisheries resources. Companies engaged in artisanal 
fishing must be composed of Colombian natural persons.73 Fishing in lakes, marshes, oxbow lakes 
and reservoirs is reserved for artisanal fishers.74
4.51.  In the case of commercial aquaculture, AUNAP issues a cultivation permit that is valid for up 
to 10 years.
4.52.  Vessels exceeding 3 net registered tonnes must have a fishing licence. Vessels of less than 
three tonnes do not require such a licence, but they must be registered with AUNAP.
4.2  Mining and energy
4.53.  The Ministry of Mining and Energy (MinMINAS) formulates and adopts Colombia's energy 
policy. In 2021, Colombia declared efficient energy use to be an issue of public and social interest.75 
As a result, in 2022, Colombia developed a new Energy Transition Policy that draws on the 
Comprehensive Climate Change Management Plan76, with a view to increasing energy security by 
promoting the use of new technologies to cut greenhouse gas emissions.77 Another aim of the policy 
is to provide electricity in areas where this service is not yet available. To achieve this, and to 
promote the use of non-conventional energy sources, the Energy Communities Programme (national 
strategy) was developed.78
4.54.  In line with these policy objectives, Colombia amended its legal framework in order to enhance 
the use of non-conventional renewable energy sources.79 In 2019, 2021 and 2023, major 
amendments were made to Law No. 1715 of 2014, which promotes the use of non-conventional 
renewable energy.80 The most relevant amendments were those to support the development of new 
projects – such as geothermal energy and hydrogen projects – that are eligible for financing through 
the Fund for Non-Conventional Energy and Efficient Energy Management (FENOGE), and those 
modifying the incentives to invest in projects that use non-conventional energy sources.
70 Under Article 3 of the General Law on the Fisheries Sector, fishing activity is defined as the process of 
researching, extracting, cultivating, processing and marketing fisheries resources.

4.55.  During the period under review, alongside the incentives set forth in Decree No. 255 of 1992 
(Box 4.1), other incentives provided by Colombia included: (a) income tax deduction of up to 50% 
of the amount invested in projects to generate electricity from non-conventional sources for a period 
of 15 years (previously five years)81; (b) VAT exemption on imported goods and services needed for 
projects that use non-conventional energy sources82; (c) tariff exemptions for imported machinery, 
equipment, materials and inputs (provided that there is no local production)83; and (d) an increase 
from 20% to 33.33% in the annual accelerated depreciation rate applied to assets (machinery, 
equipment and civil works) over a 30-year period.84 For projects to be eligible for these incentives, 
a tax incentive (IT) certificate must be obtained from the Mining and Energy Planning Division 
(UPME).85 In 2024, 1,178 IT certificates were issued, 70.8% of which were for projects using 
non-conventional energy sources, 29% for efficient energy management projects and 0.2% for 
green hydrogen projects.86

for transport by oil and gas pipeline and for hydrocarbon refining and treatment operations.

4.56.  Other measures have also been introduced, such as allowing self-generating companies 
(owned by oil and/or gas producers) to sell their surplus energy in the wholesale energy 
market (MEM).87
4.57.  The General Royalties Scheme (SGR) for mining and energy was amended through 
Law No. 2056 of 2020 and its regulations.88 Starting in 2021, major changes were made to the 
distribution of royalties from mining and hydrocarbons resources (Chart 4.1).
4.58.  The main changes to the SGR were: (a) the creation of an environmental fund (equivalent to 
1% of the royalties collected annually); (b) an increase (from 11.2% to 25%) in the local or direct 
allocations to each department in which production occurs; (c) an increase in the funds made 
available (local investment) to municipalities with few resources and inhabitants and where no 
production-related operations occur; (d) a change in the allocation of royalties for regional 
investment (that is, for regions that have no production-related operations and that are not 
categorized as low-resource regions), with 40% of the royalties allocated to the region and 60% to 
the departments within the region; (e) an increase to 10% of the funds available for investments in

4.2.1  Mining
4.60.  The regulatory framework governing the mining sector includes the Political Constitution, the 
Mining Code (Law No. 685 of 2001) and Single Regulatory Decree No. 1073 of 2015. In 2022, 
Law No. 2250 formalizing small-scale mining was enacted. This Law simplifies formalization 
procedures and created the Mining Promotion Fund (FONMIN) to support small-scale mining.90 In 
addition, the PND 2022-26 seeks to mitigate the impact that mining may have on the environment, 
through, inter alia, socio-environmental plans to ensure the protection of natural areas and 
ecosystems.91
4.61.  In accordance with a ruling of the Council of State calling for the adoption of a more 
comprehensive vision of mining with a view to taking a water-focused approach to land use planning 
in order to protect areas of ecological importance, MinMINAS is drawing up a new National Mining 
Policy to replace the one adopted in 2016, to ensure that mining activities are developed in 
accordance with the necessary environmental, social and technical standards.92 As a result, 
Decree No. 044 of 2024 was issued; it establishes the criteria for declaring and defining temporary 
natural resource reserves within the framework of environmental and mining management and 
stipulated that no environmental concessions or licences would be granted for these zones for a 
five-year period.
4.62.  In Colombia, mining production is focused primarily on the extraction of fuel minerals (coal), 
precious minerals (gold, silver and platinum), non-metallic (salt and limestone) and metallic (iron 
and nickel) minerals, precious stones (emeralds) and minerals for construction (sand and gravel).
4.63.  Exploration and extraction require a concession contract (with a 30-year, renewable term) 
awarded by the National Mining Agency (ANM). There are still two methods of awarding concessions, 
depending on whether the area has been declared a "strategic mining reserve area".93 Concession 
rights are partially or wholly transferable. During the exploration, construction and assembly phases, 
companies operating concessions pay a surface tax to the State on the total area under concession 
(not paid during the extraction phase).94 In addition to the concession contract, the mining and 
energy guidelines must be followed before exploration can begin, and an environmental licence must 
be obtained from the Ministry of the Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS) for the 
construction-assembly and extraction phases.
4.64.  Concessions can be awarded to any type of operator, such as private companies (including 
multinationals), consortiums, community associations and, since 2023, Black, Afro-Colombian, 
Raizal and Palenquera community councils with the requisite legal personality.95
90 The regulations governing FONMIN are being developed. Online information from MinMINAS. Viewed 
at: https://www.minenergia.gov.co/es/servicio-al-ciudadano/foros/reglamentar-el-fondo-de-fomento-minero-
fonmin/.
91 Articles 2.2.5.12.1.5 and 2.2.5.12.3.3 of Decree No. 0977 of 2024. 
92 The ruling upheld the rights to enjoy a healthy environment and the protection of areas of special 
ecological importance. Information viewed at: https://www.suin-

93 Concession contracts are awarded on a "first come, first served" basis. Concession contracts for 
strategic mining reserve areas are awarded through an objective selection procedure 
(ANM Resolution No. 1006 of 2023).

4.65.  In Colombia, concessions are granted in free areas of no more than 10,000 hectares, with the 
exception of strategic mining reserve areas, which are areas in which minerals of strategic interest 
to Colombia can be found.96 The list of strategic minerals was updated in 2023.97
4.66.  The royalty rate for the extraction of minerals ranges from 1% to 12%. As in 2018, the price 
at the pithead is set by the Central Bank of Colombia (BRC) for precious minerals and by the UPME 
for all other minerals and metals.98 Owing to the increase in international prices, royalties from the 
extraction of Colombia's main minerals, especially coal, rose from COP 1.5 trillion in 2019 to 
COP 7.8 trillion in 2022 (latest figure available).99
4.67.  The marketing (sale, import and export) of gold and platinum is still subject to a tax of 4% 
and 5%, respectively, based on the price set by the BRC at the end of the month.100 The parafiscal 
contribution on emeralds remains at 1% of the exported value and is used to finance the National 
Emeralds Fund.101
4.2.2  Hydrocarbons
4.68.  The hydrocarbons sector is governed by the Political Constitution, the Petroleum Code 
(Decree No. 1056 of 1963), Single Regulatory Decree No. 1073 of 2015 and the Royalties Law 
(Law No. 2056 of 2020).
4.69.  Both private operators and Ecopetrol are involved in the sector. In 2024, the State had a 
holding of 88.49% in Ecopetrol.102 According to the authorities, refining activities are not conducted 
exclusively by Ecopetrol.
4.70.  Oil production103 went from 889,345 barrels per calendar day (bpcd) in 2018 to 755,469 in 
2024104, 61.4% of which was produced by Ecopetrol.105 According to the authorities, the storage 
capacity for oil and oil products was 30 million barrels (strategic tanks and terminals) in 2024, and 
there were 7,855 service stations, of which 6,334 were operational.
4.71.  The National Hydrocarbons Agency (ANH) awards hydrocarbon exploration and production 
(E&P) contracts to companies based on the Regulations on Contracts for Areas for Exploration and 
Production, which were updated in 2022.106 The new Regulations set out four methods for awarding 
contracts, three of which are competitive and one of which involves a direct award with a counter-bid 
(Table 4.7). Direct contracting is used for the exploration of new deposits. E&P contracts have a 
maximum term of 39 years, although they may be extended until the deposit has been depleted. 
96 Articles 31 and 32 of Law No. 685 of 2001, MinMINAS Resolution No. 180102 of 2012 (gold, platinum, 
copper, phosphate ores, potassium ores, magnesium ores, coal, uranium, iron and coltan), and 
ANM Resolution No. 45 of 2012.
97 ANM Resolution No. 1006 of 2023. New minerals were included in the list, such as nickel, zinc, 
manganese, bauxite, emeralds, silicon, limestone, gypsum and chromium, while potassium, uranium and

According to the authorities, there were 293 E&P contracts in force in 2024, with Ecopetrol operating 
23 of these contracts. The awarding of new contracts has been suspended.107
Table 4.7 Methods for awarding E&P contracts in 2024
Method
Description
Open competition
Interested parties (legal persons or associations) must submit bids for the 
areas available, as identified by the ANH. The bids must not be for less than 
the minimum investment amount set by the ANH. A submission hearing is 
held and the process is made public to enable counter-bids. The initial bidder 
is entitled to match or exceed the best counter-bid.
Special open competition
The ANH issues a public notice for all interested parties to take part and 
selects the best bid.
Special closed competition
The ANH invites a certain number of interested parties to take part and 
selects the best bid, taking into account the specific characteristics of the 
area and the contract to be allocated.
Direct award with counter-bid
The ANH must provide grounds for applying this method.
This method is used when, inter alia: (a) the area's nature or location is 
special; (b) there are social or environmental restrictions; (c) there is limited 
technical information about the subsurface; (d) there are general interest 
reasons; (e) there are special economic or energy policy considerations; and 
(f) production is already under way or deposits have already been discovered 
(whether developed or not) in the area being allocated. Other interested 
parties have the possibility of submitting a counter-bid.
Source:
Articles 16, 17 and 18 of ANH Agreement No. 003 of 2022.
4.72.  In the period under review, royalties remained unchanged at 8%, 20% or 25%, depending 
on the level of production, with the total amount paid calculated based on the production price at 
the wellhead.108 Oil production prices at the wellhead are not regulated, while gas prices are 
regulated by the Energy and Gas Regulatory Commission (CREG) when the gas is intended for the 
National Transport System.109 In addition, economic rights continued to be paid to the Government 
in relation to the use of the subsurface; training, institutional strengthening and technology transfer; 
production share; additional production share; and high prices.110
4.73.  To promote the use of clean energy, companies in the sector are encouraged to obtain a 
certificate indicating their carbon neutrality. Companies that obtain this certificate are exempt from 
paying the national carbon tax.111 Imports of inputs for the hydrocarbons industry enjoy duty-free 
treatment (Section 3).
4.74.  Operators must obtain authorization from MinMINAS in order to transport, store, refine, 
distribute, market and import hydrocarbons; the authorization is granted for an indefinite period.112 
When there is spare carrying capacity, access to the oil pipeline is readily provided for the transport 
of crude oil.113 In 2023, regulations on transport via multi-phase pipelines came into force, and new 
provisions governing the capacity market for the transport of natural gas were established.114 The 
taxes levied on oil and gas pipeline transport are governed by the new SGR115 and remain at 2% 
and 6%, respectively.116
4.75.  Refiners, importers, distributors and major consumers of fuels must be registered in the 
Information System for Liquid Fuels (SICOM).
