4.76.  Colombia continues to prohibit natural gas exports when domestic production falls short of 
domestic demand.117
4.77.  Colombia still has a price control system in place for regular petrol for motor vehicles, engine 
fuel oil (diesel) and biofuels to be blended with petrol for motor vehicles or engine fuel oil. The 
system was modified during the period under review: since 2021, CREG has set the tariffs and profit 
margins relating to fuel storage and inventories118 and to the rest of the supply chain (transport, 
logistics, marketing and distribution). Producer income (IP) is set by MinMINAS.119
4.78.  Consumer end prices for regular petrol for motor vehicles and engine fuel oil – otherwise 
known, respectively, as petrol and diesel – can be broken down into 17 components grouped into 
three categories: the producer's share (IP); logistics costs and profit margins (logistics and margins) 
along the supply chain; and taxes (Chart 4.2). The IP represents the largest share, making up over 
60% of the consumer price. The fossil fuel IP includes a parafiscal contribution.
Chart 4.2 Breakdown of the retail price of fuels
a
Evaporation loss was established through Article 4 of Law No. 26 of 1989; it is set by the Ministries 
but can also be modified by the National Government where necessary.
Note:
The retail price is that paid by consumers in Bogotá.
Source:
Compiled by the WTO Secretariat, based on information provided by the authorities.
4.79.  The aim of the Fuel Price Stabilization Fund (FEPC), which operates as a compensation 
mechanism, is to prevent international price volatility from being passed on to consumer prices in 
the domestic market. The FEPC is funded through parafiscal resources and those provided by the 
State.120 It compensates producers and importers when the parity price, which is based on the 
international price, is higher than the IP. When the parity price is lower than the IP, refiners and 
importers pay the difference (or a parafiscal contribution) to the FEPC.121

4.80.  When the FEPC was created, it was expected to be self-sustaining.122 However, since 2021, 
rising international fuel prices have meant that the FEPC has had to pay out considerable 
compensation, putting it under financial strain. As a result, an increase in consumer prices was 
phased in over 18 months, which helped to reduce the FEPC's debt from COP 36.7 trillion 
(USD 7,639 million) in 2022 to COP 20.5 trillion (USD 5,363 million) in 2023123 and then to 
COP 7.6 trillion (USD 1,723 million) in 2024.124
4.81.  Since 2022, MinHacienda has been underscoring the need to reduce the FEPC's losses, which 
have an impact on the fiscal deficit, and has suggested that the consumer price should be brought 
closer to the international market price.125 In addition, the Autonomous Fiscal Rule Committee 
(CARF) indicated that it was necessary to change the FEPC's methodology to include an assessment 
of its sustainability, since it has no budget limit and, in addition, it has an impact on production 
chains and inflation.126 Exports of fuels covered by the FEPC were therefore prohibited127, and it was 
decided that major end consumers, which are those that consume an annual average of over 
20,000 gallons per month (mining, oil and cement companies), are not eligible for the FEPC.128 
MinMINAS updates the list of companies deemed to be major consumers.129
4.2.3  Electricity
4.82.  The electricity sector's regulatory framework is based on three laws, two of which are from 
1994. Law No. 142 of 1994 establishes the public services regime, Law No. 143 of 1994 establishes 
the regime governing the electricity chain and Law No. 1715 of 2014 promotes the use of 
non-conventional renewable energy. Law No. 1715 underwent major amendments in line with 
Colombia's energy policy (see above).
4.83.  The Energy and Gas Regulatory Commission (CREG) is the sector's regulator. The UPME, the 
National Operations Council, the Institute for Energy Solutions Planning and Promotion (IPSE) and 
the Supervisory Authority for Public and Household Services (SSPD) are involved in planning, 
operating, controlling and monitoring the electricity system.130 MinMINAS, MinHacienda and the 
National Planning Department manage the state-owned enterprises involved in the sector.131
4.84.  There are private, public and semi-public companies operating in the electricity sector; they 
must all be registered in the SSPD's Single Register of Service Providers. The number of registered 
companies increased between 2018 and 2024, when there were 226 registered companies 
(189 private, 30 semi-public and 7 public companies).132 A concession contract is required for the 
generation, transmission and distribution of electricity; these contracts are granted for an initial 
period of 30 years, renewable for a further term of 20 years.133
4.85.  CREG promotes free competition in the generation and marketing of electricity. In order to 
encourage efficiency in electricity transmission and distribution – areas in which there are natural

monopolies managed by private companies – work to expand the National Transmission System 
(SNT) is handled through public bidding.134 Restrictions on companies' horizontal and vertical 
integration in the market remain in place.135
4.86.  CREG determines the tariff structure for regulated users. Colombia provides subsidies for 
users based on their economic stratum and, for municipalities, on altitude and air temperature.136 
To minimize and prevent major tariff fluctuations, in 2024, CREG took measures (through 
four resolutions) to: facilitate the purchase of electricity when changes in climate, such as the El Niño 
phenomenon, occur137; enable the voluntary renegotiation of bilateral electricity contracts in the 
wholesale energy market (MEM); reduce tariffs for regulated users138; and allow MEM agents to 
purchase electricity to protect regulated users.139 Unregulated users or major consumers negotiate 
their tariffs with the companies that generate and market electricity.140
4.87.  Electricity is bought and sold on the MEM, a competitive market that brings together 
generators, transmitters, distributors, marketers and non-regulated consumers and that is run by 
the Compañía de Expertos (XM).141 Transactions are conducted through day-ahead trading on the 
spot market (short-term contracts) and through freely negotiated bilateral (long-term) contracts.142 
CREG Resolution No. 96 of 2019 authorized power plants with a capacity of less than 20 MW, which 
tend to use non-conventional renewable energy sources, to negotiate bilateral contracts with 
unregulated users. According to the authorities, 8-10% of electricity purchased through long-term 
contracts must come from non-conventional renewable energy sources.
4.88.  Electricity production in 2024 (November) stood at 20,766.26 MW (20% higher than in 
2018).143 In 2024, 63.3% of electricity was generated from hydropower, 29.2% from thermal 
energy, 6.7% from solar energy, and the rest through cogeneration. This represents a slight 
diversification towards non-conventional renewable energy sources within the energy mix, with 
hydropower accounting for 86% of the electricity generated in 2017. Colombia sells electricity to 
Ecuador through the Andean Electrical Interconnection System (SINEA).144 In response to the El Niño 
phenomenon in the period 2023-24 and to the increase in electricity exports to Ecuador, a number 
of temporary measures were brought in to protect the domestic supply, and it was decided that only 
electricity produced from thermal energy could be exported.145 In addition, gas producers may

supply the electricity system in the event of low water flows.146 In 2024, incentives to diversify

4.3  Manufacturing
4.89.  The share of GDP of the manufacturing sector remained stable over the period 2018-24, 
standing at 11.2% in 2018 and 10.1% in 2024. There was a slight decrease in the share of 
employment over the same period (from 11.4% of the working population to 10.7%), with the 
sector's share remaining below that of the agricultural sector. There was no major change in the 
structure of GDP since the previous review, with the main components being: (a) petroleum refining 
and chemical products; (b) beverages, foodstuffs and tobacco; and (c) metallurgical products, which 
replaced non-metallic products and textiles in the ranking. The overall contribution of these 
components to GDP fell from 9% in 2018 to 8.2% in 2024 (Table 4.8).

4.90.  The MinCIT Directorate of Productivity and Competition continues to be the entity responsible 
for Colombia's industrial policy.148 The industrial policy design is set forth in the National 
Reindustrialization Policy, which seeks to strengthen: (a) the energy transition by promoting the use 
of non-conventional renewable energy sources; (b) export diversification (internationalization); 
(c) agroindustrialization; and (d) new sectors, such as healthcare (through the production of 
medicines and medical devices) and the shipbuilding and aviation industries (parts and 
maintenance).
4.91.  Manufacturing exports (excluding crude and refined petroleum and electricity) as a share of 
total exports rose from 43% in 2018 to 48.3% in 2023, owing to the rising price of coal; however, 
exports declined slightly in 2019 and 2021. The main manufacturing exports were coal (primarily 
coal production), precious stones (emeralds), gold, and plastics and articles thereof. During the 
period under review, exports of emeralds, gold, and aluminium and articles thereof rose sharply, by 
66%, 79% and 120%, respectively, while exports of plastics and articles thereof fell. Meanwhile, 
manufacturing imports went from 80.2% of total imports in 2018 to 75.0% in 2023. The main 
manufacturing imports were mechanical appliances and electrical machinery and equipment used in 
the mining industry and motor vehicles (HS chapters 84, 85 and 87). Imports of plastics and articles 
thereof have fallen by 14% since the single-use plastic tax was introduced in 2022 (Table 4.8).
148 MinCIT, Noticias de Industria, Política de Reindustrialización: una Necesidad para el Desarrollo del 
País. Viewed at: https://www.mincit.gov.co/prensa/noticias/industria/politica-reindustrializacion-presentacion-
a-medios.

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 115 -
 
4.92.  The tariff protection granted to the manufacturing sector decreased slightly, from 5.8% in 
2017 to 5.4% in 2024, a trend similar to that observed in the simple average of MFN duties. Since 
2021, Colombia has applied import tariff quotas at a preferential rate of 5% on hybrid and electric 
vehicles under the Tariff Instrument for Automotive Improvement and Road Safety (IAMAS) for 
companies that are part of the Automotive Industry Promotion Programme (PROFIA).
4.93.  Colombia continues to make use of automatic and non-automatic import licences. These 
licences are approved by MinCIT and cover organic and inorganic chemicals; weapons, ammunition 
and explosives; flammable materials; and waste and scrap.
4.94.  As was the case in the previous review, Colombia still applies some trade protection measures 
(Section 3.1.6) on manufacturing sector imports (tableware, iron bars, iron rods, steel pipes and 
dishwashers), some for a period of more than 10 years.
4.95.  Colombia continues to run incentive programmes with a view to fostering domestic and foreign 
investment, employment, reindustrialization, regional development, competitiveness and exports; it 
also has horizontal incentives and programmes, such as free zones, Special Import-Export Systems 
(SEIEX) (Vallejo Plan) and Special Economic Export Zones (ZEEE) (Section 3). There are 
sector-specific incentives, such as incentives to foster the production of energy from 
non-conventional sources149; incentives for automotive production150; and the Shipyard Industry 
Promotion Programme.151
4.4  Services
4.4.1  Financial services
4.4.1.1  Regulations and general characteristics
4.96.  Financial services in Colombia are governed by Article 335 of the Political Constitution, the 
Basic Statute of the Financial System (Decree No. 663 of 1993), Law No. 964 of 2005, Law No. 1328 
of 2009, Single Decree No. 2555 of 2010, the Basic Legal Circular and the Basic Accounting and 
Financial Circular. During the period under review, significant changes were introduced in both 
circulars that affect the functioning of the system (Table 4.10).
4.97.  The entities responsible for supervising the system are the Ministry of Finance and Public 
Credit (MinHacienda), the Colombian Financial Supervisory Authority (Superfinanciera), the 
Securities Market Self-Regulator (AMV), the Financial Cooperatives Guarantee Fund (Fogacoop) and 
the Financial Institution Guarantee Fund (Fogafin). The Coordination Committee for the Monitoring 
of the Financial System carries out technical and legal studies for the regulations governing the 
system152 (Chart 4.3).
149 Law No. 2068 of 2020 and Law No. 1715 of 2014, as amended by Law No. 2099 of 2021.
150 Decree No. 1122 of 2019.
151 Decree No. 1156 of 2020.
152 The Coordination Committee for the Monitoring of the Financial System is composed of MinHacienda, 
the BRC, the Financial Supervisory Authority, Fogafin, and the Financial Regulation Unit (URF).

WT/TPR/S/472 • Colombia
- 116 -
 
Chart 4.3 Functioning of Colombia's financial system
Source:
Financial Supervisory Authority.
4.98.  The financial market in Colombia is composed of entities that engage in activities related to 
the management, utilization and investment of resources deposited by the public, overseen by the 
Financial Supervisory Authority, and solidarity sector entities that provide financial services, 
overseen by the Supervisory Authority for the Solidarity Economy. Among the entities supervised 
by the Financial Supervisory Authority are credit institutions153, financial services companies154, 
capitalization companies, insurance entities and insurance and reinsurance intermediaries (including 
representative offices of foreign companies), second-tier banks (specialized official institutions), 
investment management companies, stockbrokerage firms, companies specializing in electronic 
deposits and payments (SEDPEs), and infrastructure providers.155 Conversely, solidarity sector 
entities that provide financial services under the oversight of the Supervisory Authority for the 
Solidarity Economy, and which collect funds from their own members, include savings and credit 
cooperatives, and multi-purpose and comprehensive cooperatives that have a savings and credit 
section.156
4.99.  The Basic Statute of the Financial System (EOSF) sets out guidelines for the establishment, 
acquisitions and mergers of financial entities, and foreign investment. The Financial Supervisory 
Authority authorizes divisions (splits), mergers and acquisitions when there is no risk to 
competition.157 The Authority must also authorize acquisition transactions that equal or exceed 10% 
of a financial company's ownership.158 There are no restrictions on foreign participation in the 
Colombian financial system, but the Authority's authorization is required to be able to invest in the 
sector.159 Entities, both domestic and foreign, wishing to operate in the financial sector must prove 
that they meet the minimum capital requirement, which is updated annually based on the annual 
inflation rate (Table 4.9).
153 Credit institutions include banks, commercial finance companies, financial cooperatives, and financial 
corporations.
154 Financial services companies include general deposit warehouses (AGDs), pension and severance 
fund management companies, foreign exchange brokerages, special financial services companies, and trusts.
155 Infrastructure providers include stock exchanges; agricultural, agro-industrial and other commodity 
exchanges; futures and options exchanges; administrators of clearing and settlement systems for securities, 
futures, options, etc.; administrators of central securities depositories; central counterparty clearing houses; 
administrators of securities trading and transaction registration systems; administrators of low value payment 
systems; administrators of foreign exchange trading and registration systems; administrators of foreign 
exchange clearing and settlement systems; and crowdfunding companies.

4.100.  The most important regulatory changes introduced during the period under review include 
the new oversight rules for financial conglomerates and holding companies, including the Grupo 
Bicentenario160, and the updated capital requirements for credit institutions to align them with the 
Basel III criteria (Table 4.10). Some of these criteria are already being implemented.161 Moreover, 
during the period under review, the Financial Supervisory Authority issued 217 External Circulars 
on, among other financial matters, how supervised entities must comply with the provisions 
governing their activity and manage the risks inherent to their operations.162 Colombia participates 
in several international supervisory initiatives, in which Colombian financial groups are present, 
through memorandums of understanding with various jurisdictions and international associations.163 
This issue is important for Colombia, as in 2024 it had 14 financial groups that accounted for 44% 
of the system's institutions; these institutions hold assets abroad, especially in Central America, with 
a total value of USD 49,190 million.

4.101.  As part of the Comprehensive National Reactivation Strategy, in 2024 Colombia implemented 
a Credit Pact  with the Banking and Financial Institutions Association of Colombia (Asobancaria), 
through which the institutions that are part of Asobancaria pledged to grant a total of 
COP 252.7 trillion in credit, over 18 months from September 2024, to five priority sectors: 
agriculture; manufacturing and energy transformation; tourism; housing and infrastructure; and the 
popular economy (both agricultural and non-agricultural). Between March 2023 and August 2024, 
COP 197.7 trillion was disbursed. The credit amounts were to be distributed as follows: 16.3% to 
housing; 65.7% to manufacturing; 12.9% to agriculture; 1.6% to the popular economy; and 3.4% 
to tourism, in line with the reindustrialization policy. The Financial Supervisory Authority is 
responsible for oversight.164 The Credit Pact does not include an agreement on the interest rates of 
the loans, which are set by each entity according to the market (Table 4.11).

4.4.1.2  Banks
4.102.  Companies wishing to operate in the Colombian banking system must follow the procedure 
set out in Article 53 of the EOSF, namely, to (a) be established as a commercial public limited 
company or cooperative association; (b) be subject to oversight by the Financial Supervisory 
Authority; and (c) obtain an indefinite and non-transferable operating permit (authorization 
certificate) issued by the Financial Supervisory Authority. The minimum capital required to establish 
a bank (domestic or foreign) was COP 133,321 million in 2024 (Table 4.9). However, according to 
the authorities, foreign banks operating through representative offices, subsidiaries or branches do 
not have to provide proof of minimum capital. The Financial Supervisory Authority carries out both 
the individual and consolidated supervision of banks operating in Colombia, including the subsidiaries 
or branches of foreign banks. In the event that the subsidiary is part of a conglomerate, the parent 
company of which is domiciled outside Colombia, and it can demonstrate that it is subject to a 
comprehensive and consolidated prudential regulation and supervision regime equivalent to that of 
the Financial Supervisory Authority, the Law on Foreign Financial Holding Companies (Law No. 1870 
of 2017) will not apply to the parent company. If it cannot demonstrate this, the Financial 
Supervisory Authority may request the information it deems pertinent to the comprehensive and 
consolidated supervision of the entity. If the information does not permit adequate supervision, the 
Financial Supervisory Authority may revoke the bank's operating authorization. The authorities have 
also indicated that the supervision covers both the local bank and its financial subsidiaries. The 
number of banks in Colombia is not limited, nor is the number of offices they may have; however, 
the opening and closing of offices requires authorization from the Financial Supervisory Authority.165
4.103.  As of December 2024, the Colombian banking market had 19 domestic banks, 10 foreign 
banks, 6 domestic financial corporations, 15 domestic financing companies, 4 financial cooperatives, 
1 public bank (Banagrario), and 11 second-tier banks.166 Banks operating in Colombia that are part 
of a financial conglomerate, the parent company of which is domiciled abroad, have 13 subsidiaries 
in different segments of the financial sector (for example: trusts, stockbrokerage firms, and financial 
corporations).
4.104.  During the period under review, credits trended upward, despite a slight slowdown in 2020 
(Table 4.12). Credit institutions maintained a capital adequacy ratio above the required minimum, 
and their profitability indicators (ROA and ROE) fluctuated as a result of the 2020 pandemic and the 
increase in the monetary policy interest rate (TPM) by the BRC as of 2022, which affected both 
indicators in 2023–24. In addition, the authorities indicated that non-performing loans influenced 
the low levels of profitability recorded during the period. For this reason, several financial institutions 
needed to recapitalize in 2023-24 (Table 4.12).

4.105.  According to the authorities, all currency exchange in Colombia must be conducted through 
the foreign exchange market and registered with the BRC for statistical purposes. As a result, all 
foreign trade operations (imports and exports) must be registered.167 Importers or exporters who 
have a clearing account168 registered with the BRC can make payments for their own foreign trade 
operations. Otherwise, the payment must be made through foreign exchange market brokers 
(IMCs)169, who register it with the BRC (foreign exchange declaration170).171 International trade 
operators who have a clearing account are not subject to the financial transactions tax, which stands 
at 0.4% and was not changed during the period under review.172
4.106.  The IMCs register the payment of the foreign trade transaction with the BRC, a process also 
known as a "foreign exchange declaration". In other words, importers or exporters resident in 
Colombia must register foreign trade transactions with the BRC – whether it is a payment for imports 
or foreign currency received for exports – either through an IMC and a foreign exchange declaration, 
or personally, via a clearing account transaction statement.
4.107.  Since 2021, foreign trade transactions may be registered with the IMC through the use of 
credit or debit cards173, i.e. if the importer has a card issued by an IMC, they can pay for their 
imports with that card, and the exporter can receive the payment in the account linked to the card; 
these foreign exchange declarations are permitted by the BRC. Moreover, the BRC has established 
the procedure to be followed for the payment of imports through a payment aggregator (PSPA), 
whether resident or non-resident.174 Like IMCs, payment aggregators must keep their operations 
documents and transmit them to the BRC.
4.108.  In summary, payment for e-commerce import and export transactions is currently permitted 
through companies specializing in electronic deposits and payments175 and through payment 
aggregators. According to the National Foreign Trade Association (ANALDEX) and the Colombian 
Chamber of Electronic Commerce (CCCE), monitoring these transactions remains a challenge for the 
authorities (e.g. DIAN, the Financial Supervisory Authority, and the BRC), as it requires updating 
the regulations in force, developing new control and monitoring mechanisms, and securing resources 
to keep up with technological advancements.176

4.4.1.3  Insurance
4.109.  Foreign insurance and reinsurance companies that wish to operate in Colombia are subject 
to the same requirements as foreign banks.177 Companies must be established as a commercial 
public limited company or a cooperative association, and must obtain an operating license. The 
registration of subsidiaries and branches must be authorized by the Financial Supervisory 
Authority.178 Likewise, share purchases are permitted as long as they comply with the guidelines of 
the EOSF.179 In 2024, the minimum capital required to set up a reinsurance company, insurance 
company, 
or 
export 
credit 
insurance 
company 
in 
Colombia 
was 
COP 60,817 million, 
COP 15,217 million, or COP 13,563 million, respectively (Table 4.9). In addition to minimum capital, 
a solvency requirement also applies, which varies depending on the branch of insurance – ranging 
from COP 2,868 million (education) to COP 5,252 million (automobiles).180
4.110.  According to the authorities, insurance companies may offer general insurance, including 
damage (property and casualty insurance), life insurance (personal insurance), and insurance 
related to social security (disability and survivors' benefits, occupational risk, pensions under Law 
No. 100, voluntary pensions, commuted pensions, voluntary annuities, and the Periodic Economic 
Benefits programme (BEPS)).181 Residents of Colombia may purchase any type of national or foreign 
insurance, except insurance relating to social security, mandatory insurance and insurance where 
the State is a beneficiary, which must be provided by national insurers.182
4.111.  According to the authorities, during the period under review the rules governing cross-border 
trade in insurance were not changed. Foreign insurance companies may offer insurance related to 
international maritime transport, commercial aviation, and space launch and transport, provided 
they are registered in the RAIMAT. They may also sell agricultural insurance policies, subject to 
registration in the RAISAX.183
4.112.  Foreign reinsurance companies and reinsurance brokers that want to operate in Colombia 
can do so, provided they are registered in the Register of Foreign Reinsurers and Reinsurance 
Brokers (REACOEX), and may only engage in the activity for which they are registered.184
4.113.  To prevent the risk assumed by insurance companies from exceeding 15% of their equity, 
the Financial Supervisory Authority has been identifying and monitoring insurance companies' large 
exposures and risk concentrations since 2022.185 In addition, since 2024, all companies have to 
report their level of compliance with the risk limits determined by the Financial Supervisory 
Authority.186
4.114.  Between 2018 and 2024, four new companies – three of them domestic – entered the 
insurance market, bringing the total number of insurance companies to 46.187 Insurance penetration 
was around 3% of GDP188, with claims remaining stable in the general insurance sector. The

4.4.2  Telecommunications
4.115.  Telephony and Internet services are regulated primarily by Law No. 1341 of 2009, 
Decree No. 1078 of 2015 (Single Regulatory Decree for the Information and Communication 
Technologies Sector) and Resolution No. 5050 of 2016, which brings together the current general 
resolutions issued by the Communications Regulatory Commission (CRC). To foster Internet use, 
substantial amendments were made to Law No. 1341 during the period under review.189 The Internet 
was also identified as an essential and universal public service, while information and communication 
technologies (ICT) were identified as services of social interest and public utilities.190
4.116.  The institutional framework has not changed since 2018. The Ministry of Information and 
Communication Technologies (MinTIC) formulates and implements policy for the sector. The CRC is 
the regulatory body191, although MinTIC grants licences to use the radio spectrum.192 The CRC has 
the authority to define the relevant markets193, set the criteria for determining market dominance194, 
and issue relevant regulatory measures.195 As the competition authority, the Supervisory Authority 
for Industry and Trade (SIC) investigates and hands down penalties for anti-competitive behaviour 
in the sector.
4.117.  When it comes to telephony and the Internet, Colombia's aim is to improve connectivity in 
order to close the digital divide and drive the digital transformation in the interest of economic 
development. As a result, several plans have been rolled out since 2018, such as the Comprehensive 
Digital Connectivity Expansion Plan, which was adopted in 2024 with the aim of improving 
connectivity and service quality and promoting the use of ICT tools.196 In addition, the digital 
transformation of public services has continued apace through the Digital Government initiative. 
According to the Information and Communication Technology Satellite Account, the ICT sector 
https://www.fasecolda.com/cms/wp-content/uploads/2025/02/Cifras-diciembre-pub-2024.pdf.
189 Law No. 1341 of 2009, as amended by Laws No. 1978 of 2019, No. 2108 of 2021, No. 2294 of 2023, 
and No. 2416 of 2024.
190 Laws No. 2108 of 2021 and No. 2416 of 2024.
191 The CRC also began regulating audiovisual, postal and broadcasting services in 2019.

generated 3.5% of the Colombian economy's gross value added in 2023 (most recent year for which 
provisional data are available).197
4.118.  In line with the objectives of the sectoral policy, in the period 2018-24, Colombia 
implemented a number of regulatory changes concerning the use of the radio spectrum, with a view 
to promoting investment in the sector. The (renewable) term of the licence was extended from 10 to 
20 years in 2019198, and the maximum spectrum amount that can be used by each operator was 
increased in 2022.199 Colombia also amended the economic system governing spectrum use, given 
the high cost compared with other markets in the region, as indicated in a study published in 2021.200 
As from 2019, payment can be made either in cash or in kind (which involves implementing projects 
to connect unconnected users and/or areas).201 In 2023, the methodology for calculating the 
consideration to be paid was changed202 and the guarantee scheme was modified to ensure greater 
flexibility for operators.203
4.119.  To promote investment, the procedural requirements for installing infrastructure in 
municipalities were standardized and streamlined.204 However, some obstacles, such as land use 
restrictions, still remain.205 Meanwhile, the CRC authorized the leasing of new support components 
(such as urban furniture) for cabling and equipment206, and modified the methodology for calculating 
the caps on lease rates; regulating these rates helped to reduce costs in several municipalities.207
4.120.  Since 2018, in order to expand Internet services, new frequency bands have been assigned, 
including for 5G technology, and existing networks have been upgraded. Generally speaking, 
4G technology was used for 89.8% of connections in 2023208, although there are still major 
disparities at the local level.209 In terms of fixed Internet services, the use of fibre optic broadband 
has increased, although ADSL and cable broadband are still the most commonly used 
technologies.210 Colombia has made installing local fibre optic networks a priority.211
4.121.  Internet use in Colombia increased between 2018 and 2024 (Table 4.14) but continues to 
be held back by, inter alia, the cost and the lack of digital know-how.212 In order to reduce costs, 
prices are subsidized213 and there are tax incentives to encourage imports and/or purchases of

is the universal fund.221 As of 2021, fixed Internet service providers with fewer than 
30,000 residential users were exempted from paying the annual contributions for a period of 
five years.222
4.124.  There have been no major changes in market composition since 2018. There continue to be 
both public providers (such as ETB) and private providers. Mobile services are provided by network 
operators and virtual mobile operators. The main operators for both mobile and fixed services 
continue to be Claro, Movistar (in which the State has a 32.5% holding) and Tigo.223 Claro has a 
dominant position in the mobile market.224 In 2024, the CRC placed asymmetric regulatory measures 
on the dominant market operator concerning national roaming in certain municipalities, with the aim 
of ensuring effective competition in the market.225 The CRC also amended the conditions governing 
the use of number portability.226
4.125.  All operators, including the dominant market operator, are free to set the retail prices for 
mobile and fixed services. However, prices must be notified to the CRC, which must approve price 
increases.227 The CRC continues to cap certain wholesale prices, such as national roaming rates and 
fixed-mobile termination charges. All price caps were lowered during the review period.228
4.4.3  Transport
4.126.  Maritime transport is the primary mode of transport for Colombia's exports and imports; in 
2023, 98.3% of exports and 98.2% of imports were carried by sea.229 International road transport 
is used for dry cargo, mainly for transportation to Ecuador and the Bolivarian Republic of 
Venezuela.230 Air transport is used, in particular, for perishable goods, such as flowers, fruit and 
coffee.231 River transport is practically never used, as river border crossings do not usually have the 
equipment needed to conduct foreign trade operations.232
4.127.  Cargo is transported almost exclusively by road between production centres and ports. Rail 
is used to transport coal (Colombia's second most important export product) and some other mining 
products, and inland waterways are used for petroleum products.233 Colombia also has a network of 
pipelines to carry crude oil (the country's top export product) to ports.

4.128.  Road transport within the territory is slow and expensive234 owing to the country's 
geography, the state of the road network and traffic235, tolls and fuel costs, the age of vehicles and 
the road accident rate.236 To cut land transport costs, Colombia is promoting the modernization of 
the cargo vehicle fleet.237 It is also aiming to create an intermodal transport system to reduce times 
and costs, and successfully decarbonize the sector.238 In 2021, the transport sector consumed 47% 
of the energy produced in Colombia and was responsible for 14% of national greenhouse gas 
emissions.239 Some companies are adopting "green logistics" practices with regard to transport, such 
as the use of alternative fuels.240
4.4.3.1  Maritime transport
4.129.  According to the Maritime Sector Satellite Account, between 2019 and 2023, the maritime 
sector's share of the gross value added (GVA) of the Colombian economy ranged from 1.8% to 
1.9%.241
4.130.  Maritime transport is governed primarily by the Commercial Code (Book Five), Law No. 2133 
of 2021 and Decree No. 1079 of 2015 (Single Regulatory Decree for the Transport Sector). Law 
No. 2133 of 2021, which repealed Law No. 730 of 2001, regulates flagging and other issues, such 
as maritime mortgages. The Directorate-General of Maritime Affairs (DIMAR), under the Ministry of 
National Defence, is Colombia's maritime authority.
4.131.  The merchant marine of Colombia mainly consists of small vessels; most of the country's 
foreign trade is therefore transported by foreign-flagged ships.242 During the review period, the 
ship-flagging procedure was simplified and streamlined to promote the development of the merchant 
marine; this included eliminating prerequisites, shortening time frames, and creating a single 
register.243 In Colombia, vessels may only be registered by Colombian (natural and legal) persons 
and fly the Colombian flag.244 Colombia continues to favour the recruitment of Colombian nationals 
in the merchant marine; foreigners may not hold officer positions or make up more than 20% of the 
crew.245
4.132.  To provide a scheduled international service, either an approval (shipping companies) or an 
operating permit (vessels) from DIMAR is required, whereas an authorization is required for a 
non-scheduled service.246 Vessel owners must appoint a legal representative (maritime agent) in

Colombia247; if the maritime agent is a company, at least 60% of its capital must be of 
Colombian origin.248 Foreign shipping companies must establish branches in Colombia.249
4.133.  Shipping companies established in Colombia pay income tax at the general rate; in 2021-22, 
a preferential rate was applied to support the post-pandemic recovery.250 shipping companies that 
are not established in Colombia must pay a 5% income tax withholding, unless a double taxation 
agreement exists.251
4.134.  Cabotage is reserved to Colombian shipping companies and vessels.252 If there are no 
suitable Colombian vessels available to provide the service, DIMAR allows foreign vessels to be used 
for as long as is necessary. These may be chartered by Colombian shipping companies or by service 
users. The chartering contract is signed with the shipping companies providing the international 
service, whether Colombian or foreign.253 The authorities have indicated that, in general, cabotage 
services have been provided by Colombian vessels since 2018.
4.135.  During the review period, Colombia continued to promote the shipping industry as part of 
its strategy to attract foreign direct investment (FDI) for non-extractive activities.254 Tariff 
exemptions for imports of goods and inputs (395 10-digit tariff lines) for the construction of certain 
vessels remain in place, provided that those goods and inputs are not manufactured locally.255 
Vessels may also remain in Colombia under the temporary import regime in order to be repaired.256 
Colombia has 61 shipyards, including a public shipyard, the Science and Technology Corporation for 
the Development of the Naval, Maritime and Riverine Industry (COTECMAR).
4.136.  The port sector is governed by Law No. 1 of 1991, Decree No. 1589 of 2004 and Decree 
No. 1079 of 2015. The Transport Supervisory Authority (Supertransporte)257 and the National 
Infrastructure Agency (ANI) administer ports. The Ministry of Transport (MinTransporte) formulates 
and implements the National Ports Policy, which must be approved by CONPES.258 MinTransporte 
defines the methodology for setting prices and Supertransporte monitors them.259 The National 
Highways Institute (INVIAS) guarantees access, including through canals, to port areas.260
4.137.  Colombia has Caribbean and Pacific maritime ports. The construction and operation of these 
ports continues to be carried out through concession contracts awarded to port companies by the 
ANI. Since 2018, the procedures for awarding concession contracts have not changed substantially. 
Port companies are subject to a consideration, the amount of which is calculated in accordance with 
the methodology established in the 2013 Port Expansion Plan or is stipulated in the concession 
contract.261
4.138.  Port companies may be state- or privately owned or have mixed ownership structures, 
including foreign ownership, and may provide a public (i.e. provided to any user) or private (to a 
single user) service.

4.139.  The authorities have indicated that public service port companies are free to set their own 
prices, except for wharfage, loading/unloading, storage and supply services262, for which prices are 
regulated by MinTransporte and may not exceed the "regional competitive price" by more than 
25%.263 All prices, including those set by private port companies, are notified to Supertransporte 
and published.264
4.140.  Towage services are reserved to Colombian vessels. If there are no Colombian vessels, 
DIMAR allows foreign vessels to be used. Foreign vessels may be used for dredging.265 Harbour pilots 
must be Colombian nationals.266
4.141.  Cartagena is the country's main port. Port cargo moves through seven port areas, six of 
which are on the Caribbean coast.267 This concentration makes it difficult to access ports and port 
operations, thereby reducing competitiveness.268 For this reason, Colombia plans to develop new 
port areas, review price regulations to make prices more competitive, and improve sea and land 
access, in particular to allow for the berthing of large vessels. The main type of cargo handled in 
Colombian ports is bulk cargo, composed of coal, crude oil and oil derivatives, which are the main 
export products (Chart 4.4). These products are mainly exported by companies that extract and/or 
produce them, through their port companies. Containers are the second largest type of cargo 
handled and are both transhipped and exported/imported. Container traffic is almost exclusively 
handled by public-service port companies.269

4.4.3.2  Air transport
4.142.  In 2023, according to the International Civil Aviation Organization (ICAO), air transport 
contributed 4.3% to Colombia's GDP and created 920,000 jobs, both direct (86,000) and indirect.270 
Considering air travel an "essential" public service271, Colombia adopted temporary measures to 
boost the post-pandemic recovery of the sector, such as reducing VAT from 19% to 5% for purchases 
of fuel and air tickets272, introducing tariff concessions for imports of certain inputs, reducing pension 
system contributions, and deferring payments.273
4.143.  Air transport is regulated by the Commercial Code (Book Five), Law No. 105 of 1993 
(Title IV), Decree No. 1079 of 2015, 78 air service agreements/memorandums and the Colombian 
Aviation Regulations (RAC). During the review period, Colombia continued the process of 
harmonizing the RAC with the Latin American Aeronautical Regulations (LAR).274
270 ICAO (2023), The value of air transport to Colombia. Viewed at: https://www.iata.org/en/iata-
repository/publications/economic-reports/the-value-of-air-transport-to-colombia/.
271 Article 68 of Law No. 336 of 1996. 
272 Article 43 of Law No. 2068 of 2020; and Decree No. 575 of 2020.

274 Colombia is a member of the Regional Safety Oversight Cooperation System (SRVSOP). The

4.144.  Aerocivil is the national aviation authority, which regulates air transport and is the sole 
provider of air navigation services. It is also authorized to approve code-sharing agreements.275 
In 2021, Aerocivil's structure was altered to build its competencies (for example, in air safety and 
staff training to meet the challenges the sector is facing, such as increasing traffic).276
4.145.  International air transport services are provided in accordance with the air service 
agreements/memorandums. Cabotage is not permitted.277 In the absence of an agreement, the 
service is provided under the principle of reciprocity, which, since 2018, has been applied with a 
partner and for non-scheduled flights. Colombia usually grants full freedom between territories, as 
well as the fifth freedom of the air for passenger and/or cargo traffic, depending on the negotiation; 
however, in certain cases, the seventh freedom right has been granted for cargo.
4.146.  Airlines are considered Colombian if they have their primary domicile and main base of 
operations in Colombia.278 Foreign investors could therefore hold up to 100% equity.279
4.147.  In order to provide a scheduled international service, Aerocivil issues operating permits 
(Colombian airlines) and authorizations to operate international air services (foreign airlines). Both 
Colombian and foreign companies require an authorization to operate non-scheduled flights.280 
Foreign airlines must be established in Colombia, either through a commercial company or a branch. 
Colombia favours the employment of its nationals in the sector. Within Colombian airlines, in general, 
foreign nationals may not be pilots or account for more than 10% of the crew.281 They also may not 
hold more than 10% of posts in the branches of foreign companies.282
4.148.  Foreign airlines operating through branches pay a 5% income tax withholding, unless a 
double taxation agreement exists.283
4.149.  Airlines are free to set their own prices, which must be communicated to Aerocivil and 
published.284 In the case of scheduled flights to Colombia, the price must include the Tax on Tourism 
as a Social Investment (USD 15).285
4.150.  International air transport services are almost exclusively provided by private operators. 
SATENA is the only state-owned company.286 The Corporación de la Industria Aeronáutica 
Colombiana (CIAC), which provides maintenance, repair and overhaul (MRO) services, is also 
state-owned.287
4.151.  Colombia has 13 international airports; 4 are operated by Aerocivil and 9 by private 
companies that have a concession contract with the State through the National Infrastructure Agency 
(ANI), including Bogotá international airport.288 Concessionaire companies may be subject to 
payment of a consideration, depending on the contract.289 In 2024, the consideration was between 
11.7% and 63% of the concessionaires' gross income.290 Airport service charges are set by Aerocivil

4.152.  Any company, including airport operators and airlines, may provide ground-handling 
services, for which a permit from Aerocivil is required.293 Colombia does not limit the number of 
ground-handling service providers at airports.294
4.153.  International air passenger traffic in Colombia fell sharply in 2020, before rising again in 
2021 and recovering in 2023 (Table 4.16). Although new routes were flown for the first time during 
the review period, the sector is heavily dependent on passenger flows from the United States.295 
Bogotá international airport is the main airport for passenger traffic.296 During the review period, 
the airport's capacity was expanded297, and sustainable practices were adopted for water and energy 
consumption, and waste management.298

4.154.  A parafiscal contribution to finance the promotion, sustainability and competitiveness of 
tourism is levied on airport concessionaires and airlines; the amount remains 2.5‰ of gross income 
or USD 1 per passenger (scheduled flights).299 However, in 2021, the exemption from the parafiscal 
contribution, which was applied in accordance with the providers annual revenue, was abolished.300
4.4.4  Tourism
4.155.  According to the Tourism Satellite Account (TSA), between 2018 and 2023, the sector 
generated an average of 2.1% of the Colombian economy's gross value added (GVA) (Table 4.17). 
In 2023, the sector's contribution to the economy had not recovered to its pre-COVID level owing

4.156.  In 2024, Colombia received more than 6.8 million visitors and, in 2023, had inbound tourism 
expenditure of over COP 50 trillion (Table 4.17). Inbound tourism expenditure primarily comes from 
air transport, food and beverage-related activities, and accommodation.304 It is the main category 
of Colombian service exports and therefore an important source of foreign currency.305 Tourism is 
performing well because Colombia has a varied and affordable tourism offer, and good air 
connections.306 However, the sector remains dependent on other markets in the region; between 
2018 and 2024, the United States, Mexico, Ecuador, Peru and Chile were the main markets for 
Colombian tourism.307
4.157.  In view of the sector's importance to the economy and employment, Colombia introduced 
temporary measures to mitigate the effects of the pandemic. These included exemptions from the 
national consumption tax and VAT308; the suspension of the energy surcharge; the provision of 
financial support for job creation309; and the creation of specific lines of credit through Bancóldex, 
the Colombian development bank.310 The sector also benefited from lower VAT on the purchase of 
air tickets. In 2020, tourist service providers from the Archipelago of San Andrés, Providencia and 
Santa Catalina departments who had been affected by weather events were granted support.311
4.158.  Foreign direct investment (FDI) remains highly important to the tourism sector. Between 
2018 and 2024, this sector attracted 13% of Colombia's non-mining and non-energy FDI.312 To

4.159.  In 2024, the Government and private banks signed the Credit Pact to increase funding for 
"priority" sectors (Section 4.4.1). In the case of tourism, private banks have committed to awarding 
funding of COP 15.2 trillion.313 Between 2024 (September) and 2025 (January), 11.4% of the target 
was met; the weighted average interest rate was 20.9%.314 Bancóldex maintained financing 
programmes for tourism-related MSMEs before and during the pandemic to support the sector.315
4.160.  Colombia's tourism policy is largely set out in the Sectoral Tourism Plan (PST), a four-year 
plan developed by MinCIT.316 Colombia's aim is to have diverse, high-quality, sustainable and 
inclusive tourism.317
4.161.  Measures to promote the development of the sector undertaken since 2018 include 
strengthening coordination between the Government and the local tourism authorities to identify 
municipalities with tourism potential and improve territorial management for tourism purposes318; 
adopting a national quality tourism programme; improving tourist infrastructure; and continuing to 
diversify the tourism offer, with products and services related to gastronomy, nature, culture, health 
and well-being, and agri-tourism, with the aim of promoting local, or "zero kilometre", consumption 
and creating synergies with other sectors of the economy.319 Moreover, special tourism projects 
(PTEs) were devised to encourage large-scale projects that generate large amounts of investment 
and employment.320 PTEs are granted income-tax incentives (Table 4.18) and preferential land

use.321 The authorities indicated that a PTE had been approved, but that it had not yet been 
implemented. The sector also benefits from the Special Regime for Innovative Routes, which 
encourages the provision of air services on new domestic routes.322
4.162.  Colombia also intends to develop tourism by encouraging entrepreneurship. In 2023, the 
National Stimulus, Incentives and Development Programme for the Tourism Sector (EMPRETUR) and 
the FortaleSER scheme were launched to provide training, support and funding (seed capital) for 
tourism-related MSMEs and community tourism organizations.323 The authorities indicated that a 
considerable number of women participated in both programmes.324 Women-led tourist projects, in 
addition to obtaining technical assistance and financing through the National Tourism Fund 
(FONTUR)325, receive technical assistance for internationalization through the ProColombia "Tú, 
Mujer" programme.326
4.163.  Tourism is primarily regulated by the General Tourism Law (Law No. 300 of 1996) and Decree 
No. 1074 of 2015 (Single Regulatory Decree for the Trade, Industry and Tourism Sector). In 2020, 
Law No. 2068 was enacted to regulate digital platforms for tourism services, incorporated in or 
outside Colombia. The General Tourism Law was also amended under Law No. 2068 to strengthen 
the regulation of violations, impose tougher penalties, and promote sustainable, high-quality 
tourism. In 2022, Law No. 2239 was enacted to regulate agri-tourism specifically.
4.164.  The sector's institutional framework has not changed since 2018. At the national level, it is 
made up of MinCIT, which formulates sectoral policy, advised by the Advisory Council for the Tourism 
Industry and, since 2022, the National Committee on Tourism Education and Training. The Higher 
Tourism Council works with public bodies to coordinate the adoption of programmes and projects to 
improve the sector's competitiveness.327 Projects and programmes promoting the sector are 
implemented through FONTUR, in addition to Colombia Productiva, iNNpulsa and ProColombia. 
Colombia has local entities to regulate tourism.328
4.165.  Tourism service providers, including foreigners, may offer all types of tourism services, 
including acting as tourist guides. Operators are generally required to be listed in the National 
Tourism Register329, which is administered by chambers of commerce. During the review period, the 
registration requirement was extended to digital platforms for tourism services (including those 
incorporated abroad) and ecotourism and agri-tourism parks.330 In addition, the registration process 
was simplified and streamlined, and became free of charge.331
4.166.  Colombia has a parafiscal contribution to promote the sustainability332 and competitiveness 
of tourism, which is used to finance FONTUR.333 All tourism operators, with the exception of guides,
